Congruence modules and the Wiles-Lenstra-Diamond numerical criterion in higher codimensions
Srikanth B. Iyengar, Chandrashekhar B. Khare, Jeffrey Manning

Abstract
. We define a congruence module Ψ A ​ ( M ) \Psi_{A}(M) associated to a surjective 𝒪 \mathcal{O} -algebra morphism λ : A → 𝒪 \lambda\colon A\to\mathcal{O} , with 𝒪 \mathcal{O} a discrete valuation ring, A A a complete noetherian local 𝒪 \mathcal{O} -algebra regular at 𝔭 \mathfrak{p} , the kernel of λ \lambda , and M M a finitely generated A A -module. We establish a numerical criterion for M M to have a free direct summand over A A of positive rank. It is in terms of the lengths of Ψ A ​ ( M ) \Psi_{A}(M) and the torsion part of 𝔭 / 𝔭 2 \mathfrak{p}/\mathfrak{p}^{2} . It generalizes results of Wiles, Lenstra, and Diamond, that deal with the case when the codimension of 𝔭 \mathfrak{p} is zero. Number theoretic applications include integral (non-minimal) R = 𝕋 R=\mathbb{T} theorems in situations of positive defect conditional on certain standard conjectures. Here R R is a deformation ring parametrizing certain Galois representations and 𝕋 \mathbb{T} is a Hecke algebra. An example of a positive defect situation is that of proving modularity lifting for 2-dimensional ℓ \ell -adic Galois representations over an imaginary quadratic field. The proofs combine our commutative algebra results with a generalization due to Calegari and Geraghty of the patching method of Wiles and Taylor–Wiles and level raising arguments that go back to Ribet. The results provide new evidence in favor of the intriguing, and as yet fledgling, torsion analog of the classical Langlands correspondence. We also prove unconditional integral R = 𝕋 R=\mathbb{T} results for Hecke algebras 𝕋 \mathbb{T} acting on weight one cohomology of Shimura curves over ℚ \mathbb{Q} . This leads to a torsion Jacquet–Langlands correspondence comparing integral Hecke algebras acting on weight one cohomology of Shimura curves and modular curves. In this case the cohomology has abundant torsion and so our correspondence cannot be deduced by means of the classical Jacquet–Langlands correspondence.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
11F80 (primary); 13C10, 13D02 (secondary)

1.
Introduction

The
raison d’etre
of this paper is to develop new commutative algebra inspired by number theoretic applications. The main contribution of our work is a generalization of the theory of congruence modules and the numerical criteria of Wiles, Lenstra, and Diamond to higher codimension. As a demonstration of the utility of our work, we prove new (integral)
R={\mathbb{T}}
theorems at non-minimal level in certain positive defect situations that arise, for instance, when proving modularity of elliptic curves over imaginary quadratic fields. We also prove unconditional (integral)
R=\mathbb{T}
theorems at non-minimal level when
\mathbb{T}
is a Hecke algebra acting on the weight one cohomology of Shimura curves. This also gives instances of an analog of the Jacquet-Langlands correspondence that relates torsion in cohomology arising from weight one sheaves on Shimura curves and modular curves.

Another motivation for our work is to define congruence modules attached to augmentations of (big) ordinary deformation rings arising from cohomological cuspidal automorphic representations. These number-theoretic applications provided the intuition that guided us to our commutative algebra results.

To set the stage to present our work, we fix a prime number
\ell
, and
\mathcal{O}
the ring of integers of a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
with residue field
k
uniformizer
\varpi
. We consider the category
{\mathrm{CNL}}_{\mathcal{O}}
of complete noetherian local
\mathcal{O}
-algebras with residue field
k
, with morphisms the maps of local
\mathcal{O}
-algebras inducing the identity on
k
.

The patching method introduced in the work of Wiles
[
56
]
and Taylor–Wiles
[
53
]
on modularity lifting theorems, and instrumental in Wiles’ proof of Fermat’s Theorem, has undergone intense development. In the original approach of Wiles, patching was combined with level raising results, and a numerical criterion
[
56
, Appendix, Proposition]
, extended by Lenstra
[
41
]
, for a surjective map between rings
R\to{\mathbb{T}}
, with
{\mathbb{T}}
finite flat over
\mathcal{O}
, to be an isomorphism of complete intersections. In applications in
[
56
]
, the ring
R
is a deformation ring for Galois representations and
{\mathbb{T}}
is a Hecke algebra. Diamond
[
25
, Theorem 2.4]
developed the results of Wiles and Lenstra by proving a numerical criterion for freeness of
R
-modules
M
finite flat over
\mathcal{O}
, in terms of an augmentation
\lambda\colon R\to\mathcal{O}
supported on
M
, that is to say,
M_{\mathfrak{p}}\neq 0
for
\mathfrak{p}
the kernel of
\lambda
. The work of Wiles and Lenstra corresponds to the case when
M
is a cyclic
A
-module. Diamond’s criterion is in terms of the cotangent module
\mathfrak{p}/\mathfrak{p}^{2}
of
\lambda
, assumed to be a finite abelian group, and the congruence module for
M
at
\lambda
, defined to be:

\frac{M}{M[\mathfrak{p}]\oplus M[\operatorname{ann}\mathfrak{p}]}.

In
[
25
, §3]
, Diamond applies this in the case where
R
is a suitable deformation ring, acting on
M\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{H}_{1}(X_{0}(N),\mathcal{O})_{\mathfrak{m}}
with
\mathfrak{m}
a maximal ideal corresponding to the Galois representation
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}
whose deformations are parametrized by
R
. The action of
R
on
M
factors through the corresponding Hecke algebra
\mathbb{T}
. Thus if
M
is free over
R
, the map
R\to\mathbb{T}
is injective, and hence an isomorphism. In this way Diamond recovers the results of
[
56
]
.

The patching method has been extended by Calegari and Geraghty
[
16
]
to situations of
positive defect
in which one patches complexes rather than modules: this is the situation one finds oneself in when proving modularity of elliptic curves over number fields which are not totally real, such as imaginary quadratic fields.

The positive defect case presents a number of additional complications compared to the situation of Wiles, which have prevented the numerical criterion from being generalized in the same way as the patching method. First, the rings
R
and
{\mathbb{T}}
are no longer expected to be complete intersections in general. Secondly, they will typically have torsion, in contrast to the defect zero case where
R
and
{\mathbb{T}}
are expected to be finite flat over
\mathcal{O}
. in the positive defect case. Moreover, the torsion plays an important role in the theory (and a careful understanding of the torsion is necessary for generalizing the patching method). This is an issue as the numerical criterion cannot easily account for torsion in
{\mathbb{T}}
or
M
. To make things even more complicated,
R
and
{\mathbb{T}}
can be entirely torsion which would mean that the map
\lambda\colon R\to\mathcal{O}
used in the numerical criterion may not even exist. Lastly, replacing the module
M
with a complex makes it somewhat unclear how to correctly generalize Diamond’s criterion.

The main contribution of our work is to give a generalization of the numerical criterion to the positive defect case. The key idea is to combine the numerical criterion with patching by applying the numerical criterion directly to the patched objects rather than to the rings
R
and
{\mathbb{T}}
. This sidesteps the issues raised above, as the patched version,
R_{\infty}
, of the ring
R
will automatically be a complete intersection and flat over
\mathcal{O}
, and the patched version,
M_{\infty}
, of the complex will be a (maximal Cohen–Macaulay)
R_{\infty}
-module rather than a complex.

The complication that arises in this approach is that
\operatorname{Ker}(R_{\infty}\to\mathcal{O})
is no longer be a minimal prime of
R_{\infty}
, and
M_{\infty}
is no longer be finite over
\mathcal{O}
. We thus consider a larger families of rings
R
in
{\mathrm{CNL}}_{\mathcal{O}}
than those discussed above, placing no restrictions on the height (also known as
codimension
) of
\operatorname{Ker}(R\to\mathcal{O})
, and requiring only that
M
is finitely generated and has sufficient depth. For such
R
and
M
, we establish a criterion for
M
to have a non-zero finite free summand and
R
to be a complete intersection. Applying this to the patched objects
R_{\infty}
and
M_{\infty}
, we show that
M_{\infty}
has a free direct summand, which in turn implies that the ring
R
acts faithfully on the original complex, giving
R={\mathbb{T}}
. We elaborate on this later.

The proof of the new numerical criterion takes up the first part of this paper, and involves only commutative algebra. This is applied in the third part to prove modularity lifting theorems. The second part of the paper establishes a result concerning the compatibility of patching and duality.

In the remainder of the introduction we present the results in commutative algebra and modularity lifting in greater detail, and explain how they fit in the line of development of modularity lifting theorems pioneered by Wiles, and continued in the work of Taylor–Wiles, Diamond, Kisin, and Calegari–Geraghty.

Commutative algebra results

Consider pairs
(A,\lambda)
, with
A
a complete local noetherian
\mathcal{O}
-algebra and
\lambda\colon A\to\mathcal{O}
a surjective map of
\mathcal{O}
-algebras with kernel
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
; we treat a somewhat larger class of rings in the text. The category consisting of pairs
(A,\lambda)
for which the local ring
A_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}
is regular, of dimension
c
, is denoted
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
. The regularity hypothesis is tantamount to the condition that the conormal module
\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2}
of
\lambda
, viewed as an
\mathcal{O}
-module, has rank equal to
\dim A_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}
, the Krull dimension of
A_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}
. In what follows its torsion-submodule

\Phi_{A}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{tors}{(\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2})}\,,

also plays a key role. Here
\operatorname{tors}(U)
denotes the torsion-submodule of any finitely generated
\mathcal{O}
-module
U
. We also need to consider the torsion-free quotient of
U
, namely
{U}^{\operatorname{tf}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}U/\operatorname{tors}(U)
.

For any finitely generated
A
-module
M
the natural map
M\longrightarrow M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M
induces a map

{\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)}^{\operatorname{tf}}\longrightarrow{\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M)}^{\operatorname{tf}}

of finitely generated torsion-free
\mathcal{O}
-modules. We define the
congruence
module of
M
, denoted
\Psi_{A}(M)
, to be the cokernel of this map. When
c=0
this coincides with Diamond’s definition recalled above; see Proposition
2.10
. The definition is reminiscent of the evaluation map in rational homotopy theory and local algebra defined in exactly the same way for the augmentation
A\to k
to the residue field of
A
, and with
M\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}A
. In particular, the localization of the map above (again for
M=A
) at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
is the evaluation map of the local ring
A_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}
, and the fact this map is non-zero precisely when
A_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}
is regular—this is a result of Lescot
[
42
]
—is critical to all that follows. It implies that the
\mathcal{O}
-module
\Psi_{A}(M)
is torsion, and hence of finite length. The
Wiles defect
of
M
is the integer

\delta_{A}(M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\operatorname{rank}_{A_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}}\!M_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}})\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}{\Phi_{A}}-\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}{\Psi_{A}(M)}\,.

We view this definition of the congruence module and Wiles defect, which extends to all
c\geq 0
the usual definition for
c=0
, as one of the key contributions of this work. We expect these will play a role in number theoretic applications, like analyzing the structure of deformation rings or eigenvarieties at classical points. One piece of evidence in support of their utility is Theorem
A
below that extends the results of Wiles, Lenstra, and Diamond. Building on the results in this paper in
[
32
, Theorem 2.6]
we prove any
A
in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}
is regular if and only if
\Psi_{A}(A)=0
.

Guided by the intended number theoretic applications, for
A
in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
, we consider finitely generated
A
-modules
M
with

0pt_{A}M\geq c+1\quad\text{and}\quad M_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}\neq 0\,.

Some of our results require weaker hypothesis on the depth of
M
; see Section
3
, and in particular
3.3
, for a discussion of these various conditions. By the Auslander-Buchsbaum criterion, the properties above imply that the
A_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}
-module
M_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}
is free.

Theorem A
.

With
A
and
M
as above, set
\mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{rank}_{A_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}}(M_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}})
. The following statements hold.

(1)

One has
\delta_{A}(M)\geq 0
, with equality iff
A
is complete intersection and there is an isomorphism of
A
-modules
M\cong A^{\mu}\oplus W
, where
W_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}=0
.

(2)

If
\delta_{A}(M)=0
and
e_{A}(M)\leq\mu\cdot e(A)
, then
M\cong A^{\mu}
.

The invariant
e_{A}(M)
is the multiplicity of
M
; see
[
13
, §4.6]
. The non-negativity of the defect, which follows by a simple computation involving Fitting ideals when
c=0
, is proved in Corollary
8.3
; the remaining assertions are contained in Theorems
9.5
and
9.6
. One way to think about our result is that the vanishing of the Wiles defect allows one to propagate the local property that
M
is free at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
of rank
\mu
to the global property that
M
has a free direct summand
A^{\mu}
.

As in the proof of
[
25
, Theorem 2.4]
the first step in the proof of Theorem
A
is to deduce that
A
is complete intersection. This involves a “defect formula” that relates the defect of
M
to that of
A
; see Lemma
3.7
.
At this stage, one has the following more general criterion for detecting free summands in
M
, without the complete intersection property as a consequence.

Theorem B
.

Let
A
be a Gorenstein local ring in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}
and
M
a maximal Cohen–Macaulay
A
-module with
\mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{rank}_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}(M_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}})\neq 0
. One has
\delta_{A}(M)=\mu\cdot\delta_{A}(A)
, if and only if

M\cong A^{\mu}\oplus W\qquad\text{where $W_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}=0$.}

If moreover
e_{A}(M)\leq\mu\cdot e(A)
, then
M
is free.

This is the content of Theorem
9.2
. For
A
and
M
as above, the condition that
\delta_{A}(M)=\mu\cdot\delta_{A}(A)
is equivalent to the condition that the Tate cohomology module, in the sense of Buchweitz
[
14
]
, of the pair
(\mathcal{O},M)
in degree
c+1
vanishes; see Proposition
4.9
. This gives yet another perspective on the Wiles defect of
M
.

We prove Theorems
A
and
B
by a reduction to the case
c=0
. For this a key result, recorded below and contained in Theorem
8.2
, is that the Wiles defect remains invariant on going modulo certain
M
-regular sequences. Here
\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{(2)}
denotes the second symbolic power of
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
.

Theorem C
.

Let
A
be in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
and
M
a finitely generated
A
-module such that
0pt_{A}M\geq c+1
. Let
f
in
\mathfrak{p}_{A}\setminus\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{(2)}
be such that it is not a zero-divisor on
M
. Then the ring
B\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}A/fA
is in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c-1)
, and

\delta_{A}(M)=\delta_{B}(M/fM)\,.

In the passage from
A
to
B
, the lengths of both the cotangent module and the congruence module change; the crux is that they change by the same amount. Tracking the change in the cotangent module is relatively straightforward. The other, more subtle problem, is to control the change in the congruence module of
M
. A key result that permits this is the following structure theorem for the torsion-free quotient of Yoneda Ext-algebra
\operatorname{Ext}^{*}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})
.

Theorem D
.

For
A
in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
there is a natural isomorphism of
\mathcal{O}
-algebras

\bigwedge_{\mathcal{O}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2},\mathcal{O})\cong{\operatorname{Ext}^{*}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})}^{\operatorname{tf}}\,.

This is the content of Theorem
6.8
. It may be seen as an integral analogue of Serre’s result that for a regular local ring
R
, with maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
and residue field
k
, the
k
-algebra
\operatorname{Ext}^{*}_{R}(k,k)
is the exterior algebra on
\operatorname{Hom}_{k}(\mathfrak{m}/\mathfrak{m}^{2},k)
. Implicit in Theorem
D
is the fact that
\operatorname{Ext}_{A}^{1}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})
is the dual of the cotangent module of
\lambda
; this is not difficult to verify. The theorem above implies that the torsion-free quotient of
\operatorname{Ext}^{*}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})
is finite and graded-commutative, which is striking because the Ext-algebra
\operatorname{Ext}^{*}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})
itself is typically infinite and non-commutative. The proof of the theorem above uses techniques that grew out of Tate’s work
[
52
]
, which introduced methods of differential graded algebras into commutative algebra.

Another noteworthy feature of congruence modules, is an “invariance of domain” property, Theorem
7.4
, extending
[
12
, Lemma 3.4]
which is the case
c=0
. It too is an important ingredient in the proof of Theorems
B
and
C
.

Theorem E
.

Let
\varphi\colon A\to B
be a surjective map of local rings in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
. For any finitely generated
B
-module
M
with
0pt_{B}M\geq c
, one has

\Psi_{A}(M)\cong\Psi_{B}(M)\,.

Thus
\delta_{A}(M)\geq\delta_{B}(M)
with equality if and only if
\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Phi_{A}=\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Phi_{B}
.

These results above, especially the invariance of domain property, and the fact that the element
f
in Theorem
C
need only be
M
-regular and not
A
-regular, convinces us that the definition of Wiles defect we have is the correct one.

Torsion Langlands correspondence and integral
R=\mathbb{T}
theorems for Betti cohomology in positive defect

We restrict ourselves to considering 2-dimensional representations of
G_{F}
with
F
a number field, and assume that the prime
\ell
does not ramify in
F
. The relevant cohomology groups arise from complexes which are conjectured to be of length
\ell_{0}
where
\ell_{0}=r_{2}
is the defect (see Conjecture
C
). Here
[F:\mathbb{Q}]=r_{1}+2r_{2}
with
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
the number of real and complex places of
F
, respectively. The strategy to use our commutative algebra results for proving (integral, non-minimal) modularity lifting theorems in positive defect is to apply them for suitable augmented rings and modules that arise after carrying out patching. Thus we prove that in a patched non-minimal situation a module
M_{\infty}
over a ring
R_{\infty}
has a free direct summand
R_{\infty}^{\mu}
for a positive integer
\mu
, and
R_{\infty}
is a complete intersection.

These applications to modularity lifting assume Conjectures
A
,
B
,
C
and
D
listed in Section
13
, and are similar to those in
[
16
]
. Conjectures
A
,
B
are about existence of Galois representations attached to Hecke eigenclasses arising from the cohomology of symmetric manifolds and resulting maps
R\to\mathbb{T}
. Conjecture
C
is about concentration of integral cohomology of these manifolds in a certain range
\ell_{0}
, and Conjecture
D
is a version of Ihara’s lemma.

Patching methods of
[
1
]
can be used in principle to prove that a certain patched module
M_{\infty}
is maximal Cohen–Macaulay as a module over a patched deformation ring
R_{\infty}
. Using Taylor’s Ihara avoidance
[
1
, §6.3]
one may in principle deduce (even when considering non-minimal deformations, so
R_{\infty}
need not be a domain) that
M_{\infty}
is faithful as a
R_{\infty}
-module and
M_{\infty}[1/\ell]
is free over
R_{\infty}[1/\ell]
. After passing to a quotient ring by a sequence that may not be regular when
l_{0}>0
, one gets a deformation ring
R
acting on the homology module
M
in lowest degree
q_{0}=r_{1}+r_{2}
of an arithmetic manifold, and one may deduce that
M[1/\ell]
is a free
R[1/\ell]
-module. However, by these methods (that rely primarily on patching) it does not seem possible to get that
M
itself is a faithful
R
-module.

Our work is an integral refinement of such results (conditional on the conjectures referred to above) and when applied in the situation of 2-dimensional Galois representations over imaginary quadratic fields
K
, and with
X
a Bianchi 3-manifold, yields that
M\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{H}_{1}(X,\mathcal{O})_{\mathfrak{m}}
has a free direct summand
R^{\mu}
for some
\mu\geq 1
for a suitable (possibly non-minimal) deformation ring
R
that acts on
M
. Here
\mathfrak{m}
is the maximal ideal of a Hecke algebra
\mathbb{T}
that acts faithfully on
M
with associated residual Galois representation
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}\colon G_{K}\to\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{T}/{\mathfrak{m}})
. (In the statement of Theorem
F
below the non-minimality of the deformation ring
R_{\Sigma}
which acts on the homology of an arithmetic manifold
Y_{0}(\mathcal{N}_{\Sigma})
via Conjectures
A
,
B
is reflected in the fact that the level
\mathcal{N}_{\Sigma}
might be strictly divisible by the conductor of the residual representation
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}
.) The resulting integral
R=\mathbb{T}
theorems that follow produce new evidence towards an emerging
torsion
Langlands correspondence. This addresses torsion Galois representations
\rho\colon G_{K}\to\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}/\omega^{n})
that may not lift to characteristic 0. Our results show that such a
\rho
is automorphic in the sense that it arises from specialising the universal modular Galois deformation
\rho_{\mathbb{T}}\colon G_{K}\to\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{T})
lifting
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}
, arising thus from torsion Hecke eigenclasses in
\operatorname{H}_{1}(X,\mathcal{O})_{\mathfrak{m}}
.

With
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}
,
\Sigma
and
\mathcal{N}_{\Sigma}
as in Section
13
, our main number theory application, Theorem
(Wt1)
, is the following result. In the main text we define
Y_{0}(\mathcal{N}_{\Sigma})
to have additional level structure at an auxiliary prime
t\nmid\mathcal{N}_{\Sigma}
, in order to make the subgroups neat.

Theorem F
.

Assume Conjectures
A
,
B
,
C
and
D
hold for
F
and assume
\ell>3
. Let
\mathfrak{m}
be a non-Eisenstein maximal ideal of
{\mathbb{T}}(K_{0}(\mathcal{N}_{\varnothing}))
such that
N(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})=\mathcal{N}_{\varnothing}
and
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}|_{G_{F(\zeta_{\ell})}}
is absolutely irreducible. There exists an integer
\mu\geq 1
such that for all
\Sigma
there is an isomorphism of
R_{\Sigma}
-modules

\operatorname{H}_{r_{1}+r_{2}}(Y_{0}(\mathcal{N}_{\Sigma}),\mathcal{O})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\Sigma}}\cong R_{\Sigma}^{\mu}\oplus W_{\Sigma}

for some
R_{\Sigma}
-module
W_{\Sigma}
. In particular,
R_{\Sigma}
acts faithfully on
\operatorname{H}_{r_{1}+r_{2}}(Y_{0}(\mathcal{N}_{\Sigma}),\mathcal{O})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\Sigma}}
and so the map
R_{\Sigma}\twoheadrightarrow{\mathbb{T}}_{\Sigma}
from Conjecture
B
is an isomorphism for all
\Sigma
.

In analogy with
[
25
, Theorem 3.4]
, one might expect our method to produce the stronger result that
\operatorname{H}_{r_{1}+r_{2}}(Y_{0}(\mathcal{N}_{\Sigma}),\mathcal{O})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\Sigma}}\cong R_{\Sigma}^{\mu}
(that is, one would expect that
W_{\Sigma}=0
; if further
F
is totally complex one would expect
\mu=1
). The reason for our slightly weaker result is that one cannot control the generic rank of the patched module
M_{\infty}
unless the ring
R
has enough characteristic
0
points (in order to appeal to classical multiplicity one results for automorphic forms), which is not guaranteed in the positive defect case. Fortunately the weaker statement above is still enough to deduce
R={\mathbb{T}}
. Building on these techniques, in subsequent work
[
33
]
, we prove that
\operatorname{H}_{r_{1}+r_{2}}(Y_{0}(\mathcal{N}_{\Sigma}),\mathcal{O})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\Sigma}}\cong R_{\Sigma}
when
F
has no real places and there are geometric characteristic 0 lifts of
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}
.

When
F
is a CM field and
\ell
is sufficiently large
1
1

1

The precise bound is given in
[
45
, (1.2)]
, in our situation,
\ell>20
is sufficient if
[F:\mathbb{Q}]=2
.
, Conjecture
A
is known by
[
45
, Theorem 1.3]
. Moreover if
F
is an imaginary quadratic field, then Conjectures
C
and
D
are known as well (see the discussions after their statements), and so Theorem
F
gives:

Theorem G
.

Let
F
be a imaginary quadratic field. Assume Conjecture
B
holds for
F
. Let
\mathfrak{m}
be a non-Eisenstein maximal ideal of
{\mathbb{T}}(K_{0}(\mathcal{N}_{\varnothing}))
such that
N(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})=\mathcal{N}_{\varnothing}
and
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}|_{G_{F(\zeta_{\ell})}}
is absolutely irreducible.
For
\ell>20
, there exists an integer
\mu\geq 1
such that for all
\Sigma
there is an isomorphism of
R_{\Sigma}
-modules

\operatorname{H}_{1}(Y_{0}(\mathcal{N}_{\Sigma}),\mathcal{O})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\Sigma}}\cong R_{\Sigma}^{\mu}\oplus W_{\Sigma}

for some
R_{\Sigma}
-module
W_{\Sigma}
. In particular,
R_{\Sigma}
acts faithfully on
\operatorname{H}_{1}(Y_{0}(\mathcal{N}_{\Sigma}),\mathcal{O})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\Sigma}}
and so the map
R_{\Sigma}\twoheadrightarrow{\mathbb{T}}_{\Sigma}
from Conjecture
B
is an isomorphism for all
\Sigma
.

When
F
is CM weaker versions of Conjecture
B
were proven in
[
1
]
(which primarily replaced
\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}
with
\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}/I
for a nilpotent ideal
I
, and also placed some restrictions on
F
). It is likely that a refinement of these methods could prove Conjecture
B
for all CM fields, which would make Theorem
G
hold unconditionally.

Torsion Jacquet–Langlands correspondence for weight one coherent cohomology of modular and Shimura curves

We also have the following unconditional applications to integral modularity lifting of our new results in commutative algebra. These applications arise when we consider coherent cohomology of the weight one line bundle
\omega_{\mathcal{O}}
on modular and Shimura curves.

We refer to §
14
for the unexplained notation used in the statement below, which is Theorem
(Wt1)
of the main text. In the case of modular curves (when
\mathfrak{D}=\varnothing
) such a result was proven in
[
18
]
using
q
-expansion principle. One crucial place where
q
-expansions are used in
[
18
]
is to show that, in the relevant non-minimal patched situation, not only is
M_{\infty}
faithful as a
R_{\infty}
-module, but that it is free over the (patched) Hecke algebra, and hence also free as a
R_{\infty}
-module. This argument does not work for Shimura curves for the compelling reason that these have no cusps. Our methods on the other hand do carry over to Shimura curves.

Theorem H
.

Let
X^{\mathfrak{D}}_{\Sigma}
be a Shimura curve arising from an indefinite quaternion algebra
D_{\mathfrak{D}}
over
\mathbb{Q}
that ramifies only at a finite set of primes
\mathfrak{D}
and assume
\ell>3
. Let
\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}}_{\Sigma}
be the Hecke algebra acting on
\operatorname{H}^{1}(X^{\mathfrak{D}}_{\Sigma},\omega_{\mathcal{O}})_{\mathfrak{m}}
and
R^{\mathfrak{D}}_{\Sigma}
the corresponding deformation ring which parametrizes deformations of
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}
that are in particular unramified at
\ell
. The surjective map
R^{\mathfrak{D}}_{\Sigma}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}}_{\Sigma}
is an isomorphism.

From this we deduce a Jacquet–Langlands correspondence comparing the degree 1 integral cohomology with coefficients in the “weight one” sheaf
\omega_{\mathcal{O}}
of modular and Shimura curves. As these cohomology groups typically have abundant torsion this cannot be deduced from the classical Jacquet–Langlands correspondence and, as far as we know, this is the first such correspondence in the literature relating torsion in the coherent cohomology of Shimura varieties that arise from inner forms. The possibility of such a result had been considered earlier by Boxer, Calegari and Gee. The following result is Theorem
(Wt1)
of the main text.

Theorem I
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}\neq\varnothing
and take
\ell>3
. Consider a residual representation
\overline{\rho}:G_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\mathrm{GL}_{2}(k)
that arises from a maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{D}}
of the Hecke algebra acting on
\operatorname{H}^{1}(X^{\mathfrak{D}}_{\Sigma},\omega_{\mathcal{O}})
. We assume that
\overline{\rho}|_{\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{\ell})}
is irreducible. Then:

(1)

\overline{\rho}
arises from a maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{\varnothing}
of the Hecke algebra acting on
\operatorname{H}^{1}(X^{\varnothing}_{\Sigma\cup\mathfrak{D}},\omega_{\mathcal{O}})
.

(2)

If
\mathbb{T}^{\varnothing}_{\Sigma\cup\mathfrak{D}}
and
\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}}_{\Sigma}
are the Hecke algebras acting on
\operatorname{H}^{1}(X^{\varnothing}_{\Sigma\cup\mathfrak{D}},\omega_{\mathcal{O}})
and
\operatorname{H}^{1}(X^{\mathfrak{D}}_{\Sigma},\omega_{\mathcal{O}})
, localized at
\mathfrak{m}_{\varnothing}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{D}}
respectively, then there is a natural surjective map
\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma\cup\mathfrak{D}}^{\varnothing}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathfrak{D}}
with kernel generated by
q_{v}U_{v}^{2}-\psi(\Frob_{v})
for
v\in\mathfrak{D}
.

Wiles defects of cohomology groups of modular curves and Shimura curves

The deformation invariance proved in Theorem
C
has applications to reproving and generalizing, by a different method, the results of
[
8
]
. Specifically an easy corollary of Theorem
C
is the following:

Theorem J
.

Let
R_{\infty}
be a complete local noetherian
\mathcal{O}
-algebra and
\lambda\colon R_{\infty}\to\mathcal{O}
a surjective map with kernel
\mathfrak{p}
, such that
(R_{\infty})_{\mathfrak{p}}
is regular of dimension
c
. Let
M_{\infty}
be an
R_{\infty}
-module with
0pt_{R_{\infty}}M_{\infty}\geq c+1
and
(M_{\infty})_{\mathfrak{p}}\neq 0
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{y},\varpi)
be an
M_{\infty}
-regular sequence, where
{\boldsymbol{y}}=\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{c}\}\subseteq\mathfrak{p}
and let
R_{\boldsymbol{y}}=R_{\infty}/(\boldsymbol{y})
,
M_{\boldsymbol{y}}=M_{\infty}/(\boldsymbol{y})
and
\mathfrak{p}_{\boldsymbol{y}}=\mathfrak{p}/(\boldsymbol{y})\subseteq R_{\boldsymbol{y}}
, so
M_{\boldsymbol{y}}
is flat over
\mathcal{O}
.

Provided that
\mathfrak{p}_{\boldsymbol{y}}/\mathfrak{p}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{y}}
has finite length, the Wiles defect
\delta_{R_{\boldsymbol{y}}}(M_{\boldsymbol{y}})
depends only on
R_{\infty}
,
M_{\infty}
and
\lambda\colon R_{\infty}\to\mathcal{O}
, and not on the choice of
\boldsymbol{y}
.

When
M_{\infty}=R_{\infty}
(and hence
R_{\infty}
is Cohen–Macaulay) this recovers
[
8
, Theorem 3.25]
. Theorem
J
represents a significant generalization of this result, as it does not require that
R_{\infty}
is Cohen–Macaulay.

Removing the requirement that
R_{\infty}
is Cohen–Macaulay is particularly significant for number theoretic applications. In the context considered in
[
8
]
, the ring
R_{\infty}
would be a power series ring over a completed tensor product of certain local Galois deformation rings,
M_{\infty}
would be a ‘patched’ module arising from the cohomology of certain Shimura curves and the augmentation
\lambda\colon R_{\infty}\to\mathcal{O}
would arise from a certain cuspidal Hilbert modular form (this is still a special case, the same commutative algebra setup would occur in any patching situation with defect
\ell_{0}=0
). Crucially, while the ring
R_{\infty}
is only known to be Cohen–Macaulay in some rather restrictive cases, the module
M_{\infty}
would automatically satisfy
0pt_{R_{\infty}}M_{\infty}=c+1
in all cases, as a consequence of the patching argument.

The primary objects of number theoretic interest would be a Galois deformation ring
R
and a cohomology module
M
, which would have the form
R=R_{\boldsymbol{y}}
and
M=M_{\boldsymbol{y}}
for some sequence
{\boldsymbol{y}}
as above. Theorem
J
would thus imply that
\delta_{R}(M)
(which was referred to as the
cohomological Wiles defect
in
[
7
]
and
[
8
]
) depends only
R_{\infty}
,
M_{\infty}
and
\lambda\colon R_{\infty}\to\mathcal{O}
, and not on
R
and
M
directly.

As in
[
8
]
, both
R_{\infty}
and
\lambda\colon R_{\infty}\to\mathcal{O}
are determined entirely by local Galois theoretic information. The module
M_{\infty}
is not always known to be determined by local information, but it is reasonable to expect that it is. In fact it is plausible that one can prove that it is in some circumstances where
R_{\infty}
cannot be proven to be Cohen–Macaulay. This would then imply that the cohomological Wiles defect,
\delta_{R}(M)
, is determined entirely by local information without requiring the assumption that
R_{\infty}
is Cohen–Macaulay, allowing one to generalize
[
8
, Theorem 6.5]
.

Links between the commutative algebra and modularity lifting

We outline where our results fit in the evolution of modularity lifting results.

The original papers of Wiles and Taylor–Wiles,
[
56
]
and
[
53
]
, deduced modularity lifting from an
R={\mathbb{T}}
result which they first proved in the minimal level case via a patching argument, and then proved in non-minimal levels via an induction argument using the numerical criterion. The innovation of Diamond in
[
25
]
reformulated the patching and numerical criterion for modules rather than rings, which allowed the argument to prove freeness results for certain cohomology groups (rather than requiring these results as inputs to the argument).

Kisin’s work in
[
39
]
gave the patching method far greater flexibility and applicability by patching global deformation rings and Hecke modules relative to local deformation rings. The work of Kisin almost erases the distinction between minimal and non-minimal cases, and removes the need for the numerical criterion. This comes at the expense of only proving
R[1/\ell]={\mathbb{T}}[1/\ell]
, rather than
R={\mathbb{T}}
, which does not matter for proving modularity of
\ell
-adic Galois representations.

These works were in the case when the (global) defect
\ell_{0}
is 0. The insight of Calegari and Geraghty in
[
16
]
generalized the patching method to positive defect (subject to certain conjectures, which we must also assume for our work). Following the approach of Kisin, this gives modularity lifting results for
\ell
-adic representations in both minimal and non-minimal levels. However in non-minimal levels it again can only prove that
R[1/\ell]={\mathbb{T}}[1/\ell]
, or even only that
R^{\mathrm{red}}={\mathbb{T}}^{\mathrm{red}}
. In the
\ell_{0}>0
case one typically expects
R
and
{\mathbb{T}}
to have torsion, and so not having an approach to proving
R={\mathbb{T}}
is a noticeable shortcoming to the patching method.

Our work generalizes the numerical criterion to positive defect, and thus gives us
R={\mathbb{T}}
results in non-minimal level by an inductive argument similar to the one in
[
25
]
. Unlike in the
\ell_{0}=0
case, when the patching and numerical criterion arguments were treated fairly independently of each other, when
\ell_{0}>0
we are compelled to work at a patched level to apply our numerical criterion. This is one of the key insights of this paper: patching is a necessary prelude to using our numerical criterion for applications to modularity lifting in positive defect.

In the positive defect case patching is much more intrinsic to deducing non-minimal
R={\mathbb{T}}
theorems from their minimal analogs than in the defect 0 case. In both the defect
0
case and the positive defect case the augmentation
\lambda\colon R_{\infty}\to\mathcal{O}
is crucial for formulating the numerical criterion. In the positive defect case the augmentation which we consider does not need to factor though the global deformation ring
R_{\infty}\twoheadrightarrow R
, and indeed may not have any relation to Galois representations or modular forms. This is necessary, as
R
may not have any characteristic 0 points if the residual representation
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}
has no appropriate characteristic 0 lifts, and so the augmentation
\lambda
may only exist after patching.

As an important step in our proof of Theorem
F
we have to generalize the classical change of congruence modules arguments of Ribet
[
46
]
(in codimension
c=0
setting) to the present higher (
c>0
) codimension setting to deduce that the Wiles defect
\delta_{R_{\infty}’}(M_{\infty}’)
vanishes, for patched rings
R_{\infty}’
and modules
M_{\infty}’
in non-minimal situations, from
\delta_{R_{\infty}}(M_{\infty})=0
, for patched rings
R_{\infty}
and modules
M_{\infty}
in minimal situations. For this one uses the following results in concert:

(1)

patching in Theorem
10.6
;

(2)

Theorem
11.3
which gives a self-duality statement for a patched module that arises from patching complexes with self-duality properties;

(3)

a version of Ihara’s lemma for surjectivity of maps between patched modules of different levels in §
13.7
;

(4)

change of congruence module arguments for our congruence modules in higher codimensions which is inspired by
[
46
]
and uses in addition the commutative algebra results Lemma
3.10
and Proposition
4.4
, as well as Theorem
7.4
.

Earlier work

As noted before, our work is inspired by work of Wiles, Lenstra, and Diamond. The result of Wiles and Lenstra has been extended by Huneke and Ulrich
[
31
]
, and Zarzuela
[
57
]
, to cover more general surjective maps of rings
A\to B
, where
B
need not be a discrete valuation ring. The direct precursors to the commutative algebra developed in this paper are
[
26
]
,
[
7
]
,
[
8
]
(and its appendix),
[
12
]
, as well as the work of Tilouine and Urban in
[
54
]
. We describe briefly the previous work, to provide historical context.

The results of
[
26
, Appendix]
and
[
12
]
generalized results of Wiles, Lenstra and Diamond in
[
56
,
41
,
25
]
so that rather than asking that the ring
A
or the module
M
be finite and flat over
\mathcal{O}
, it is only required that
M
has positive depth. Theorem
A
in this paper also requires that
M
have sufficient depth.

The papers
[
7
,
8
,
54
]
, introduced the notion of Wiles defect of rings, and showed that it has arithmetic significance. The appendix to
[
8
]
(written by N. Fakhruddin and the second author) established a formula for the Wiles defect proposed by Venkatesh for
\mathcal{O}
-algebras
A\in\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(0)
with
\dim A=1
. This involves the André–Quillen cohomology module
\mathrm{D}^{1}(A/\mathcal{O},E/\mathcal{O})
, where
E
is the fraction field of
\mathcal{O}
, and another invariant that he defined using suitable complete intersections that map onto
A
. The main article
[
8
]
extended the definition of Venkatesh invariants, and Wiles defect, to higher dimensional rings
A
that are Cohen–Macaulay and flat over
\mathcal{O}
and with an augmentation
\lambda\colon A\to\mathcal{O}
such that
A
is smooth at
\lambda
, basically by proving that the invariants remain unchanged on going modulo regular sequences, so that one can reduce to dimension one.

The paper
[
12
]
considered Wiles defect for
A\in\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(0)
, and
M
a finitely generated
A
-module. One of the main observations of that paper,
[
12
, Theorem 1.2]
, is a formula relating the Wiles defects of the module
M
and ring
A
. This is generalized in our paper Lemma
3.7
and plays an important role in the proof of Theorem
B
.

The present paper defines the Wiles defect for all rings
A
in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
for arbitrary
c
. This uses invariants
\Phi_{A}
and
\Psi_{A}
that are more direct generalizations of the invariants of Wiles; their definition does not use André–Quillen homology of rings.
They do not remain invariant individually on going modulo regular sequences, but the Wiles defect
\delta_{A}
does; see Theorem
C
and the discussion around it. Our invariants are defined in a way that is more intrinsic to
A
than the ones introduced by Venkatesh which use as a crutch complete intersections mapping to
A
.

Further applications

In a sequel
[
32
]
to this work, we have used the techniques of this paper to relate our work on congruence modules in positive codimension to “zeta-elements” (see
[
55
]
) for the adjoint motive of a newform
f
of weight at least 2 which is ordinary at
p
. (For this paragraph we denote the prime
\ell
of the rest of the paper by
p
.) This is contained in
[
32
, Proposition 2.5, Theorem 3.7]
that we briefly describe. The
p
-stablization of
f
induces an augmentation
\lambda_{f}:\mathbb{T}^{\rm ord}\to\mathcal{O}
of Hida’s ordinary
p
-adic Hecke algebra. By Hida theory
{\mathbb{T}}^{\rm ord}
is finite flat over the Iwasawa algebra
\Lambda=\mathcal{O}[\![{T}]\!]
(and hence
\operatorname{Ker}\lambda_{f}
has height
1
), and it is smooth at
\lambda_{f}
. Let
{\rm Ad}_{f}
be the adjoint motive of
f
,
L(s,{\rm Ad}_{f})
the corresponding degree 3
L
-function and
\rho_{f}:G_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O})
the Galois representaiton associated to a
p
-adic place of the Hecke field
K_{f}
of
f
. Our construction of the congruence module
\Psi_{\lambda_{f}}(\mathbb{T}^{\rm ord})
leads to “zeta elements” in Galois cohomology
H^{1}_{\rm ord}(\mathbb{Q},{\rm Ad}\rho_{f})
whose image in quotients of the corresponding local cohomology groups at
p
are related to the congruence module
\Psi_{\lambda_{f}}(\mathbb{T})
. Here
{\mathbb{T}}
is a classical Hecke algebra of fixed level that is finite flat over
\mathcal{O}
and
\Psi_{\lambda_{f}}(\mathbb{T})
is its classical (codimension 0) congruence module. Hida’s work in
[
30
]
relates the length of the congruence module
\Psi_{\mathbb{T}}
to an
L
-value
L(1,{\rm Ad}_{f})
.

In
[
8
]
we study situations when we have
R=\mathbb{T}
without the rings being complete intersections and determine the Wiles defect
\delta_{\lambda_{f}}(R)
at
\lambda_{f}:R\to\mathcal{O}
; we show it is given by
\sum_{q}\delta_{\lambda_{f}}(R_{q})
of Wiles defects of the corresponding local deformation rings
R_{q}
. The work of
[
8
]
and the present paper lead to the intriguing possibility of proving the Bloch-Kato conjecture for
L(1,{\rm Ad}_{f})
in cases which will go beyond the known results in
[
23
]
that are proved when the map
R\to\mathbb{T}
is an isomorphism of complete intersections. It also raises the intriguing possibility that
\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Psi_{\lambda_{f}}(R_{q})
is related to
{\rm ord}_{\varpi}c_{q}
where
c_{q}
is the local Tamagawa number at
q
which Bloch-Kato associate to the motive
{\rm Ad}_{f}
. This would be a local analog of the results in
[
32
]
; we will pursue this in future work.

The residual representations
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}
and non-minimal levels
\mathcal{N}_{\Sigma}
that we allow in Theorem
F
are restrictive: for instance we assume
q_{v}\not\equiv 1\pmod{\ell}
if
v
divides
\mathcal{N}_{\Sigma}
and
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is unramified at
v
. In forthcoming work with Fred Diamond we will extend the methods of this paper to prove a version of Theorem
F
without such restrictions: the level raising arguments in Part
III
become more complicated in the general case. We will also prove a more general version of Theorem
I
which will include proving under the necessary assumptions the existence of maximal ideals of the Hecke algebra acting on
\operatorname{H}^{1}(X^{\mathfrak{D}}_{\Sigma},\omega_{\mathcal{O}})
that correspond to maximal ideals
\mathfrak{m}_{\varnothing}
of the Hecke algebra acting on
\operatorname{H}^{1}(X^{\varnothing}_{\Sigma\cup\mathfrak{D}},\omega_{\mathcal{O}})
(see Remark
(Wt1)
below); namely the converse to Theorem
I
(1).

Comparison to the literature

The question of what might be the right generalization of Wiles numerical criterion to positive defect has been considered before us. For example in
[
18
]
Calegari proves some non-minimal modularity lifting theorems in the case of weight one forms. There he combines known multiplicity one theorems for the relevant Hecke modules with the patching argument from
[
16
]
. He remarks in the introduction to the paper that in the weight one case he considers, as the relevant Hecke rings
{\mathbb{T}}^{1}
are not complete intersections in general, and may not be flat over
\mathcal{O}
, the arguments of Wiles in
[
56
, Chapter 2]
seem not to be applicable. The work of this paper gives a work around such an obstruction. Our methods apply unconditionally, to prove the non-minimal
R={\mathbb{T}}
theorems of
[
18
]
without a priori using any multiplicity one theorems. As explained above we use our methods to prove similar results in the setting of Shimura curves (Theorems
H
and
I
above): the methods of
[
18
]
do not extend to this case.

The work of Calegari and Venkatesh in
[
17
]
considers level raising in the positive defect case—for example in the Bianchi case—and remarks that a form of Ihara’s lemma is true for homology in degree one of Bianchi manifolds. In our work we apply Ihara’s lemma at a patched level: as the patched complexes are then quasi-isomorphic to a self-dual module, Ihara’s lemma gives that the growth of the congruence module we define is given by an Euler factor.

Part I
Commutative algebra of congruence modules

In this part we introduce congruence modules for a suitable class of local rings, and establish some of their fundamental properties. Although the focus is on the pure commutative algebraic aspects of congruence modules, the results we prove, and even their formulations, are dictated by applications to number theory, and indeed our intuition about congruence modules comes from that realm. Nevertheless, many of the statements are interesting in their own right, so with an eye towards possible applications, also to commutative algebra, the setting is more general and the development is more in-depth than is strictly needed for the number theory applications presented in this work.

2.
Congruence modules and cotangent modules

Throughout
\mathcal{O}
is a discrete valuation ring, with uniformizer
\varpi
.

2.1
.

We write
\operatorname{tors}(U)
for the torsion submodule of an
\mathcal{O}
-module
U
. The
torsion-free quotient
of
U
, denoted
{U}^{\operatorname{tf}}
, is the cokernel of the inclusion
\operatorname{tors}(U)\subseteq U
. There is thus an exact sequence of
\mathcal{O}
-modules

0\longrightarrow\operatorname{tors}(U)\longrightarrow U\longrightarrow{U}^{\operatorname{tf}}\longrightarrow 0\,,

functorial in
U
. When
U
is finitely generated, so is
{U}^{\operatorname{tf}}
and hence it is a free
\mathcal{O}
-module. In particular, the sequence above splits. Though there is no functorial splitting, the existence of a splitting does mean that for any additive functor, say
E(-)
, on finitely generated
\mathcal{O}
-modules, the induced map

E(U)\longrightarrow E({U}^{\operatorname{tf}})

remains surjective. This remark will be used often, as will be the following ones: Consider a map of
\mathcal{O}
-modules

\alpha\colon U\longrightarrow V

and the map
{\alpha}^{\operatorname{tf}}\colon{U}^{\operatorname{tf}}\to{V}^{\operatorname{tf}}
. The induced map
\operatorname{Coker}\alpha\to\operatorname{Coker}({\alpha}^{\operatorname{tf}})
is surjective; in particular, when
\alpha
is onto, so is
{\alpha}^{\operatorname{tf}}
. If
\operatorname{Ker}\alpha
is torsion, then the induced map
{\alpha}^{\operatorname{tf}}
is injective.

2.2
.

Throughout this manuscript
A
is a noetherian local ring and
\lambda_{A}\colon A\to\mathcal{O}
a surjective local homomorphism. We set

\mathfrak{p}_{A}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{Ker}(\lambda_{A})\qquad\text{and}\qquad c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}0pt\mathfrak{p}_{A}\,.

Thus
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
is a prime ideal in
A
and
c
is its height, also known as the codimension, whence the choice of notation. It is also the Krull dimension of the local ring
A_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}
. Our focus will be on rings for which
c
is the rank of the cotangent module of
\lambda_{A}
; see Lemma
2.7
. One has that

\dim A\geq 0pt\mathfrak{p}_{A}+\dim(A/\mathfrak{p}_{A})=c+1\,;

we place no restrictions on
\dim A
.

Torsion-free quotients of Ext

We write
\mathsf{D}(A)
for the derived category of all
A
-modules; the suspension (also known as shift) functor is denoted
[-]
. As usual, a module is viewed as complex concentrated in degree
0
. Although the focus of our work is on finitely generated modules, some of the basic definitions extend, and it is helpful in some arguments to do so, to complexes with finite homology. We write
\operatorname{mod}A
for the category of finitely generated
A
-modules. Let
\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\,A)
denote the full subcategory of
\mathsf{D}(A)
consisting of
A
-complexes
M
with
\operatorname{H}_{i}(M)
finitely generated for each
i
and equal to zero for
|i|\gg 0
. For such
M
the
\mathcal{O}
-modules
\operatorname{Tor}^{A}_{i}(\mathcal{O},M)
and
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)
are finitely generated for each integer
i
, and equal to
0
for
i\ll 0
.
For
M
in
\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\,A)
consider the
\mathcal{O}
-module

\operatorname{F}^{i}_{A}(M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)}^{\operatorname{tf}}\,,

namely, the torsion-free quotient of
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)
. Evidently
\operatorname{F}^{n}_{A}(-)
is a functor from
\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\,A)
, the derived category of
A
-modules, to
\mathcal{O}
-modules.

Congruence modules

Let
M
be a finitely generated
A
-module. The map

\lambda_{A}(M)\colon M\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}\otimes_{A}M=M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M

induces a map of
\mathcal{O}
-modules

\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\lambda_{A}(M))\colon\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(M)\longrightarrow\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M)\,;

recall
c=0pt\mathfrak{p}_{A}
. The cokernel of this map is the
congruence
module of
M
:

\Psi_{A}(M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{Coker}\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\lambda_{A}(M))\,.

For a given
A
there may be many choices of an augmentation
\lambda\colon A\to\mathcal{O}
and for some applications it is important to keep track of this dependency, and then it would be better to write
\Psi_{\lambda}(M)
for the congruence module. However, in this work the map
\lambda
is fixed, so we stick to the notation above.

We write
\Psi_{A}
, instead of
\Psi_{A}(A)
, for the congruence module of
A
. See the discussion around Proposition
2.10
and
4.8
for connections to other notions of congruence modules in the literature. It is easy to check that the assignment
M\mapsto\Psi_{A}(M)
is a functor from
\operatorname{mod}A
to
\operatorname{mod}\mathcal{O}
; see
3.8
.

Here is a simple but useful observation.

2.3
.

For any finitely generated
\mathcal{O}
-module
U
, viewed as an
A
-module via
\lambda_{A}
, the surjection
U\to{U}^{\operatorname{tf}}
is split with torsion kernel as an
\mathcal{O}
-map, and hence also as an
A
-map—see
2.1
—and so it induces the first isomorphism below:

\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(U)\xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}({U}^{\operatorname{tf}})\cong\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}{U}^{\operatorname{tf}}\,.

The second one holds because the
\mathcal{O}
-module
{U}^{\operatorname{tf}}
is finite free. In particular, for any finitely generated
A
-module
M
, there is a natural isomorphism:

(2.4)

\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M)\cong\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}{(M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M)}^{\operatorname{tf}}

Regularity

Given a prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
in
A
, we say
A
is
regular at
\mathfrak{p}
to mean that the local ring
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
is regular. The result below is the starting point of our work.

Theorem 2.5
.

In the context of
2.2
, the ring
A
is regular at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
if and only if the
\mathcal{O}
-module
\Psi_{A}
is torsion. When this holds, so do the following statements:

(1)

The
\mathcal{O}
-modules
\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(A)
and
\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O})
are of rank one;

(2)

The map
\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\lambda_{A})\colon\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(A)\to\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O})
is injective;

(3)

The
\mathcal{O}
-module
\Psi_{A}
is torsion and cyclic;

Proof.

Write
\mathfrak{p}
instead of
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
and let
k(\mathfrak{p})
be the field of fractions of
\mathcal{O}
; it is also the residue field of the local ring
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
. For any integer
i
there is an isomorphism

\operatorname{Ext}_{A}^{i}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})_{\mathfrak{p}}\cong\operatorname{Ext}_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{i}(k(\mathfrak{p}),k(\mathfrak{p}))

of
k(\mathfrak{p})
-vector spaces. It is well-known that the vector space on the right is nonzero for each
0\leq i\leq c
, hence the
\mathcal{O}
-module
\operatorname{Ext}_{A}^{i}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})
has nonzero rank for the same range of
i
; see, for instance,
[
13
, Corollary 1.3.2, Theorem 1.3.3]
. This observation will be used in what follows.

It helps to introduce one more property equivalent to the regularity of
A
at
\mathfrak{p}
, and prove that the following conditions are equivalent:

(i)

A
is regular at
\mathfrak{p}
;

(ii)

The rank of the map
\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\lambda_{A})
is nonzero;

(iii)

\Psi_{A}
is torsion as an
\mathcal{O}
-module.

(i)
\Leftrightarrow
(ii): Condition (ii) holds if and only if the localization of the map
\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\lambda_{A})
at
\mathfrak{p}
is nonzero; namely the map

\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}(k(\mathfrak{p}),A_{\mathfrak{p}})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}(k(\mathfrak{p}),k(\mathfrak{p}))

induced by the surjection
A_{\mathfrak{p}}\to k(\mathfrak{p})
is nonzero. It remains to recall Lescot’s result
[
42
, 1.4]
that the map above is nonzero if and only if the local ring
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
is regular; see also
[
3
, Theorem 2.4]
.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(iii): Since
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
is regular of Krull dimension
c
one has

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},A)_{\mathfrak{p}}\cong\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}(k(\mathfrak{p}),A_{\mathfrak{p}})\cong k(\mathfrak{p})

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})_{\mathfrak{p}}\cong\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}(k(\mathfrak{p}),k(\mathfrak{p}))\cong k(\mathfrak{p})\,.

Thus the source and target of the map
\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\lambda_{A})
have rank one, and the rank of the map is nonzero, since we already know that (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii). It follows that its cokernel is torsion, justifying (iii).

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(ii): The rank of the
\mathcal{O}
-module
\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})
is nonzero, and
\Psi_{A}
is the cokernel of
\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\lambda_{A})
, so (ii) follows.

This completes the proof of the equivalences of conditions (i), (ii), and (iii). As to remaining claims, (1) and (2) were implicitly verified in that proof that (i)
\Rightarrow
(iii), whilst (3) is a consequence of (1).
∎

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\lambda_{A}\colon A\to\mathcal{O}
be as in
2.2
with
A
is regular at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
, and
M
a finitely generated
A
-module. Then for each integer
n\geq 0pt\mathfrak{p}_{A}+1
one has
\operatorname{F}^{n}_{A}(M)=0
, and the
\mathcal{O}
-module
\Psi_{A}(M)
is torsion.

Proof.

As before we write
\mathfrak{p}
instead of
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
and
k(\mathfrak{p})
for the fraction field of
\mathcal{O}
. For each
i
there is an isomorphism of
k(\mathfrak{p})
-vector spaces

\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)_{\mathfrak{p}}\cong\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}(k(\mathfrak{p}),M_{\mathfrak{p}})\,.

As
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
is regular of dimension
c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}0pt\mathfrak{p}
, for
i\geq c+1
one has
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}(k(\mathfrak{p}),M_{\mathfrak{p}})=0
. Thus the
\mathcal{O}
-module
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)
is torsion, and hence
\operatorname{F}^{i}_{A}(M)=0
for
i\geq c+1
.

It remains to verify that
\Psi_{A}(M)
is torsion; equivalently, with
\pi\colon M\to M/\mathfrak{p}M
the natural surjection, the cokernel of
\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\pi)
is torsion; equivalently that

\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}(k(\mathfrak{p}),\pi_{\mathfrak{p}})\colon\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}(k(\mathfrak{p}),M_{\mathfrak{p}})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}(k(\mathfrak{p}),M_{\mathfrak{p}}/\mathfrak{p}M_{\mathfrak{p}})

is onto. Replacing
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
by
R
and
M_{\mathfrak{p}}
by
N
, we find ourselves in the situation where
R
is a regular local ring, with residue field
k(\mathfrak{p})
and Krull dimension
c
, and
N
is an
R
-module. The desired result is that the map
\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{R}(k(\mathfrak{p}),\lambda)
is onto, where
\lambda\colon N\to N/\mathfrak{m}N
is the natural map with
\mathfrak{m}
the maximal ideal of
R
.

Since
R
is regular, the
R
-module
k(\mathfrak{p})
has a finite free resolution, so for any
R
-module
N
the natural map

\operatorname{RHom}_{R}(k(\mathfrak{p}),R)\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{R}N\longrightarrow\operatorname{RHom}_{R}(k(\mathfrak{p}),N)

is a quasi-isomorphism. Again because
R
is regular
\operatorname{RHom}_{R}(k(\mathfrak{p}),R)\simeq k(\mathfrak{p})[-c]
; this fact yields a natural isomorphism

k(\mathfrak{p})\otimes_{R}N\xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{R}(k(\mathfrak{p}),N)\,.

We apply this also for the
R
-module
N/\mathfrak{m}N
. Then the map
N\to N/\mathfrak{m}N
induces a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(\mathfrak{p})\otimes_{R}N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(\mathfrak{p})\otimes_{R}(N/\mathfrak{m}N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{R}(k(\mathfrak{p}),N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{R}(k(\mathfrak{p}),N/\mathfrak{m}N)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

It remains to note that the map in the top row, and hence in the bottom row, is even an isomorphism.
∎

Cotangent modules

We are also interested in the cotangent module
\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2}
of the map
\lambda_{A}
. This module too detects when
A
is in the category
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}
.

Lemma 2.7
.

For
\lambda_{A}\colon A\to\mathcal{O}
as in
2.2
there is an equality

\operatorname{rank}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2})=\operatorname{edim}A_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}\,.

Thus
0pt\mathfrak{p}_{A}\leq\operatorname{rank}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2})
; equality holds if and only if
A
is regular at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
.

Proof.

We write
\mathfrak{p}
instead of
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
, and let
e\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{rank}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2})
. With
\mathfrak{m}
the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{p}A_{\mathfrak{p}}
of the local ring
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
, one has an isomorphism

\mathfrak{m}/\mathfrak{m}^{2}=(\mathfrak{p}/\mathfrak{p}^{2})_{\mathfrak{p}}\cong k(\mathfrak{p})^{e}

of
k(\mathfrak{p})
-vector spaces, where
k(\mathfrak{p})
is field of fractions of
\mathcal{O}
, which is also the residue field of
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
. This justifies the first part of the statement. This also yields inequality
0pt{\mathfrak{p}}\leq e
. Since
0pt{\mathfrak{p}}=e
if and only if
A
is regular at
\mathfrak{p}
, by definition
[
13
, §2.2]
, the second part of the result follows as well.
∎

The preceding result has to do with the torsion-free quotient of the cotangent module of
\lambda_{A}
. In the sequel, its torsion submodule plays the major role. Set

(2.8)

\Phi_{A}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{tors}{(\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2})}\,.

When
c=0
this is all of the cotangent module for the latter is torsion when
A
is regular at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
, by Lemma
2.7
.

Wiles defect

Let
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}
be the category of pairs
(A,\lambda_{A})
where
A
is a complete noetherian local ring and
\lambda_{A}\colon A\to\mathcal{O}
is a surjective local homomorphism with
A
regular at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{Ker}(\lambda_{A})
. The morphisms in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}
are the obvious ones. We impose the hypothesis that
A
is complete only for ease of exposition, especially in Section
5
and beyond. Often we speak of the ring
A
being in the category, rather than the pair
(A,\lambda_{A})
. Let
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
be the subcategory of
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}
of rings
A
with
0pt\mathfrak{p}_{A}=c
; equivalently
\operatorname{rank}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2})=c
; see Lemma
2.7
.

Fix
A
in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
. The
Wiles defect
of finitely generated
A
-module
M
is the integer

(2.9)

\delta_{A}(M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\operatorname{rank}_{A_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}}\!M_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}})\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}{\Phi_{A}}-\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}{\Psi_{A}(M)}\,.

For
c=0
this invariant has been studied in
[
7
,
12
]
; see the result below. For general
c
but only for
M=A
and Cohen–Macaulay, an analogous invariant was introduced in
[
8
]
; see
4.8
and
8.4
. we have a lot more to say about this invariant from Section
7
onwards.

The result below reconciles the definition of congruence modules introduced in this work with the one when
c=0
that has been discussed in the literature.

Proposition 2.10
.

Suppose
A
is in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(0)
and set
I_{A}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}A[\mathfrak{p}_{A}]
. For any finitely generated
A
-module
M
there is a natural surjection of
\mathcal{O}
-modules

\frac{M}{M[\mathfrak{p}_{A}]+M[I_{A}]}\twoheadrightarrow\Psi_{A}(M)\,.

with kernel a torsion
\mathcal{O}
-module. This map is an isomorphism if
0pt_{A}M\geq 1
.

Proof.

Given the equality

\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M)=M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M

the congruence module of the
M
is the cokernel of the composition

M[\mathfrak{p}_{A}]\longrightarrow M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M\to{(M/{\mathfrak{p}_{A}M})}^{\operatorname{tf}}\,.

Consider the exact sequence of
\mathcal{O}
-modules

0\longrightarrow\frac{M[I_{A}]}{\mathfrak{p}_{A}M}\longrightarrow\frac{M}{\mathfrak{p}_{A}M}\longrightarrow\frac{M}{M[I_{A}]}\longrightarrow 0

The term on the left is
\mathcal{O}
-torsion for it is annihilated by
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
and
I_{A}
, and so a module over the ring
\Psi_{A}
. Thus there are natural surjections

M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M\twoheadrightarrow M/M[I_{A}]\twoheadrightarrow{(M/{\mathfrak{p}_{A}M})}^{\operatorname{tf}}

and the map on the right is bijective when
0pt_{A}M\geq 1
, for then the term in the middle is torsion-free, being a submodule of
\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(I_{A},M)
. This gives the desired result. Along the way we get that when
0pt_{A}M\geq 1
, one has

M/M[I_{A}]={\left(M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M\right)}^{\operatorname{tf}}

so one can recover the module on the left without recourse to
I_{A}
.
∎

Here is an example that shows that the map in the preceding proposition is not always an isomorphism.

Example 2.11
.

Let
A\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{O}[\![{t}]\!]/(\varpi^{2}t)
and
M\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}A/(\varpi^{3})
. Then
M
is
\mathcal{O}
-torsion so
\Psi_{A}(M)=0
, whereas, in the notation of Proposition
2.10
one has
I_{A}=(\varpi^{2})
, so

M[\mathfrak{p}_{A}]=(\varpi^{2})M\quad\text{and}\quad M[I_{A}]=(\varpi,t)M\,,

and hence
M/(M[\mathfrak{p}_{A}]+M[I_{A}])=\mathcal{O}/(\varpi)
.

3.
Depth conditions

Two invariants of a finitely generated
A
-module
M
play a key role in what follows: its depth as an
A
-module, denoted
0pt_{A}M
, and the length of the longest
M
-regular sequence in
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
, denoted
\operatorname{grade}(\mathfrak{p}_{A},M)
. Their value is closely connected to the property that
M
is free at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
. These interdependencies are clarified in the next two results; see also
3.3
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
A
be in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
and
M\in\operatorname{mod}A
.

(1)

If
M_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}\neq 0
, then
\operatorname{grade}(\mathfrak{p}_{A},M)\leq c
and
0pt_{A}M\leq c+1
.

(2)

If
\operatorname{grade}(\mathfrak{p}_{A},M)\leq c
and
0pt_{A}M\geq c+1
, then in fact
\operatorname{grade}(\mathfrak{p}_{A},M)=c
and
0pt_{A}M=c+1
.

(3)

If
\operatorname{grade}(\mathfrak{p}_{A},M)\geq c
, then
M
is free at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
.

In particular,
0ptA\leq c+1\leq\dim A
.

Proof.

(1) When
M_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}
is nonzero, its depth as an
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
-module is at most
\dim A_{\mathfrak{p}}
; this justifies the second inequality below:

\operatorname{grade}(\mathfrak{p}_{A},M)\leq 0pt_{A_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}}M_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}\leq c\,.

The first one is clear. This justifies the first inequality in (1). The second one follows, because for any finitely generated
A
-module
M
there in an inequality

0pt_{A}M\leq\operatorname{grade}(\mathfrak{p}_{A},M)+1\,.

This holds by
[
13
, Exercise 1.2.23]
, since
\dim(A/\mathfrak{p}_{A})=\dim\mathcal{O}=1
.

(2) The hypotheses gives inequalities

c+1\leq 0pt_{A}M\leq\operatorname{grade}(\mathfrak{p}_{A},M)+1\leq c+1\,.

Thus one has equalities everywhere.

(3) The hypothesis implies that the depth of
M_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}
as a module over
A_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}
is at least
c
. Since
A_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}
is a regular local ring of dimension
c
, the equality of Auslander and Buchsbaum
[
13
, Theorem 1.3.3]
implies that
M
is free at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
.

Finally, the inequality
0ptA\leq c+1
follows from (1), as
A_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}\neq 0
, whereas the upper bound on
\dim A
is the special case
\mathfrak{p}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathfrak{p}_{A}
of the inequality

\dim(A/\mathfrak{p})+0pt\mathfrak{p}\leq\dim A

that holds for any prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
in
A
.
∎

In what follows the focus will be on the modules whose depth is at least
c+1
.
One reason for this is the following result.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
A
be in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
and
M\in\operatorname{mod}A
with
0pt_{A}M\geq c+1
.

(1)

If
M
is supported at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
, then
\operatorname{grade}(\mathfrak{p}_{A},M)=c
and
0pt_{A}M=c+1
.

(2)

If
M
is not supported at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
, then
\operatorname{grade}(\mathfrak{p}_{A},M)=0pt_{A}M
.

(3)

M
is free at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
.

(4)

\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)
is torsion-free as an
\mathcal{O}
-module.

Proof.

Part (1) is immediate from parts (1) and (2) of Lemma
3.1
.

(2) Since the only prime ideals containing
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
are itself and the maximal ideal of
A
, by
[
13
, Proposition 1.2.10]
there is an equality

\operatorname{grade}(\mathfrak{p}_{A},M)=\min\{0pt_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}M_{\mathfrak{p}},0pt_{A}M\}\,.

Thus if
M
is not supported at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
, then the equality above gives

\operatorname{grade}(\mathfrak{p}_{A},M)=0pt_{A}M\,,

since the depth of
0
is infinity.

(3) Since
\operatorname{grade}(\mathfrak{p}_{A},M)\geq c
, by (1) and (2), one can invoke Lemma
3.1
(3).

(4) Let
k
denote the residue field of
\mathcal{O}
, and consider the exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}\xrightarrow{\ \varpi\ }\mathcal{O}\longrightarrow k\longrightarrow 0\,.

It induces the exact sequence of
\mathcal{O}
-modules

\cdots\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(k,M)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)\xrightarrow{\ \varpi\ }\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)\longrightarrow\cdots

Since
0pt_{A}M=c+1
one has
\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(k,M)=0
; see
[
13
, Theorem 1.2.5]
. Thus the sequence above implies that
\varpi
is not a zero-divisor on
\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)
.
∎

3.3
.

Perhaps it is helpful to summarize the results above as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0pt_{A}M\geq c+1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{grade}(\mathfrak{p}_{A},M)\geq c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0pt_{A}M\geq c}
M
is free at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}

The two conditions on the right are vacuous when
c=0
, but not so the left-most one. This condition comes up in the criteria for freeness and complete intersection presented in Section
9
. The condition on grade comes up in arguments involving reduction to the case
c=0
; see Section
8
. Only the weaker lower bound on
0pt_{A}M
is required for the invariance of domain property discussed in Section
7
.

We round up this discussion on depth with the following observation that is needed for reduction to
c=0
. In what follows, given a prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
in a ring
A
, we write
\mathfrak{p}^{(2)}
for the ideal
\mathfrak{p}^{2}A_{\mathfrak{p}}\cap A
; this is the second symbolic power of
\mathfrak{p}
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Fix
A
in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
for
c\geq 1
, and an
f\in\mathfrak{p}_{A}
nonzero in
A_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}
. The local ring
A/fA
is in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}
if and only if
f\not\in\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{(2)}
; in that case
A/fA
is in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c-1)
.

Proof.

Set
B\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}A/fA
; since
f
is in
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
the map
\lambda_{A}\colon A\to\mathcal{O}
factors through
B
, yielding an augmentation
\lambda_{B}\colon B\to\mathcal{O}
. Its kernel,
\mathfrak{p}_{B}
, equals
\mathfrak{p}_{A}/f
. Since the local ring
A_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}
is regular the quotient ring
B_{\mathfrak{p}_{B}}\cong A_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}/fA_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}
is regular if and only if the image of
f
is not in
\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2}A_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}
, that is to say,
f
is not in
\mathfrak{p}^{(2)}
; see
[
13
, Proposition 2.2.4]
. When this property holds
\dim B_{\mathfrak{p}_{B}}=\dim A_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}-1
, so
B
is in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c-1)
.
∎

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
A
be a local ring in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
and
M
a finitely generated
A
-module with
\operatorname{grade}(\mathfrak{p}_{A},M)\geq c
. Given any integer
1\leq n\leq c
, there exists a sequence
\boldsymbol{g}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}g_{1},\dots,g_{n}
in
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
such that:

(1)

The ring
A/{\boldsymbol{g}}A
is in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c-n)
;

(2)

The sequence
\boldsymbol{g}
is
M
-regular and
0pt_{A/{\boldsymbol{g}}A}(M/{\boldsymbol{g}}M)=0pt_{A}M-n
.

When
A
is Cohen–Macaulay,
\boldsymbol{g}
can be chosen to be regular on
A
as well.

Proof.

It suffices to verify the statement for
n=1
. Given Lemma
3.4
, one has to find an element
g
in
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
that avoids the associated primes of
M
and also
\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{(2)}
. This can be done by a standard prime avoidance argument; see
[
36
, Theorem 81]
.
∎

We illustrate the use of these conditions by establishing a link between the Wiles defect of a module to that of the underlying ring, and by tracking the change in the congruence modules under certain maps of
A
-modules.

A defect formula

The natural morphism of complexes

\operatorname{RHom}_{A}(\mathcal{O},A)\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{A}M\longrightarrow\operatorname{RHom}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)

and the Künneth map induce the map of
\mathcal{O}
-modules

\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},A)\otimes_{A}M\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)\,,

and hence the map

\eta_{M}\colon\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(A)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}{(M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M)}^{\operatorname{tf}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(M)\,.

This map fits into a commutative diagram of
\mathcal{O}
-modules

(3.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(A)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}{(M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M)}^{\operatorname{tf}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}{(M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M)}^{\operatorname{tf}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{c}(\lambda_{A})\otimes\textrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{c}{(\lambda_{A}(M))}}

The isomorphism on the right is from (
2.4
). In fact, one has such a diagram for each cohomological index
i
. This leads to the following “defect formula” for congruence modules. It generalizes
[
12
, Theorem 1.2]
, which is the case
c=0
, for then
\operatorname{Coker}(\eta_{M})=M[\mathfrak{p}_{A}]/I_{A}M
, where
I_{A}=A[\mathfrak{p}_{A}]
. See Proposition
4.9
for another point of view on
\operatorname{Coker}(\eta_{M})
.

Lemma 3.7
.

When
M
is free at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
, the map
\operatorname{F}^{c}{(\lambda_{A}(M))}
is injective, and there is an exact sequence of
\mathcal{O}
-modules

0\longrightarrow\operatorname{Coker}(\eta_{M})\longrightarrow\Psi_{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}{{(M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M)}^{\operatorname{tf}}}\longrightarrow\Psi_{A}(M)\longrightarrow 0

and hence there is an equality

\delta_{A}(M)=\operatorname{rank}_{A_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}}(M_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}})\cdot\delta_{A}(A)+\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\operatorname{Coker}(\eta_{M})\,.

Proof.

The map
\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\lambda_{A})\colon\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(A)\to\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O})
is injective, by Theorem
2.5
. Since
M
is free at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
, the map
\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\lambda_{A}(M))
is injective when localized at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
, and hence injective. Given this observation the stated exact sequence is immediate from (
3.6
). Since the
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
-module
M_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}
is free, its rank is the rank of the
\mathcal{O}
-module
{{(M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M)}}^{\operatorname{tf}}
. This remark, and the given exact sequence, yield the desired equality.
∎

3.8
.

Let
A
be in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
and
\pi\colon M\to N
a map of finitely generated
A
-modules. This data induces the commutative diagram

(3.9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(N/\mathfrak{p}_{A}N)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\pi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\overline{\pi})}

where
\overline{\pi}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\pi\otimes_{A}\mathcal{O}
. This induces a map
\Psi_{A}(M)\to\Psi_{A}(N)
of
\mathcal{O}
-modules. Thus
\Psi_{A}(-)
is a functor from the category of finitely generated
A
-modules to
\mathcal{O}
-modules.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
A
be in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
and
\pi\colon M\to N
a map in
\operatorname{mod}A
such that
M
and
N
are free at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
and of the same rank.

(1)

The free-
\mathcal{O}
-modules
\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(M)
and
\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(N)
have the same rank.

(2)

When
\pi
is surjective,
\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\pi)
is injective and
\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\overline{\pi})
is bijective, and hence

\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Psi_{A}(M)=\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Psi_{A}(N)+\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\operatorname{Coker}\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\pi)\,.

(3)

When
\pi
is injective with cokernel of depth
c+1
, the map
\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\pi)
is bijective.

Proof.

(1) This follows from the fact that the rank of an
\mathcal{O}
-module can be computed after localization at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
, and the isomorphisms

\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(M)_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}\cong\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}\cong\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}}(k(\mathfrak{p}),M_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}})

where
k(\mathfrak{p})
is the residue field of
\mathcal{O}
.

The following observation will be used below: Since
M
and
N
are free of the same rank at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
, when
\pi
is surjective, it is an isomorphism at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
. The same conclusion holds when
\pi
injective and
0pt_{A}\operatorname{Coker}(\pi)\geq c+1
. In particular,
\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\pi)
is bijective when localized at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
and hence
\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\pi)
is injective.

(2) Since
\pi
is surjective, so is
\overline{\pi}
, and hence also the induced map

{(\overline{\pi})}^{\operatorname{tf}}\colon{(M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M)}^{\operatorname{tf}}\xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }{(N/\mathfrak{p}_{A}N)}^{\operatorname{tf}}\,.

That the map is also injective can be verified by localizing at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
, and using the fact
\pi
is an isomorphism at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
. Thus the natural isomorphism
2.4
implies that
\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\overline{\pi})
is bijective as claimed. Given that
\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\pi)
is one-to-one and
\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\overline{\pi})
is bijective, the stated formula for the lengths of congruence modules is immediate from the commutative diagram (
3.9
). Here we also use the fact that the vertical maps in the diagram are one-to-one, since
M
and
N
are free at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
; see Lemma
3.7
.

(3) We already know that
\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\pi)
is injective, so it remains to verify that it is also surjective. Complete
\pi
