to an exact sequence

0\longrightarrow M\longrightarrow N\longrightarrow C\longrightarrow 0\,.

Since
M,N
are free at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
and of the same rank,
C_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}=0
, and so the hypothesis that
0pt_{A}C=c+1
and Lemma
3.2
yield
\operatorname{grade}(\mathfrak{p}_{A},C)=c+1
. Thus
\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},C)=0
, by
[
13
, Theorem 1.2.5]
, and so the exact sequence above induces the exact sequence

\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)\xrightarrow{\ \operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\pi)\ }\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},N)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},C)=0\,.

Therefore the induced map on torsion-free quotients,
\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\pi)
, is also surjective.
∎

4.
Cohen–Macaulay modules

In this section we record results on congruence modules special to Cohen–Macaulay modules, intended for later use. There is also an alternative description of congruence modules which brings up a connection to duality. See also
[
32
, 2.10]
, which contains a more streamlined and transparent description of this pairing.

Dualizing complexes

Since each
A
in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}
is a quotient of a regular ring, namely, a power series ring over
\mathcal{O}
, it has a dualizing complex,
\omega_{A}
, unique up to isomorphism in
\mathsf{D}(A)
, once it is normalized so that
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{A}(k,\omega_{A})\neq 0
precisely when
i=0
; see
[
51
,
Tag 0A7M
]
. Given any
A
-complex
M
in
\mathsf{D}(A)
, set

(4.1)

\omega_{A}(M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{RHom}_{A}(M,\omega_{A})\,.

The dualizing complex captures the depth and the dimension of any nonzero finitely generated
A
-module
M
, in that

(4.2)

\displaystyle 0pt_{A}M

\displaystyle=\min\{i\mid\operatorname{H}_{i}(\omega_{A}(M))\neq 0\}

\displaystyle\dim M

\displaystyle=\max\{i\mid\operatorname{H}_{i}(\omega_{A}(M))\neq 0\}\,.

This follows from Grothendieck’s local duality theorem and the fact that depth and dimension are detected by the local cohomology of
M
; see
[
51
,
Tag 0DWZ
]
and also
[
13
, Theorem 3.5.7]
. The assignment
M\mapsto\omega_{A}(M)
induces an auto-equivalence

\omega_{A}(-)\colon{\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\,A)}^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\ \equiv\ }\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\,A)\,.

Hence the biduality map
M\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}}{\omega_{A}^{2}}(M)
is an isomorphism for
M
in
\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\,A)
.

Cohen–Macaulay modules

Fix
A
in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
. An
A
-module
M
is said to be
Cohen–Macaulay
if it is finitely generated and satisfies
0pt_{A}M\geq\dim M
; equality holds when
M\neq 0
. Thus if
M
is a Cohen–Macaulay
A
-module
\operatorname{H}_{i}(\omega_{A}(M))=0
for all
i\neq\dim_{A}M
. When
M
is a non-zero Cohen-Macaulay module, the
A
-module

(4.3)

M^{\vee}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{H}_{d}(\omega_{A}(M))\quad\text{for $d=\dim_{A}M$}

is also Cohen–Macaulay of dimension
d
; this is easy to verify, given
M\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}}{\omega_{A}^{2}}(M)
. We speak of this as the
dual
of
M
.
Since

\omega_{A}(M)\simeq M^{\vee}[d]\qquad\text{in $\mathsf{D}(A)$.}

the assignment
M\mapsto M^{\vee}
is an auto-equivalence on the category of Cohen–Macaulay
A
-modules. In what follows, we say a Cohen–Macaulay module
M
is
self-dual
to mean that there is an isomorphism of
A
-modules

M\cong M^{\vee}\,.

The result below concerns Cohen–Macaulay modules
M
supported at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
and having maximal possible depth; namely,
\dim M=c+1=0ptM
; see Lemma
3.1
. Let
\nu_{\mathcal{O}}
be the valuation associated to
\mathcal{O}
. The determinant of an endomorphism
\alpha\colon U\to U
of free
\mathcal{O}
-modules of finite rank is denoted
\det\alpha
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
A
be a local ring in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
and
M,N
Cohen–Macaulay
A
-modules of dimension
c+1
, that are self-dual, and have the same rank at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
. If
\pi\colon M\to N
is a surjective map, then

\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Psi_{A}(M)-\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Psi_{A}(N)=\nu_{\mathcal{O}}(\det\alpha)=\nu_{\mathcal{O}}(\det\beta)\,,

where
\alpha\colon\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(M)\to\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(M)
and
\beta\colon\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(N)\to\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(N)
are the compositions

\displaystyle\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(M)\xrightarrow{\ \operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\pi)\ }\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(N)\cong\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(N^{\vee})\xrightarrow{\ \operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}({\pi}^{\vee})\ }\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(M^{\vee})\cong\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(M)

\displaystyle\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(N)\cong\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(N^{\vee})\xrightarrow{\ \operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}({\pi}^{\vee})\ }\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(M^{\vee})\cong\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(M)\xrightarrow{\ \operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}({\pi})\ }\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(N)\,.

Proof.

Since
M
and
N
have depth
c+1
, they are free at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
, by Lemma
3.2
. We can assume their ranks are nonzero, else each term in the desired equality is zero, and also that
\pi
is not an isomorphism. Consider the exact sequence

0\longrightarrow L\longrightarrow M\longrightarrow N\longrightarrow 0

induced by
\pi
. There are (in)equalities

c+1\leq 0pt_{A}L\leq\dim L\leq\dim M=c+1\,,

where the first and last inequality on the left can be verified easily using (
4.2
). Thus
L
too is Cohen–Macaulay of dimension
c+1
. Thus the exact sequence above induces an exact sequence

0\longrightarrow N^{\vee}\xrightarrow{\ \pi^{\vee}\ }M^{\vee}\longrightarrow L^{\vee}\longrightarrow 0\,.

Since
L^{\vee}
is also Cohen–Macaulay of dimension
c+1
, Lemma
3.10
(3) yields that
\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\pi^{\vee})
is bijective. Given this, the desired equalities are immediate from the definition of
\alpha
and
\beta
, and Lemma
3.10
(2).
∎

4.5
.

Let
A
be in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
and
M
a Cohen–Macaulay
A
-module of dimension
c+1
. Then
M^{\vee}
is also Cohen–Macaulay of dimension
c+1
and there is a pairing

M\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{A}M^{\vee}\simeq M\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{A}\omega_{A}(M)[-c-1]\longrightarrow\omega_{A}[-c-1]\,.

Up to a shift, this is the adjoint of the natural biduality map

M\to\operatorname{RHom}_{A}(\operatorname{RHom}_{A}(M,\omega_{A}),\omega_{A})\,;

see, for instance,
[
51
,
Tag 0A5W
]
. This yields a natural pairing

(4.6)

\langle-,-\rangle\colon\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M^{\vee})\longrightarrow\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O})\cong\mathcal{O}\,.

of
\mathcal{O}
-modules defined as follows: Given morphisms

\alpha\colon\mathcal{O}\to M[c]\quad\text{and}\quad\beta\colon\mathcal{O}\to M^{\vee}[c]

in
\mathsf{D}(A)
consider the morphism

\alpha\otimes\beta\colon\mathcal{O}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{A}\mathcal{O}\longrightarrow M[c]\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{A}M^{\vee}[c]\simeq(M\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{A}M^{\vee})[2c]\longrightarrow\omega_{A}[c-1]

This induces by adjunction a morphism

\gamma\colon\mathcal{O}\longrightarrow\operatorname{RHom}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\omega_{A}[c-1])\simeq\mathcal{O}[c]\,,

where the isomorphism holds as the ring
\mathcal{O}
is Gorenstein and
\dim\mathcal{O}=1
. Thus
\gamma
represents an element in
\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})
. Set

\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\text{the class of the morphism $\gamma$ in $\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O})$.}

For the Cohen–Macaulay modules of dimension
c+1
, the pairing above gives another interpretation of the congruence module.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
M
be a Cohen–Macaulay
A
-module of dimension
c+1
. The congruence module
\Psi_{A}(M)
is the cokernel of the map

\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}}(\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M^{\vee}),\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O}))\,.

adjoint to the pairing (
4.6
).

Proof.

One has natural isomorphisms

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M^{\vee})

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{D}(A)}(\mathcal{O},M^{\vee}[c])

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{D}(A)}(\mathcal{O},\omega_{A}(M)[-1])

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{D}(A)}(M[1],\omega_{A}(\mathcal{O}))

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{D}(A)}(M[1],\mathcal{O}[1])

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(M,\mathcal{O})

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}}(M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M,\mathcal{O})

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}}({(M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M)}^{\operatorname{tf}},\mathcal{O})

In this chain, the third isomorphism is a composition of two adjunctions. The fourth uses the fact that
\omega_{A}(\mathcal{O})
is the dualizing complex of
\mathcal{O}
, and hence equal to
\mathcal{O}[1]
since the ring
\mathcal{O}
is Gorenstein; see
[
51
,
Tag 0AX1
,
Tag 0DW7
]
. The rest of the isomorphisms are standard. This yields isomorphisms

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}}(\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M^{\vee}),\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O}))

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}}(\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}}({(M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M)}^{\operatorname{tf}},\mathcal{O}),\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O}))

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}{(M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M)}^{\operatorname{tf}}\,.

With this on hand, and a diagram chase, one gets that the adjoint map in question is naturally isomorphic to the map

\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)\longrightarrow\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}{(M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M)}^{\operatorname{tf}}\,,

induced by
M\to M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M
. This justifies the claim; see (
2.4
).
∎

4.8
.

Let
M
be a Cohen–Macaulay
A
-module of dimension
c+1
. Suppose there is map
R\to A
such that
R
is Gorenstein with
\dim R=c+1
, and
M
is finitely generated over
R
. Thus
M
is maximal Cohen–Macaulay as an
R
-module and hence
M^{\vee}\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(M,R)
. The pairing (
4.6
) is induced by the natural pairing

M\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(M,R)\longrightarrow R

The paring (
4.6
) becomes even more concrete when
c=0
, in which case one can take
R=\mathcal{O}
, and then the pairing is the composite

M[\mathfrak{p}_{A}]\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}}(M,\mathcal{O})[\mathfrak{p}_{A}]\subseteq M\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}}(M,\mathcal{O})\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}\,.

This observation reconciles the definition of congruence modules introduced in this work with that in
[
7
, §3]
.

The following result that expresses, under certain conditions, the invariant
\operatorname{Coker}(\eta_{M})
appearing in the defect formula
3.7
in terms of the Tate cohomology modules,
\operatorname{\hbox to0.0pt{$\smash{\underline{\mathrm{Ext}}}$\hss}\phantom{\mathrm{Ext}}}^{i}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)
, of the pair
(\mathcal{O},M)
; see
[
14
, Definition 6.1.1]
.

Proposition 4.9
.

If
A
is Gorenstein and
M
is maximal Cohen-Macaulay, then there is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{O}
-modules

\operatorname{Coker}(\eta_{M})\cong\operatorname{\hbox to0.0pt{$\smash{\underline{\mathrm{Ext}}}$\hss}\phantom{\mathrm{Ext}}}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)\,.

Proof.

By construction,
\eta_{M}
is the composition of maps

\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},A)\otimes_{A}M\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)\longrightarrow{\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)}^{\operatorname{tf}}\,.

Since
M
is maximal Cohen-Macaulay the
\mathcal{O}
-module
\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)
is torsion-free, by Lemma
3.2
, so the map on the right is an equality. We identify
\eta_{M}
with the map on the left. Moreover, as
A
is Gorenstein one gets that

\omega_{A}(\mathcal{O})\cong\operatorname{Ext}_{A}^{c}(\mathcal{O},A)[-c]\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{Ext}_{A}^{c}(\mathcal{O},A)\cong\mathcal{O}\,.

From the first isomorphism we deduce that

\operatorname{Tor}^{A}_{-i}(M,\omega_{A}(\mathcal{O}))\cong\begin{cases}0&\text{for $i\leq c+1$}\\
\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},A)\otimes_{A}M&\text{for $i=c$}\end{cases}

We leave it to the reader to verify that
\eta_{M}
identifies with the map

\operatorname{H}^{c}\mathbf{N}(\mathcal{O},M)\colon\operatorname{Tor}^{A}_{-c}(M,\omega_{A}(\mathcal{O}))\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)\,.

from
[
14
, Theorem 6.2.5]
. It remains to note that from part (3) of
op. cit.
and the computation above one gets an exact sequence

\operatorname{Tor}^{A}_{-c}(M,\omega_{A}(\mathcal{O}))\xrightarrow{\ \eta_{M}\ }\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)\longrightarrow\operatorname{\hbox to0.0pt{$\smash{\underline{\mathrm{Ext}}}$\hss}\phantom{\mathrm{Ext}}}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)\longrightarrow 0

of
\mathcal{O}
-modules.
∎

5.
Complete intersections

5.1
.

From now on we focus on rings
A
of the form
P/I
where
P\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{O}[\![{t_{1},\dots,t_{n}}]\!]
, a ring of formal power series over
\mathcal{O}
, and
I\subseteq(\varpi)(\boldsymbol{t})+(\boldsymbol{t})^{2}
. Set
\lambda_{P}\colon P\to\mathcal{O}
to be the quotient modulo
(\boldsymbol{t})
, and
\lambda_{A}\colon A\to\mathcal{O}
the induced map.

Let
\boldsymbol{f}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}f_{1},\dots,f_{m}
be a minimal generating set for
I
. The cotangent module
\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2}
depends only on
n
and the linear part of the
f_{i}
, in the following sense: By our assumption on
I
, each
f_{i}
has an unique expression of the form

(5.2)

f_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}{u_{ij}}t_{j}+g_{i}\qquad\text{with $u_{ij}\in(\varpi)\mathcal{O}$ and $g_{i}\in(\boldsymbol{t})^{2}$.}

Then one has a presentation

\mathcal{O}^{m}\xrightarrow{\ (u_{ij})\ }\mathcal{O}^{n}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2}\longrightarrow 0\,.

The following observations will be useful in the sequel.

Lemma 5.3
.

Fix
A
as in
5.1
. Then
A
is in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
if and only if
\operatorname{rank}(u_{ij})=n-c
. When this holds
\dim A\geq c+1
and
0ptI\leq n-c
; the latter inequality is an equality if and only if
\dim A=c+1
.

Proof.

The first part of the statement is immediate from Lemma
2.7
, and the lower bound on
\dim A
has been commented on in
2.2
. As the ring
P
is regular ring,
[
13
, Corollary 2.1.4]
gives the first equality below:

0ptI=\dim P-\dim A\leq n+1-(c+1)=n-c

and the rest of the statement follows.
∎

It also follows from the preceding discussion that when
A
is in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
one can put it in the form
P/(\boldsymbol{f})
, where the relations
\boldsymbol{f}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}f_{1},\dots,f_{m}
satisfy

(5.4)

f_{i}=\varpi^{d_{i}}t_{i}+g_{i}\qquad\text{with $g_{i}\in(\boldsymbol{t})^{2}$}

and
d_{1}\leq\cdots\leq d_{m}
, where
d_{i}<\infty
for
i\leq n-c
and
d_{i}=\infty
for
i>n-c
, and then
\varpi^{d_{i}}=0
. Observe that the
d_{i}
are independent of the choice of the presentation for
A
, since one gets an isomorphism

\frac{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}{\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2}}\cong\mathcal{O}^{c}\oplus\frac{\mathcal{O}}{\varpi^{d_{1}}\mathcal{O}}\oplus\cdots\oplus\frac{\mathcal{O}}{\varpi^{d_{n-c}}\mathcal{O}}

of
\mathcal{O}
-modules. In particular the
d_{i}
are Fitting invariants of
\mathcal{O}
-module
\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2}
Thus

(5.5)

\Phi_{A}\cong\frac{\mathcal{O}}{\varpi^{d_{1}}\mathcal{O}}\oplus\cdots\oplus\frac{\mathcal{O}}{\varpi^{d_{n-c}}\mathcal{O}}

A standard prime-avoidance argument allows us to go further and obtain the result below; see
[
56
, Appendix, Proposition 2]
for the case
c=0
, and also
[
22
, Theorem 5.26]
. The hypothesis on
\dim A
cannot be omitted in the last part of the statement for the complete intersection
C
satisfies
\dim C=c+1
.

Theorem 5.6
.

Fix
A
in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
. In the notation above, let
h\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}0ptI
. There exists a minimal generating set
\boldsymbol{f}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}f_{1},\dots,f_{m}
of the ideal
I
where the
f_{i}
have the form in (
5.4
) and the sequence
f_{1},\dots,f_{h}
is regular. In particular, when
\dim A=c+1
, the complete intersection
C\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{O}[\![{\boldsymbol{t}}]\!]/(f_{1},\dots,f_{h})
is in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
and the surjective map
C\to A
induces isomorphisms

\mathfrak{p}_{C}/\mathfrak{p}_{C}^{2}\xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\Phi_{C}\xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }\Phi_{A}\,.

Proof.

We start by choosing a minimal generating set
f_{1}^{\prime},\dots,f_{m}^{\prime}
for the ideal
I
where the
f^{\prime}_{i}
are of the form in (
5.4
). Since the ring
\mathcal{O}[\![{\boldsymbol{t}}]\!]
is regular, and hence Cohen–Macaulay, the height of
I
equals its grade, that is to say, the length of any maximal
\mathcal{O}[\![{\boldsymbol{t}}]\!]
-regular sequence contained in
I
; see
[
13
, Corollary 2.1.4]
. We claim that there exists a regular sequence
f_{1},\dots,f_{h}
such that

f_{i}\equiv f^{\prime}_{i}\mod I^{2}\,.

It is clear that setting
f_{i}=f^{\prime}_{i}
for
i=h+1,\dots,m
gives the desired sequence. We construct
f_{1},\dots,f_{h}
by an induction argument.

To start with, set
f_{1}=f^{\prime}_{1}
. Suppose that for some
1\leq j<h
elements
f_{1},\dots,f_{j}
have been found, with the desired properties.
We can suppose
f^{\prime}_{j+1}
is zero-divisor in
\overline{A}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{O}[\![{\boldsymbol{t}}]\!]/(f_{1},\dots,f_{j})
; else we can take
f_{j+1}=f^{\prime}_{j+1}
. The ideal
I\overline{A}
contains an element that is not a zerodivisor, because the grade of
I
is
h
, and
f_{1},\dots,f_{j}
is a regular sequence in
I
of length
j<h
. Since the ideal generated by
(f^{\prime}_{j+1})+(f^{\prime}_{j+1},\dots,f^{\prime}_{h})^{2}
in
\overline{A}
agrees with
I\overline{A}
up to radical, a prime avoidance argument yields an element
f^{\prime\prime}
in
(f^{\prime}_{j+1},\dots,f^{\prime}_{h})^{2}
such that
f^{\prime}_{j+1}+f^{\prime\prime}
is a not a zerodivisor in
\overline{A}
; see
[
36
, Theorem 124]
, or
[
13
, Lemma 1.2.2]
. Setting
f_{j+1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}f^{\prime}_{j+1}+f^{\prime\prime}
completes the induction step.

Suppose
\dim A=c+1
, so that
0ptI=n-c
, by Lemma
5.3
. It is clear by the choice of the sequence
\boldsymbol{f}
that the ring
C
is a complete intersection, of dimension
c+1
, that it is in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
, and that the induced map on cotangent modules has the stated properties.
∎

Example 5.7
.

Consider ring
A\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{O}[\![{s,t}]\!]/I
where
I=(\varpi s,\varpi^{2}s+st)
. This ring is in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(1)
and of dimension two. We have at least two choices for a complete intersection in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(1)
mapping onto
A
:
\mathcal{O}[\![{s,t}]\!]/(\varpi s)
or
\mathcal{O}[\![{s,t}]\!]/(\varpi^{2}s+st)
. The former is the one we want, so choosing any minimal generator for
I
would not do.

In the same vein, consider
A\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{O}[\![{s,t}]\!]/I
where
I=(\varpi s,\varpi t,st)
. This ring is in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(0)
and of dimension one. The ring
\mathcal{O}[\![{s,t}]\!]/(\varpi s,\varpi t)
is in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(0)
, maps onto
A
, and has the same cotangent space, but it is not a complete intersection. What we want is the ideal
(\varpi s,\varpi t+st)
, which is a complete intersection.

5.8
.

Next we record a criteria for detecting isomorphisms in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}
in terms of cotangent modules. To that end, consider a surjective map
\varphi\colon A\to B
in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
. Since
\varphi
is surjective, so is the vertical map in the middle in the commutative diagram:

(5.9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2})}^{\operatorname{tf}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{p}_{B}/\mathfrak{p}_{B}^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathfrak{p}_{B}/\mathfrak{p}_{B}^{2})}^{\operatorname{tf}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Thus the vertical map on the right is also surjective; the source and target of this map are free
\mathcal{O}
-modules of the same rank, since
A
and
B
are in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
, so we deduce that the map is an isomorphism. The Snake Lemma then implies
\Phi_{\varphi}
is surjective.

Here is a criterion for detecting isomorphisms in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}
; it generalizes
[
22
, Theorem 5.21]
that covers the case
c=0
. The argument below is essentially the one in
op. cit.
, but couched in terms of
\operatorname{D}_{i}({B}/{A};\mathcal{O})
, the
i
th André–Quillen homology module of the map
A\to B
.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
\varphi\colon A\to B
be a surjective a map in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
with
B
a complete intersection. If
\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Phi_{A}=\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Phi_{B}
, then
\varphi
is an isomorphism, and hence
A
is also a complete intersection.

Proof.

The crucial point is that
\operatorname{D}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}/{B};\mathcal{O})=0
, since
B
is a complete intersection in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
. To see this, consider a presentation
P\to B
, as in
5.1
. Since the augmentation
P\to\mathcal{O}
is generated by a regular sequence
\operatorname{D}_{i}({\mathcal{O}}/{P};\mathcal{O})=0
for all
i\neq 1
, thus the Jacobi-Zariski sequence associated to
P\to B\to\mathcal{O}
yields an injection:

0\longrightarrow\operatorname{D}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}/{B};\mathcal{O})\longrightarrow\operatorname{D}_{1}({B}/{P};\mathcal{O})\,.

Since
B_{\mathfrak{p}_{B}}
is regular, the
\mathcal{O}
-module
\operatorname{D}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}/{B};\mathcal{O})
is torsion, as can be seen by localizing at
\mathfrak{p}_{B}
. On the other hand, the
\mathcal{O}
-module
\operatorname{D}_{1}({B}/{P};\mathcal{O})
is free, as the map
P\to B
is complete intersection. Thus
\operatorname{D}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}/{B};\mathcal{O})=0
.

Consider the commutative diagram (
5.9
). The hypothesis is that the source and target of
\Phi_{\varphi}
have the same length, hence it is a bijection. Then the diagram implies that the map
\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2}\to\mathfrak{p}_{B}/\mathfrak{p}_{B}^{2}
is also a bijection.

Set
J\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{Ker}(\varphi)
. The Jacobi-Zariski sequence associated to
A\to B\to\mathcal{O}
reads

0\longrightarrow(J/J^{2})\otimes_{B}\mathcal{O}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{p}_{B}/\mathfrak{p}_{B}^{2}\longrightarrow 0\,.

The map on the left is one-to-one because
\operatorname{D}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}/{B};\mathcal{O})=0
. The map between the cotangent modules is bijective, so we conclude that
(J/J^{2})\otimes_{B}\mathcal{O}=0
. Hence
J=0
, by Nakayama’s Lemma.
∎

6.
Tate constructions

Let
A
be in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
, with presentation as in
5.1
. When convenient we can assume that the relations
\boldsymbol{f}
defining have
A
the form given in Theorem
5.6
. In this section we completely describe the structure of the torsion-free quotient,
\operatorname{F}^{*}_{A}(\mathcal{O})
, of the
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
\operatorname{Ext}_{A}^{*}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})
, which is the target of the map that computes the congruence module of
A
. The key tool used in the proofs are constructions of free resolutions introduced by Tate
[
52
]
, and developed further in, for instance, Avramov’s book
[
5
]
. We take the latter as our basic reference.

6.1
.

Let
A\langle X\rangle
be the exterior algebra over
A
on indeterminates
X\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
, with
|x_{i}|=1
for each
i
. We make this into a DG (=differential graded) algebra with differential determined by
d(x_{i})=t_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
. Thus
A\langle X\rangle
is the Koszul complex on elements
\boldsymbol{t}
.

Since the
g_{i}
, from
5.2
, are in
(\boldsymbol{t})^{2}
one can write
g_{i}=\sum_{j}g^{\prime}_{ij}t_{j}
with
g^{\prime}_{ij}
in
(\boldsymbol{t})
.
Evidently, the elements

z_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}u_{ij}x_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}g^{\prime}_{ij}x_{j}\qquad\text{for $1\leq i\leq m$,}

are cycles in
A\langle X\rangle
of degree one. Since
\boldsymbol{f}
is a minimal generating set for the ideal
I
, the homology classes
z_{1},\dots,z_{m}
are a minimal generating set for the
\mathcal{O}
-module
\operatorname{H}_{1}(A\langle X\rangle)
. Following Tate
[
52
]
, we adjoin divided powers variables
Y\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{y_{1},\dots,y_{m}\}
, in degree two, to kill these homology classes:

A\langle X,Y\rangle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}A\langle X,Y\mid d(y_{i})=z_{i}\text{ for $1\leq i\leq m$}\rangle\,.

This is sometimes called the
Tate construction
of
\mathcal{O}
over
A
; up to an isomorphism of DG algebras with divided powers, it is independent of the choice of a minimal generating set for the ideal
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
, and the
\mathcal{O}
-module
\operatorname{H}_{1}(A\langle X\rangle)
. The surjection
\lambda_{A}\colon A\to\mathcal{O}
extends to a map
A\langle X,Y\rangle\to\mathcal{O}
of DG algebras over
A
, where
X,Y
map to
0
, for degree reasons. By construction
\operatorname{H}_{1}(A\langle X,Y\rangle)=0
. Unless the local ring
A
is a complete intersection,
A\langle X,Y\rangle
will have homology in higher degrees; see Proposition
6.11
. Adjoining further exterior variables and divided power variables one can extend
A\langle X,Y\rangle
to an acyclic closure

\varepsilon\colon A\langle U\rangle\xrightarrow{\ \simeq\ }\mathcal{O}\,;

see
[
52
, Theorem 1]
and
[
5
, Construction 6.3.1]
. By construction
U=\{U_{i}\}_{i\geqslant 1}
is a graded set of indeterminates, with
U_{1}=X
and
U_{2}=Y
, and
A\langle U\rangle
is the free
A
-algebra with divided powers, on the set
U
.

The differential on
A\langle U\rangle
satisfies

(6.2)

d(A\langle X\rangle)\subseteq(\boldsymbol{t})A\langle X\rangle\quad\text{and}\quad d(A\langle U\rangle)\subseteq(\varpi,\boldsymbol{t})A\langle U\rangle

The first inclusion holds by construction. The second one, which says that the
A\langle U\rangle
is a minimal free resolution of
\mathcal{O}
over
A
, holds because the map
A\to\mathcal{O}
is large, for it has an algebra section, namely the structure map
\mathcal{O}\to A
; for instance, see
[
4
, Corollary 2.7]
. The latter observation does not play a role in the sequel.

Since the acyclic closure is a free resolution of
\mathcal{O}
over
A
, one has

\operatorname{Ext}^{*}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})=\operatorname{H}^{*}(\operatorname{End}_{A}(A\langle U\rangle))\,,

where
\operatorname{End}_{A}(A\langle U\rangle)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(A\langle U\rangle,A\langle U\rangle)
. The complex
\operatorname{End}_{A}(A\langle U\rangle)
is a DG
A
-algebra, with composition product, and induces the product on cohomology. This endows
\operatorname{Ext}^{*}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})
with a multiplication, making it into a (typically non-commutative)
\mathcal{O}
-algebra. In the sequel we view
\operatorname{Ext}^{*}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})
as an
\mathcal{O}
-algebra with this product; it coincides with the Yoneda product, up to a sign.

The quasi-isomorphism
A\langle U\rangle\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{O}
induces a quasi-isomorphism

\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(A\langle U\rangle,A\langle U\rangle)\xrightarrow{\ \simeq\ }\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(A\langle U\rangle,\mathcal{O})

so one can also use the complex on the right to compute
\operatorname{Ext}_{A}^{*}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})
as an
\mathcal{O}
-module. More generally, for any
A
-module, or complex
M
, one has

\operatorname{Ext}_{A}^{*}(\mathcal{O},M)\cong\operatorname{H}^{*}(\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(A\langle U\rangle,M))\,.

This identification is functorial in
M
.

6.3
.

So far we have not used the fact that
A
is in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
, but now we do, to construct an element in
\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})
that maps to a generator for
\operatorname{F}_{A}^{c}(\mathcal{O})
. The discussion below is vacuous when
c=0
so we assume
c\geq 1
.

We can assume the relations
\boldsymbol{f}
defining
A
have the form in (
5.4
). Thus the residue class of the
t_{n-c+1},\cdots,t_{n}
are a basis for the free
\mathcal{O}
-module
{(\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2})}^{\operatorname{tf}}
. Applying
[
34
, Proposition 1.4]
yields
A
-linear maps

\theta_{i}\colon A\langle U\rangle\to A\langle U\rangle\,,\quad\text{for $n-c+1\leq i\leq n$,}

with the following properties.

(a)

\theta_{i}
is a
\varGamma
-derivation and for each
1\leq j\leq n
one has

\theta_{i}(x_{j})=\begin{cases}1&j=i\\
0&\text{else}\end{cases}

where the
x_{j}
are as in
6.1
.

(b)

d\theta_{i}+\theta_{i}d=0
, where
d
is the differential on
A\langle U\rangle
.

See
[
34
, 1.1]
for the notion of a
\varGamma
-derivation; the convention in
op. cit.
is that such derivations commute with the differential, up to the usual sign. Condition (b) above states that each
\theta_{i}
represents a cycle, of (upper) degree
1
, in the complex
\operatorname{End}_{A}(A\langle U\rangle)
and hence a class in
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})
. Moreover the composition

(6.4)

\theta_{A}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\varepsilon\circ\theta_{n}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{n-c+1}\colon A\langle U\rangle\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}

is a chain-map so represents a class in
\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})
. Evidently

\theta_{A}(x_{n-c+1}\cdots x_{n})=1

This computation is interesting because of the result below. Recall that by construction
6.1
the Koszul complex,
A\langle X\rangle
, is a DG subalgebra of
A\langle U\rangle
, so any map from
A\langle U\rangle
can be restricted to
A\langle X\rangle
.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
\theta\colon A\langle U\rangle\to\mathcal{O}
be any
A
-linear chain-map, of upper degree
c
, with
\theta(A\langle X\rangle)=\mathcal{O}
. The class
[\theta]
in
\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})
generates the free
\mathcal{O}
-module
\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O})
. This conclusion applies, in particular, to the class
[\theta_{A}]
from (
6.4
).

Proof.

Suppose to the contrary that
\theta
does not generate the free
\mathcal{O}
-module
\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O})
. Since the latter has rank one, there must exist an
A
-linear chain-map
\alpha\colon A\langle U\rangle\to\mathcal{O}
of upper degree
c
such that the class of
\theta-\varpi\alpha
is zero in
\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O})
; equivalently that, for some integer
s\geq 1
, the class of
\varpi^{s}(\theta-\varpi\alpha)
is zero in
\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})
. Thus, with
d
denoting the differential on
A\langle U\rangle
, there exists a
A
-linear homotopy
\beta\colon A\langle U\rangle\to\mathcal{O}
, of upper degree
c-1
, such that

\varpi^{s}(\theta-\varpi\alpha)=\beta d\,.

By the hypothesis, there exists an element
a
in
A\langle X\rangle
such that
\theta(a)=1
. Then from the equality above we get

\varpi^{s}=\varpi^{s}\theta(a)=\varpi^{s+1}\alpha(a)+\beta d(a)=\varpi^{s+1}\alpha(a)

where the last equality holds as
d(a)
is in
(\boldsymbol{t})A\langle U\rangle
, hence
\beta d(a)=0
; see (
6.2
). This is a contradiction.
∎

The preceding result can be upgraded to a complete description of the torsion-free quotient of
\operatorname{Ext}^{*}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})
. This is discussed below.

6.6
.

Consider
\operatorname{F}_{A}^{*}(M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\operatorname{F}_{A}^{i}(M)\}_{i\geqslant 0}
for any
A
-complex
M
. The graded
\mathcal{O}
-algebra structure on
\operatorname{Ext}^{*}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})
descends to
\operatorname{F}_{A}^{*}(\mathcal{O})
. Moreover,
\operatorname{F}^{*}_{A}(M)
is a graded module over
\operatorname{F}^{*}_{A}(\mathcal{O})
for any
A
-complex
M
.

By construction,
\operatorname{F}^{*}_{A}(\mathcal{O})
is a subring of
\operatorname{Ext}^{*}_{R}(k(\mathfrak{p}),k(\mathfrak{p}))
where
R\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}A_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}
and
k(\mathfrak{p})
is its residue field. Since
R
is regular,
\operatorname{Ext}^{*}_{R}(k(\mathfrak{p}),k(\mathfrak{p}))
is an exterior algebra on
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{R}(k(\mathfrak{p}),k(\mathfrak{p}))
, and hence strictly graded-commutative; see
[
28
, pp. 110]
. Thus
\operatorname{F}^{*}_{A}(\mathcal{O})
is also graded-commutative and so there is a natural map

(6.7)

\xi_{A}\colon\bigwedge_{\mathcal{O}}\operatorname{F}^{1}_{A}(\mathcal{O})\longrightarrow\operatorname{F}^{*}_{A}(\mathcal{O})

of graded
\mathcal{O}
-algebras, with
\operatorname{F}^{1}_{A}(\mathcal{O})
in degree one. It is easy to check that this map is bijective when
c=0
, for then
\operatorname{F}^{*}_{A}(\mathcal{O})\cong\mathcal{O}
and
\bigwedge_{\mathcal{O}}\operatorname{F}^{1}_{A}(\mathcal{O})\cong\mathcal{O}
, since
\operatorname{F}^{1}_{A}(\mathcal{O})=0
. The result below generalizes this to arbitrary
c
.

Theorem 6.8
.

For any
A
in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
the following statements hold.

(1)

There is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{O}
-modules

\mathcal{O}^{c}\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2},\mathcal{O})\cong\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})\,;

in particular, the map
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})\to\operatorname{F}^{1}_{A}(\mathcal{O})
is bijective.

(2)

The map
\xi_{A}
from (
6.7
) is bijective.

Proof.

Since the
\mathcal{O}
-module
\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2}
has rank
c
, its
\mathcal{O}
-dual is a free module of rank
c
; this explains the first isomorphism in (1). As to the other one, consider the exact sequence of
A
-modules

0\longrightarrow\mathfrak{p}_{A}\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}\longrightarrow 0

Since
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{A}(A,-)=0
, applying
\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(-,\mathcal{O})
yields an exact sequence of
\mathcal{O}
-modules

0\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})\xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(A,\mathcal{O})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(\mathfrak{p}_{A},\mathcal{O})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})\longrightarrow 0\,.

It remains to note that adjunction yields an isomorphism

\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(\mathfrak{p}_{A},\mathcal{O})\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{O}\otimes_{A}\mathfrak{p}_{A},\mathcal{O})\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2},\mathcal{O})\,.

This justifies the isomorphisms in (1).

(2) Set
\Lambda\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\wedge_{\mathcal{O}}\operatorname{F}^{1}_{A}(\mathcal{O})
; thus
\Lambda^{1}=\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})
, by (1). In the notation of
6.3
, the class of the maps

\theta_{i}\colon A\langle U\rangle\longrightarrow A\langle U\rangle\quad\text{for $n-c+1\leq i\leq n$}

form a basis for the
\mathcal{O}
-module
\Lambda^{1}
. These generate the
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
\Lambda
so Lemma
6.5
is equivalent to:
\xi_{A}(\Lambda^{c})=\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O})
. This is the key to the bijectivity of
\xi_{A}
.

Indeed, the map
\xi_{A}
is an isomorphism when localized
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
. Thus
\xi_{A}
is one-to-one, for its source is torsion-free, and its cokernel is
\mathcal{O}
-torsion. If
\xi_{A}
is not surjective, then for some integer
i
there exists an element
\alpha
in
\Lambda^{i}\setminus\varpi\Lambda^{i}
such that
\xi_{A}(\alpha)
is in
\varpi\operatorname{F}^{i}_{A}(\mathcal{O})
. Since
\Lambda
is an exterior algebra on
\Lambda^{1}
, a free
\mathcal{O}
-module of rank
c
, one has
\Lambda^{r-i}\alpha=\Lambda^{c}
so that

\xi_{A}(\Lambda^{c})=\xi_{A}(\Lambda^{c-i}\alpha)\subseteq\xi_{A}(\Lambda^{c-i})\xi_{A}(\alpha)\subseteq\operatorname{F}_{A}^{c-i}(\mathcal{O})\varpi\operatorname{F}_{A}^{i}(\mathcal{O})\subseteq\varpi\operatorname{F}_{A}^{c}(\mathcal{O})\,.

This is a contradiction. Therefore
\xi_{A}
is bijective, as claimed.
∎

It is clear from the construction that the map
\xi_{A}
is functorial in
A
. Namely, given a map
\varphi\colon A\to B
in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}
, the natural map of
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
\operatorname{Ext}^{*}_{B}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})\to\operatorname{Ext}^{*}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})
induces the commutative diagram

(6.9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigwedge_{\mathcal{O}}\operatorname{F}^{1}_{B}(\mathcal{O})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigwedge_{\mathcal{O}}\operatorname{F}^{1}_{A}(\mathcal{O})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{*}_{B}(\mathcal{O})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{*}_{A}(\mathcal{O})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wedge_{\mathcal{O}}\operatorname{F}^{1}_{\varphi}(\mathcal{O})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{*}_{\varphi}(\mathcal{O})}

where the isomorphisms are by Theorem
6.8
.

Proposition 6.10
.

Let
\varphi\colon A\to B
be a surjective map in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
. If
\varphi
is an isomorphism at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
, then induced map

\operatorname{F}^{*}_{\varphi}(\mathcal{O})\colon\operatorname{F}^{*}_{B}(\mathcal{O})\to\operatorname{F}^{*}_{A}(\mathcal{O})

of
\mathcal{O}
-algebras is an isomorphism. In particular,
\operatorname{F}^{c}_{\varphi}(\mathcal{O})
is bijective.

Proof.

Set
I\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{Ker}(\varphi)
. Since
\varphi
is an isomorphism at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
, one has
I_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}=0
. The diagram
A\to B\to\mathcal{O}
induces an exact sequence

I/\mathfrak{p}_{A}I\longrightarrow\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{p}_{B}/\mathfrak{p}_{B}^{2}\longrightarrow 0

of
\mathcal{O}
-modules. Since
I_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}=0
the
\mathcal{O}
-module
I/\mathfrak{p}_{A}I
is torsion, so the exact sequence above induces an isomorphism

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{p}_{B}/\mathfrak{p}_{B}^{2},\mathcal{O})\xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2},\mathcal{O})\,.

Thus the map
\operatorname{F}^{1}_{\varphi}(\mathcal{O})
is bijective; see Theorem
6.8
(1). It remains to recall the commutative diagram (
6.9
).
∎

Complete intersections

The remainder of the discussion in this section is on complete intersection rings, leading to the statement that the Wiles defect of such rings is zero; see Theorem
9.5
. The result below, which is already contained in the literature, leads us to it.

Proposition 6.11
.

Let
A
be in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}
and
A\langle X,Y\rangle
the Tate construction from
6.1
. The following conditions are equivalent:

(a)

The local ring
A
is complete intersection;

(b)

The map
A\langle X,Y\rangle\to\mathcal{O}
is a quasi-isomorphism;

(c)

The
\mathcal{O}
-module
\operatorname{H}_{1}(A\langle X\rangle)
is free and the natural map is bijective:

\bigwedge_{\mathcal{O}}(\operatorname{H}_{1}(A\langle X\rangle))\xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }\operatorname{H}_{*}(A\langle X\rangle)

Proof.

Let
P\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{O}[\![{t_{1},\dots,t_{n}}]\!]
and
A\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}P/(\boldsymbol{f})
, as in
5.1
. The kernel of the surjection
\lambda_{P}\colon P\to\mathcal{O}
is generated by the regular sequence
\boldsymbol{t}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}t_{1},\dots,t_{m}
.

(a)
\Rightarrow
(b): Since
A
is complete intersection,
\boldsymbol{f}
is a regular sequence, contained in the ideal
(\boldsymbol{t})
. Thus
[
52
, Theorem 4]
applies, and yields the stated result.

(b)
\Leftrightarrow
(c): This is a special case of
[
6
, Theorem 1]
; see also
[
34
, Theorem 2.3]
.

(c)
\Leftrightarrow
(a): Condition (c) says precisely that the map
\lambda_{A}\colon A\to\mathcal{O}
is a quasi-complete intersection, in the sense of
[
2
, 1.1]
. Since
\mathcal{O}
is regular, hence a complete intersection, the desired equivalence is contained in
[
2
, Proposition 7.7]
.
∎

6.12
.

Let
A
in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
be a complete intersection. We can assume
A\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}P/(\boldsymbol{f})
, where
\boldsymbol{f}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}f_{1},\dots,f_{m}
is a regular sequence, and of the form given in (
5.4
); see Theorem
5.6
. By Proposition
6.11
, the Tate construction
A\langle X,Y\rangle
is the acyclic closure of
\mathcal{O}
over
A
. Thus there is an isomorphism

\operatorname{Ext}^{*}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})\cong\operatorname{H}^{*}(\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(A\langle X,Y\rangle,\mathcal{O}))\,.

Writing
\mathcal{O}\langle X,Y\rangle
for
\mathcal{O}\otimes_{A}A\langle X,Y\rangle
, standard adjunction yields an isomorphism

\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(A\langle X,Y\rangle,\mathcal{O})\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{O}\langle X,Y\rangle,\mathcal{O})

Given that the
f_{i}
are as in (
5.4
), the differential
d
on
\mathcal{O}\langle X,Y\rangle
satisfies
d(X)=0
and
d(y_{i})=\varpi^{d_{i}}x_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq m=n-c
. Keeping in mind that the dual of a free algebra with divided powers is a symmetric algebra, one gets an isomorphism of complexes of
\mathcal{O}
-modules

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{O}\langle X,Y\rangle,\mathcal{O})\cong\mathcal{O}[\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{n},\chi_{1}\,\dots,\chi_{m}\mid d(\eta_{i})=\varpi^{d_{i}}\chi_{i}\,,d(\chi_{j})=0]

where each
\eta_{i}
is exterior variable of (upper) degree one, and each
\chi_{j}
is a polynomial variable of degree two. This yields an isomorphism of
\mathcal{O}
-modules

\operatorname{Ext}^{*}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})\cong\frac{\mathcal{O}[\eta_{m+1},\dots,\eta_{n},\chi_{1}\,\dots,\chi_{m}]}{(\varpi^{d_{1}}\chi_{1},\dots,\varpi^{d_{m}}\chi_{m})}

Recall that
d_{i}=\infty
for
c+1\leq i\leq n
. Thus one gets an isomorphism of
\mathcal{O}
-modules

\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})\cong\mathcal{O}\eta_{m+1}\cdots\eta_{n}\oplus(\text{torsion})

where the torsion part depends on
c
and
m
. Thus
\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O})
is generated by the residue class of
\eta_{m+1}\cdots\eta_{n}
; confer the construction of the class
\theta
in
6.3
and Lemma
6.5
.

Since
A
and
\mathcal{O}
are both complete intersections, the map
\lambda_{A}\colon A\to\mathcal{O}
is a quasi-complete intersection map, in the sense of
[
2
]
. Applying Theorem 2.5(4) from
op. cit.
yields

\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{A}(\mathcal{O},A)=\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}&\text{when $i=c$}\\
0&\text{else}.\end{cases}

In fact, one can follow the proof of
[
2
, Theorem 2.5(4)]
and deduce that

\Psi_{A}\cong\mathcal{O}/(\varpi^{d_{1}+\cdots+d_{m}})

Given this isomorphism and the description of
\Phi_{A}
in (
5.5
), it follows that when
A
in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}
is a complete intersection ring,
\delta_{A}(A)=0
. We omit details, for later on we present another proof that goes by a reduction to the case
c=0
; see Theorem
9.5
, which also contains a converse.

7.
Invariance of domain

In this section we establish an “invariance of domains” property for congruence modules; see Theorem
7.4
.
The statement takes some preparation.

7.1
.

Let
\varphi\colon A\to B
be a surjective map in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
and
M
a finitely generated
B
-module. For each integer
i
, the natural transformation
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{B}(\mathcal{O},-)\to\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{A}(\mathcal{O},-)
induces the map:

\operatorname{F}^{i}_{\varphi}(M)\colon\operatorname{F}^{i}_{B}(M)\longrightarrow\operatorname{F}^{i}_{A}(M)\,.

Noting that
\mathfrak{p}_{A}M=\mathfrak{p}_{B}M
, the naturality of the transformation
\operatorname{F}^{i}_{\varphi}(-)
gives a commutative diagram of
\mathcal{O}
-modules

(7.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{i}_{B}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{i}_{A}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{i}_{B}(M/\mathfrak{p}_{B}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{i}_{A}(M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{i}_{\varphi}(M)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{i}_{\varphi}(M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M)}

For
i=c
, the diagram above induces a map on the cokernels of the vertical maps:

(7.3)

\Psi_{\varphi}(M)\colon\Psi_{B}(M)\longrightarrow\Psi_{A}(M)\,.

This result below may be viewed as a statement about invariance of domains for congruence modules. The case
c=0
is covered by
[
12
, Lemma 3.4]
.

Theorem 7.4
.

Let
\varphi\colon A\to B
be a surjective map of local rings in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
. For any finitely generated
B
-module
M
with
0pt_{B}M\geq c
, the map of
\mathcal{O}
-modules in (
7.3
) is bijective, and hence

\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Psi_{A}(M)=\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Psi_{B}(M)\,.

Thus
\delta_{A}(M)\geq\delta_{B}(M)
with equality if and only if
\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Phi_{A}=\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Phi_{B}
.

Proof.

It suffices to verify that (
7.3
) is bijective; the claim about defects is a simple consequence. The map
\varphi
induces the surjective map
\varphi_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}\colon A_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}\to B_{\mathfrak{p}_{B}}
of local rings. Its source and target are regular local rings of dimension
c
so it is an isomorphism. This remark will be used in the argument below.

Consider the commutative diagram (
7.2
). We prove that the horizontal maps are isomorphisms for
i=c
; this implies the desired result.

First we verify that the induced map
\operatorname{F}^{c}_{\varphi}(M)\colon\operatorname{F}^{c}_{B}(M)\to\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(M)
is bijective. Indeed since
\varphi_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}
is an isomorphism, it is easy to see by localizing at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
that the kernel of the map

\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{B}(\mathcal{O},M)\to\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)

is
\mathcal{O}
-torsion, and hence the map
\operatorname{F}^{c}_{\varphi}(M)
is one-to-one. This requires no hypotheses on
M
. The crucial point is that since
0pt_{B}M\geq c
the map above is surjective, and hence so is
\operatorname{F}^{c}_{\varphi}(M)
. The argument goes as follows: Since
A
-acts on
M
through
B
, there is an isomorphism of complexes

\operatorname{RHom}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)\cong\operatorname{RHom}_{B}(B\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{A}\mathcal{O},M)\,,

and the map
\operatorname{RHom}_{B}(\mathcal{O},M)\to\operatorname{RHom}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)
is induced by the natural morphism
B\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{A}\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{O}
in
\mathsf{D}(B)
. Consider the exact triangle in
\mathsf{D}(B)
that it generates:

J\longrightarrow B\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{A}\mathcal{O}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}\longrightarrow J[1]

For the desired statement it suffices that
\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{B}(J,M)=0
. Evidently the map
\operatorname{H}_{i}(B\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{A}\mathcal{O})\to\operatorname{H}_{i}(\mathcal{O})
is surjective for
i\geq 1
and bijective for
i\leq 0
; moreover, it is bijective for all
i
once we localize at
\mathfrak{p}_{B}
. It follows that
\operatorname{H}_{i}(J)
is
0
for
i\leq 0
and
\mathcal{O}
-torsion for all
i
; equivalently,
\mathfrak{m}_{B}
-power torsion, where
\mathfrak{m}_{B}
denotes the maximal ideal of
B
, for the
B
action on
\operatorname{H}_{i}(J)
factors through
\lambda_{B}
. Since
0pt_{B}M\geq c
, any
\mathfrak{m}_{B}
-power torsion
B
-module
W
satisfies

\operatorname{Ext}_{B}^{i}(W,M)=0\quad\text{for $i\leq c-1$.}

Given that
\operatorname{H}_{i}(J)=0
for
i\leq 0
and
\mathfrak{m}_{B}
-power torsion for all
i
, it follows, for example by using a standard spectral sequence argument, that

\operatorname{Ext}_{B}^{i}(J,M)=0\quad\text{for $i\leq c$.}

This is as desired.

Next we verify that the following map is bijective:

\operatorname{F}^{c}_{B}(M/\mathfrak{p}_{B}M)\to\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M)\,.

This part of the proof also does not require that
0pt_{B}M\geq c
. Given the isomorphism (
2.4
) it is enough to check that
\operatorname{F}^{c}_{B}(\mathcal{O})\to\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O})
is bijective. This is contained in Proposition
6.10
.
∎

The following application of Theorem
7.4
will be used often in the sequel.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
A
be in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
and
M
a finitely generated
A
-module supported at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
and satisfying
\operatorname{grade}(\mathfrak{p}_{A},M)\geq c
. Let
A^{\prime}
be the image of the natural map
A\to\operatorname{End}_{A}(M)
.
The following statements hold.

(1)

A^{\prime}
is in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
and the surjective map
A\to A^{\prime}
is an isomorphism at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
;

(2)

\mathrm{ass}_{A}A^{\prime}\subseteq\mathrm{ass}_{A}M
;

(3)

If
f\in A
is not a zero-divisor on
M
, it is also not a zero-divisor on
A^{\prime}
.

(4)

There are equalities

\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Psi_{A}(M)=\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Psi_{A^{\prime}}(M)\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Phi_{A}\geq\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Phi_{A^{\prime}}\,.

Hence
\delta_{A}(M)\geq\delta_{A^{\prime}}(M)
, with equality if and only if
\Phi_{A}\cong\Phi_{A^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

The hypothesis on
M
implies that
0pt_{A}M\geq c
and also that
M
is free at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
; see Lemma
3.1
. These observations will be used below.

(1) Since
M
is faithful as an
A^{\prime}
-module and free at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
, it follows that the map
A\to A^{\prime}
is an isomorphism at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
.

(2) Since
A^{\prime}
is an
A
-submodule of
\operatorname{End}_{A}(M)
, the inclusion below holds:

\mathrm{ass}_{A}A^{\prime}\subseteq\mathrm{ass}_{A}\operatorname{End}_{A}(M)=\mathrm{ass}_{A}M\,.

The equality is a direct computation; solve
[
13
, Exercise 1.2.27]
.

(3) The zero-divisors of a module over a noetherian ring are the union of its associated primes, so (3) is a consequence of (2).

(4) The action of
A
on
M
factors through
A^{\prime}
so
0pt_{A^{\prime}}M=0pt_{A}M\geq c
. Hence, given (1), it remains to apply Theorem
7.4
to the map
A\to A^{\prime}
.
∎

8.
Deformations

The main result of this section, Theorem
8.2
, is that the defect of a module does not change under deformations. Throughout we fix the following notation.

8.1
.

Fix
A
in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
, for some
c\geq 1
, and
M
a finitely generated
A
-module supported at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
, and with
\operatorname{grade}(\mathfrak{p}_{A},M)=c
; this is the maximum value possible, by Lemma
3.1
. Let
f
in
\mathfrak{p}_{A}\setminus\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{(2)}
be such that it is not a zero-divisor on
M
. Set

B\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}A/fA\qquad\text{and}\qquad N\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}M/fM\,,

where
N
is viewed as a
B
-module.

Theorem 8.2
.

In the context of
8.1
, the ring
B
is in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c-1)
, and

\delta_{A}(M)=\delta_{B}(N)\,.

Moreover
N
is supported at
\mathfrak{p}_{B}
and
\operatorname{grade}(\mathfrak{p}_{B},N)=\operatorname{grade}(\mathfrak{p}_{A},M)-1
.

This result often permits one to reduce questions about defects to the case when
c=0
, which has been studied earlier in the literature. Here is one application; see also Section
9
.

Corollary 8.3
.

Let
A
be a local ring in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
. If
M
is a finitely generated
A
-module with
\operatorname{grade}(\mathfrak{p}_{A},M)\geq c
, then
\delta_{A}(M)\geq 0
.

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{g}
be an
M
-regular sequence of length
c
provided by Lemma
3.5
. Set
B\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}A/\boldsymbol{g}A
and
N\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}M/\boldsymbol{g}M
. Then a repeated application of Theorem
8.2
yields
\delta_{A}(M)=\delta_{B}(N)
. After replacing
A
and
M
by
B
and
N
, respectively, one can assume
c=0
. Then given the defect formula Lemma
3.7
, it suffices to verify that
\delta_{A}(A)\geq 0
, that is to say that

\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Phi_{A}\geq\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Psi_{A}\,.

Since
c=0
, one has
\Phi_{A}=\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2}
, and so the inequality above is exactly the one proved by Wiles’; see
[
56
, Appendix, Proposition 1]
and
[
22
, #(5.2.3)]
.
∎

8.4
.

Theorem
8.2
also permits one to reconcile our notion of Wiles defect with the one introduced in
[
7
]
when
M=A
is a Cohen–Macaulay ring. Indeed, both invariants remain unchanged when we go modulo a suitable regular sequence; for the former, this is by Theorem
8.2
, and for the latter, this is essentially by definition
[
7
, §3.4]
. So it suffices to compare them when
c=0
, in which case they are defined in the same way.

The proof of Theorem
8.2
makes repeated appeal to Theorem
7.4
, and some observations of independent interest recorded below.

8.5
.

Let
A
be a local ring in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}
, and consider the natural pairing

\langle-,-\rangle\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2},\mathcal{O})\times{(\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2})}^{\operatorname{tf}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}\,.

Given an element
f\in\mathfrak{p}_{A}
, let
[f]
denote its residue class in
{(\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2})}^{\operatorname{tf}}
, and consider the induced map

\langle-,[f]\rangle\colon{(\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2})}^{\operatorname{tf}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}\,.

Let
O(f)
denote its image; this is called the order ideal of
[f]
. It is the subset of
\mathcal{O}
consisting of elements
\alpha(f)
, as
\alpha
ranges over
\mathcal{O}
-linear maps
\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2}\to\mathcal{O}
. Set

\nu_{A}(f)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{O}/O(f))\,.

Let
f
be as in
8.1
. The maps
A\to B\to\mathcal{O}
give rise to an exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}\longrightarrow\frac{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}{\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2}}\longrightarrow\frac{\mathfrak{p}_{B}}{\mathfrak{p}_{B}^{2}}\longrightarrow 0\,,

of
\mathcal{O}
-modules, where the map on the left sends
1\in\mathcal{O}
to the class of
f
in
\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2}
; it is one-to-one because it is so when it is localized at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
. This uses the hypothesis that
f
is not in
\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{(2)}
. Applying
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}}(-,\mathcal{O})
gives an exact sequence

(8.6)

0\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{B}(\mathfrak{p}_{B}/\mathfrak{p}_{B}^{2},\mathcal{O})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2},\mathcal{O})\xrightarrow{\ \eth^{1}\ }\mathcal{O}

where the map on the right is evaluation on the class of
f
. In particular, the image of
\eth^{1}
is
O(f)
, so

\operatorname{length}\operatorname{Coker}(\eth^{1})=\nu_{A}(f)\,.

The relevance of this discussion is manifest from the result below.

Lemma 8.7
.

In the set-up of
8.1
, the ring
B
is in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c-1)
and

\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Phi_{B}-\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Phi_{A}=\nu_{A}(f)\,.

Proof.

Lemma
3.4
yields that
B
is in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c-1)
. By construction, the exact sequence (
8.6
) continues as the exact sequence

\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2},\mathcal{O})\xrightarrow{\ \eth^{1}\ }\mathcal{O}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{p}_{B}/\mathfrak{p}_{B}^{2},\mathcal{O})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2},\mathcal{O})\longrightarrow 0\,.

It is easy to check that the length of
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{p}_{B}/\mathfrak{p}_{B}^{2},\mathcal{O})
equals that of
\Phi_{B}
, and similarly for
A
. Therefore the exact sequence above yields

\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Phi_{B}-\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Phi_{A}=\operatorname{length}\operatorname{Coker}(\eth^{1})=\nu_{A}(f)\,.

This is the desired equality.
∎

We need one more observation regarding these order ideals.

Lemma 8.8
.

With
A
and
f
as in
8.1
, let
\pi\colon A\to A^{\prime}
be a surjective map in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
that is an isomorphism at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
.
Then
\pi(f)
is in
\mathfrak{p}_{A^{\prime}}\setminus\mathfrak{p}_{A^{\prime}}^{(2)}
, and
\nu_{A^{\prime}}(\pi(f))=\nu_{A}(f)
.

Proof.

Since
\pi
is surjective and an isomorphism at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
, one has

\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{(2)}=\pi^{-1}(\mathfrak{p}_{A^{\prime}}^{(2)})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2}A_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}\cong\mathfrak{p}_{A^{\prime}}^{2}A^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{\prime}}\,.

Thus
f
not in
\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{(2)}
implies that
\pi(f)
is not in
\mathfrak{p}_{A^{\prime}}^{(2)}
. The equality
\nu_{A^{\prime}}(\pi(f))=\nu_{A}(f)
is immediate from the definition of
\nu(-)
, given that
\pi
induces an isomorphism

{(\mathfrak{p}_{A}/\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{2})}^{\operatorname{tf}}\cong{(\mathfrak{p}_{A^{\prime}}/\mathfrak{p}_{A^{\prime}}^{2})}^{\operatorname{tf}}\,.

See (
5.9
).
∎

Now we present a proof of Theorem
8.2
. See
[
32
, Theorem 2.28]
for a different perspective.

Proof of Theorem
8.2
.

The hypothesis is that
A
is in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
, the
A
-module
M
has depth at least
c
, and
f\in\mathfrak{p}_{A}\setminus\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{(2)}
is not a zero-divisor on
M
. We have to verify

\delta_{A}(M)=\delta_{B}(N)\quad\text{for $B\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}A/fA$ and $N\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}M/fM$.}

One has
0pt_{A}(\mathfrak{p}_{A},M)=c
and
f\in\mathfrak{p}_{A}
is not a zero-divisor on
M
. Thus
0pt_{B}(\mathfrak{p}_{B},N)=c-1
. Since
f
is in
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
, the surjection
M\to N
induces an isomorphism
M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M\cong N/\mathfrak{p}_{B}N
, so there is an equality

(8.9)

\operatorname{rank}_{A_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}}(M_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}})=\operatorname{rank}_{B_{\mathfrak{p}_{B}}}(N_{\mathfrak{p}_{B}})\,.

We reduce to the case where
f
is not a zero-divisor also on
A
, as follows: Let
A^{\prime}
be the image of
A
in
\operatorname{End}_{A}(M)
and set
B^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}A^{\prime}/fA^{\prime}
. Then
A^{\prime}
is in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
and the map
A\to A^{\prime}
is an isomorphism at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
, by Lemma
7.5
. Thus
image of
f
in
A^{\prime}
is not contained in
\mathfrak{p}_{A^{\prime}}^{(2)}
and
\nu_{A^{\prime}}(f)=\nu_{A}(f)
, by Lemma
8.8
.
Applying Lemma
8.7
to
A
and
A^{\prime}
we deduce that
B
and
B^{\prime}
are in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}{(c-1)}
, and

\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Phi_{B}-\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Phi_{A}=\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Phi_{B^{\prime}}-\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Phi_{A^{\prime}}\,.

Moreover
\operatorname{grade}(\mathfrak{p}_{A^{\prime}},M)=\operatorname{grade}(\mathfrak{p}_{A},M)\geq c
, since the
A
action on
M
factors through
A^{\prime}
, and similarly
\operatorname{grade}(\mathfrak{p}_{B^{\prime}},N)\geq c-1
, so Theorem
7.4
yields equalities

\displaystyle\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Psi_{A^{\prime}}(M)=\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Psi_{A}(M)

\displaystyle\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Psi_{B^{\prime}}(N)=\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Psi_{B}(N)\,.

The displayed equalities above yield

\delta_{A}(M)-\delta_{B}(N)=\delta_{A^{\prime}}(M)-\delta_{B^{\prime}}(N)

It thus suffices to verify the desired result for the
A^{\prime}
-module
M
, so replacing
A
and
A^{\prime}
, and so
B
by
B^{\prime}
, one can assume
f
is not a zero-divisor also on
A
, as claimed; see Lemma
7.5
.

The next step in the proof is a reduction to the case
M=A
.
The assertion below concerns the map
\eta_{M}
from (
3.6
).

Claim
.

There is an isomorphism
\operatorname{Coker}(\eta_{N})\cong\operatorname{Coker}(\eta_{M})
of
\mathcal{O}
-modules.

Indeed, since
f
is not a zero-divisor on
A
, the free resolution of the
A
-module
B
is
0\to A\xrightarrow{f}A\to 0
. In particular, it is finite free, so for any
A
-complex
X
there is a natural isomorphism

\operatorname{RHom}_{A}(B,X)\cong\operatorname{RHom}_{A}(B,A)\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{A}X\cong(B\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{A}X)[-1]

in
\mathsf{D}(B)
. This gives rise to isomorphisms

\operatorname{RHom}_{A}(\mathcal{O},X)\cong\operatorname{RHom}_{B}(\mathcal{O},\operatorname{RHom}_{A}(B,X))\cong\operatorname{RHom}_{B}(\mathcal{O},(B\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{A}X)[-1])\,.

Thus, passing to cohomology and applying
{(-)}^{\operatorname{tf}}
yields an isomorphism

\operatorname{F}^{i}_{A}(X)\cong\operatorname{F}_{B}^{i-1}(B\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{A}X)

for each integer
i
, and natural in
X
. Moreover, since
f
is not a zero-divisor on
M
, the natural map

B\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{A}M\to\operatorname{H}_{0}(B\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{A}M)\cong M/fM=N

is a quasi-isomorphism. Summing up, one gets a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(A)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}{(M/\mathfrak{p}_{A}M)}^{\operatorname{tf}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{c-1}_{B}(B)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}{(N/\mathfrak{p}_{B}N)}^{\operatorname{tf}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{c-1}_{B}(N)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

of
\mathcal{O}
-modules. This justifies the claim.

Given the preceding claim, (
8.9
), and the exact sequence Lemma
3.7
, it suffices to establish the desired equality for
M=A
, namely that
\delta_{A}(A)=\delta_{B}(B)
. In view of Lemma
8.7
, this is equivalent to

(8.10)

\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Psi_{B}-\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Psi_{A}=\nu_{A}(f)\,.

To that end, consider the commutative square

(8.11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{c-1}_{B}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{c-1}_{B}(B\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{A}\mathcal{O})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{F}^{c-1}_{B}(\mathcal{O})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

The maps in the lower row are induced by canonical maps
B\to B\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{A}\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{O}
whose composition is
\lambda_{B}
. The key point is this:

Claim
.

The map
\operatorname{F}^{c-1}_{B}(B\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{A}\mathcal{O})\to\operatorname{F}^{c-1}_{B}(\mathcal{O})
is one-to-one and its cokernel has length equal to
\nu_{A}(f)
.

Given this claim and the diagram (
8.11
) one gets inclusions

\operatorname{F}^{c-1}_{B}(B)\hookrightarrow\operatorname{F}^{c-1}_{B}(B\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{A}\mathcal{O})\hookrightarrow\operatorname{F}^{c-1}_{B}(\mathcal{O})

These and the isomorphisms in (
8.11
) yield (
8.10
), as desired.

To verify the claim we consider the canonical exact triangle

\mathcal{O}[1]\longrightarrow B\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{A}\mathcal{O}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}[2]

in
\mathsf{D}(B)
. Applying
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{D}(B)}(\mathcal{O},(-)[{c-1}])
yields an exact sequence of
\mathcal{O}
-modules

\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{B}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{c-1}_{B}(\mathcal{O},B\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{A}\mathcal{O})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{c-1}_{B}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})\xrightarrow{\ \chi\ }\operatorname{Ext}^{c+1}_{B}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})\longrightarrow

The first part of the claim follows because the
\mathcal{O}
-module
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{B}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})
is torsion for all
i\geq c
; see Lemma
2.6
and
2.1
. As an aside we note that
\chi
is the cohomology operator of the map
A\to B
, discovered by Eisenbud and Gulliksen; see
[
5
, §9.1]
.

It remains to verify the second part of the claim. In view of the commutative diagram
8.11
, this is tantamount to the statement that the cokernel of the map
\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O})\to\operatorname{F}^{c-1}_{B}(\mathcal{O})
has length
\nu_{A}(f)
. This follows from a certain equivariance property of the map. Namely, the connecting map

\operatorname{Ext}^{*}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{*-1}_{B}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})

is
\operatorname{Ext}^{*}_{B}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})
-linear, where the source is viewed as an
\operatorname{Ext}^{*}_{B}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})
-module via the natural map
\operatorname{Ext}^{*}_{B}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})\to\operatorname{Ext}^{*}_{A}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O})
. Thus the induced map

\eth^{*}\colon\operatorname{F}^{*}_{A}(\mathcal{O})\longrightarrow\operatorname{F}^{*-1}_{B}(\mathcal{O})

is
\operatorname{F}^{*}_{B}(\mathcal{O})
-linear. The map
\eth^{1}
is precisely the one in (
8.6
). In view of Theorem
6.8
, the exact sequence (
8.6
) implies that the image of
\operatorname{F}^{1}_{B}(\mathcal{O})
in
\operatorname{F}^{1}_{A}(\mathcal{O})
is a nonzero direct summand, and hence also that
\operatorname{F}^{c-1}_{B}(\mathcal{O})\cdot\operatorname{F}^{1}_{A}(\mathcal{O})=\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O})
. Since the image of
\eth^{1}
is the order-ideal of
f
, the equivariance property of
\eth^{*}
implies

\eth^{c}(\operatorname{F}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O}))=\eth^{c}(\operatorname{F}^{c-1}_{B}(\mathcal{O})\operatorname{F}^{1}_{A}(\mathcal{O}))=\operatorname{F}^{c-1}_{B}(\mathcal{O})\eth^{1}(\operatorname{F}^{1}_{A}(\mathcal{O}))=\varpi^{\nu_{A}(f)}\operatorname{F}^{c-1}_{B}(\mathcal{O})

Thus the cokernel of
\eth^{c}
has length
\nu_{A}(f)
, as claimed.

This completes the proof of the claim and hence of the theorem.
∎

9.
Criteria for freeness

In this section we establish numerical criteria, in terms of congruence modules and cotangent modules, for detecting when a module has a free summand, and also for detecting the complete intersection local rings in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}
.

Gorenstein rings

A noetherian local ring
R
is
Gorenstein
if
R
, viewed as a module over itself, has finite injective dimension.
Over such a ring
R
, a finitely generated
R
-module
M
is maximal Cohen–Macaulay if and only if

(9.1)

\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{R}(M,R)=0\quad\text{for all $i\geq 1$.}

This follows from (
4.2
), keeping in mind that
\omega_{R}\cong R[\dim R]
in
\mathsf{D}(R)
; see also
[
13
, Theorem 3.3.10]
. The result below is Theorem
B
from the Introduction. In the statement,
e_{A}(M)
denotes the multiplicity of an
A
-module
M
; see
[
13
, §4.6]
.

Theorem 9.2
.

Let
A
be a Gorenstein local ring in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}
and
M
a maximal Cohen–Macaulay
A
-module with
\mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{rank}_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}(M_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}})\neq 0
. Then
\delta_{A}(M)=\mu\cdot\delta_{A}(A)
holds if and only if

M\cong A^{\mu}\oplus W\qquad\text{where $W_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}=0$.}

When this holds and
e_{A}(M)\leq\mu\cdot e(A)
, then
M
is free.

A key step in the proof of Theorem
9.2
is a criterion for detecting free summands in modules over general Gorenstein local rings, and not special to the category
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}
. This is explained below.

Lemma 9.3
.

Let
R
be a Gorenstein local ring,
M\in\operatorname{mod}R
a maximal Cohen–Macaulay module, and
x\in R
not a zero-divisor on
R
and on
M
. If the
R/xR
-module
M/xM
has a free summand of rank
\mu
, then so does the
R
-module
M
.

Proof.

It suffices to verify that if
R^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}R/xR
is a direct summand of
M^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}M/xM
, then
R
is a direct summand of
M
. The latter condition is equivalent to the condition that the trace ideal of
M
is
R
; that is to say, the natural map is surjective:

\tau_{M}(R)\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(M,R)\otimes_{R}M\longrightarrow R\,,\quad\text{given by $f\otimes m\to f(m)$.}

See, for instance,
[
43
, Proposition 2.8(iii)]
. By Nakayama’s Lemma, it suffices to prove that
\tau_{M}(R)
is surjective after applying
-\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}
; equivalently, the horizontal map in the diagram below is surjective:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(M,R)\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})\otimes_{R^{\prime}}M^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{R^{\prime}}(M^{\prime},R^{\prime})\otimes_{R^{\prime}}M^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{M^{\prime}}(R^{\prime})}

The vertical map is induced by the map

\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(M,R)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(M,R^{\prime})\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{R^{\prime}}(M^{\prime},R^{\prime})\,,

where the first map is induced by
R\to R^{\prime}
, and the isomorphism is standard adjunction. It is a simple exercise to check that the diagram above is commutative. Moreover,
\tau_{M^{\prime}}(R^{\prime})
is surjective because
R^{\prime}
is a direct summand of
M^{\prime}
. Thus, it suffices to verify that the vertical map is surjective. Since
M
is maximal Cohen–Macaulay,
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{R}(M,R)=0
by (
9.1
), so applying
\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(M,-)
to the exact sequence

0\longrightarrow R\xrightarrow{\ x\ }R\longrightarrow R^{\prime}\longrightarrow 0

yields the desired result.
∎

Here is a general result on detecting free summands. The hypothesis on the Ext modules is equivalent to the vanishing of the Tate cohomology module of the pair
(\mathcal{O},M)
, in degree
c
; see Proposition
4.9
and also
[
14
, Corollary 6.3.4]
.

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
R
be a Gorenstein local ring and
M
a maximal Cohen–Macaulay
R
-module. Let
\lambda\colon R\to\mathcal{O}
be surjective map, set
\mathfrak{p}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{Ker}(\lambda)
and
c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}0pt\mathfrak{p}
. If the induced map
\widetilde{\eta}_{M}\colon\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{R}(\mathcal{O},R)\otimes_{R}M\to\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{R}(\mathcal{O},M)
is surjective and
M_{\mathfrak{p}}
is nonzero, then
M
has a free
R
-summand.

Proof.

The argument is by reduction to Krull dimension zero. Choose an element
x
in
R
such that
\lambda(x)=\varpi
, the uniformizer for
\mathcal{O}
, and is not zero-divisor on
R
or on
M
. Set
S\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}R/xR
and
N\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}M/xM
. For any
R
-module, say
U
, on which
x
is not a zero-divisor, the surjection
U\to U/xU
induces, for each integer
n
, a map

\operatorname{Ext}^{n}_{R}(\mathcal{O},U)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{n}_{R}(\mathcal{O},U/xU)\cong\operatorname{Ext}^{n}_{S}(k,U/xU)

natural in
U
. This isomorphism is by standard adjunction. Specializing
U
to
R
and to
M
gives a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{R}(\mathcal{O},R)\otimes_{R}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{R}(\mathcal{O},M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{S}(k,S)\otimes_{S}N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{S}(k,N)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\eta}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\eta}_{N}}

The vertical map on the right is nonzero, as can be seen by considering the long exact sequence induced by
\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(\mathcal{O},-)
to the exact sequence

0\longrightarrow M\xrightarrow{\ x\ }M\longrightarrow N\longrightarrow 0

and Nakayama’s Lemma. This is where we need the hypothesis that
M_{\mathfrak{p}}\neq 0
, for it implies
\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{R}(\mathcal{O},M)\neq 0
; localize at
\mathfrak{p}
. Thus we conclude that the map
\widetilde{\eta}_{N}
in the diagram above is nonzero. It thus suffices to prove following claim, for then
M
has a free
R
-summand, by Lemma
9.3
.

Claim
.

When
\widetilde{\eta}_{N}
is nonzero, the
S
-module
N
has a free summand.

To verify the claim, choose a sequence
\boldsymbol{x}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}x_{1},\dots,x_{r}
in
\mathfrak{m}_{S}
that is regular on
S
and on
N
; this is possible as both have maximal depth. Set
S^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}S/{\boldsymbol{x}}S
and
N^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}N/{\boldsymbol{x}}N
. By
[
13
, Lemma 3.1.16]
one gets the isomorphisms in the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{S}(k,S)\otimes_{S}N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{S}(k,N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Soc}(S^{\prime})\otimes_{S^{\prime}}N^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}^{0}_{S^{\prime}}(k,S^{\prime})\otimes_{S^{\prime}}N^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}^{0}_{S^{\prime}}(k,N^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Soc}(N^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\eta}_{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\eta}_{N^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

The diagram is commutative because of the naturality of the maps. Since
\widetilde{\eta}_{N}
is nonzero so is
\widetilde{\eta}_{N^{\prime}}
, that is to say,
\mathrm{Soc}(S^{\prime})\cdot N^{\prime}\neq 0
. We claim that this property implies
N^{\prime}
has a free
S^{\prime}
-summand, so then
N
has a free
S
-summand, by Lemma
9.3
, which is as desired. Here is one way to verify the claim.

Choose an element
a\in N^{\prime}
such that
\mathrm{Soc}(S^{\prime})a\neq 0
and consider the
S^{\prime}
-linear map
\alpha\colon S^{\prime}\to N^{\prime}
defined by
1\mapsto a
. Since
S^{\prime}
is a Gorenstein ring of Krull dimension zero,
\mathrm{Soc}(S^{\prime})
is the smallest nonzero ideal in
S^{\prime}
. Thus the choice of
a
implies that
\alpha
is injective. It remains to observe that
S^{\prime}
being a Gorenstein ring of Krull dimension zero, it is injective as a module over itself, so
\alpha
is split-injective.
∎

We can now present a proof of Theorem
9.2
. Instead of the argument given below, one could also use Theorem
8.2
to reduce to the case
c=0
, as in the proof of Corollary
8.3
; then the result is implicit in the proof of
[
25
, Theorem 2.4]
. The latter statement is contained in Theorem
9.6
further below.

Proof of Theorem
9.2
.

Suppose
A
is in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
. Since
M
is maximal Cohen–Macaulay,
0pt_{A}M\geq c+1
, so it is free at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
and the
\mathcal{O}
-module
\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)
is torsion-free, by Lemma
3.2
