. Thus if
\delta_{A}(M)=\mu\cdot\delta_{A}(A)
, then Lemma
3.7
yields that the map

\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},A)\otimes_{A}M\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{c}_{A}(\mathcal{O},M)

is surjective. Then a repeated application of Lemma
9.4
yields that
M
has a free summand of rank
\mu
. Thus
M\cong A^{\mu}\oplus W
for some
A
-module
W
. It remains to observe that since
M
is free of rank
\mu
at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
, one must have
W_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}=0
.

Conversely, if
M\cong A^{\mu}\oplus W
with
W
not supported at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
, then
\delta_{A}(W)=0
, by Lemma
3.7
, so

\delta_{A}(M)=\mu\cdot\delta_{A}(A)+\delta_{A}(W)=\mu\cdot\delta_{A}(A)\,.

As to the last part of the statement, write
M\cong A^{\mu}\oplus W
for some
A
-module
W
. When the stated bound on the multiplicity of
M
holds, one gets

\mu\cdot e(A)+e_{A}(W)=e_{A}(A^{\mu}\oplus W)=e_{A}(M)\leq\mu\cdot e(A)

Thus
e_{A}(W)=0
. Since
M
is maximal Cohen–Macaulay, either
W
is
0
or it is also maximal Cohen–Macaulay. The latter case cannot hold,
the multiplicity of a nonzero maximal Cohen–Macaulay module is positive; see
[
13
, §4.6]
.
∎

Complete intersection rings

Next we turn to complete intersections. Any local ring
A
in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}
is of the form
P/I
, where
P\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{O}[\![{\boldsymbol{t}}]\!]
, a ring of formal power series, and in particular, a regular local ring. Such an
A
is complete intersection if and only if the ideal
I
can be generated by a regular sequence; see
[
13
, §2.3]
.

The result below generalizes a criterion for complete intersection due to Wiles
[
56
, Proposition, Appendix]
and Lenstra
[
41
, Theorem in introduction]
, as extended in
[
26
, Proposition A.1]
, both of which deal with the case
c=0
. Compare this result with
[
32
, Theorem 2.6]
that states that
A
in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}
is regular if and only if
\Phi_{A}=0
, if and only if
\Psi_{A}(A)=0
.

Theorem 9.5
.

Let
A
be a local ring in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
with
0ptA\geq c+1
. The ring
A
is complete intersection if, and only if,
\delta_{A}(A)=0
.

Proof.

One can reduce to the case
c=0
as in the proof of Corollary
8.3
. At that point, one can invoke
[
26
, Proposition A.1]
.
∎

Here is an extension of a result due to Diamond
[
25
, Theorem 2.4]
.

Theorem 9.6
.

Let
A
be a local ring in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
and
M
a finitely generated
A
-module with
0pt_{A}M\geq c+1
and
\mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{rank}_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}(M_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}})\neq 0
. Then
\delta_{A}(M)=0
if and only if
A
is complete intersection and

M\cong A^{\mu}\oplus W\qquad\text{where $W_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}=0$.}

When this holds and
e_{A}(M)\leq\mu\cdot e(A)
, then
M
is free.

Proof.

The “if” direction of the statement is clear, given Theorem
9.5
. The proof of “only if” direction uses the observation below.

Claim
.

The desired statement holds for all
A
in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
if it holds for all such
A
with
0ptA\geq 1
.

Indeed, let
A^{\prime}
be the image of
A
in
\operatorname{End}_{A}(M)
. Since the
A
action of
M
factors through the surjection
A\to A^{\prime}
, the depth of
M
as an
A^{\prime}
-module also equals
c+1
. Moreover, Lemma
7.5
yields the first inequality below:

0=\delta_{A}(M)\geq\delta_{A^{\prime}}(M)\geq 0\,.

The equality is by hypothesis and the second inequality is from Corollary
8.3
. Thus one gets the first equality below:

\delta_{A^{\prime}}(M)=0\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Phi_{A}=\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Phi_{A^{\prime}}\,.

The second one is again by Lemma
7.5
. It thus suffices to prove the desired result for
A^{\prime}
, for when
A^{\prime}
is complete intersection Lemma
5.10
implies that
A\cong A^{\prime}
.

This justifies the claim.

We verify the first part of the statement by induction on
c
; the second part then follows as in the proof of Theorem
9.2
.

The base case is
c=0
. By the preceding claim, we can assume
A
has positive depth. Since
c=0
, one has
\delta_{A}(A)\geq 0
; see Corollary
8.3
. Thus the hypothesis that
\delta_{A}(M)=0
and Lemma
3.7
implies
\delta_{A}(A)=0
. Thus
A
is complete intersection, by Theorem
9.5
. At this point, we can invoke Theorem
9.2
to conclude that
M
has a free summand; this is where the hypothesis that the
M_{\mathfrak{p}_{A}}
is nonzero is required, so that the rank of
M
at
\mathfrak{p}_{A}
is nonzero; see Lemma
3.2
.

This completes the proof when
c=0
.

Suppose that
c\geq 1
. One again, thanks to the claim above, we can suppose
0ptA\geq 1
. Pick an element
f
in
\mathfrak{p}_{A}\setminus\mathfrak{p}_{A}^{(2)}
that is not a zero-divisor on
M
and on
A
. Set
B\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}A/fA
and
N\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}M/fM
. The ring
B
is in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}{(c-1)}
and
\delta_{B}(N)=0
, by Theorem
8.2
. Moreover the rank of
N
at
\mathfrak{p}_{B}
equals
\mu
, as can be easily verified. Thus, by induction,
B
is a complete intersection and the
B
-module
N
has a free summand of rank
\mu
. Thus
A
is complete intersection, by
[
13
, Theorem 2.3.4]
, and that the
A
-module
M
has a free summand of rank
\mu
, by Lemma
9.3
.

This completes the proof.
∎

Here is one corollary, which may be seen as a counterpart to Lemma
5.10
. When
c=0
the result below is
[
41
]
, and
[
22
, Theorem 5.8]
, both of which were inspired by Wiles’ work in
[
56
, Appendix]
.

Corollary 9.7
.

Let
\varphi\colon A\to B
be a surjective a map in
\operatorname{C}_{\mathcal{O}}(c)
with
B
a complete intersection. If
\delta_{A}(B)=0
, then
\varphi
is bijective and
A
is a complete intersection.

Proof.

Since
B
is a complete intersection,
\delta_{B}(B)=0
, by Theorem
9.5
. Thus the hypothesis and Theorem
7.4
, applied with
M=B
, imply
\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Phi_{A}=\operatorname{length}_{\mathcal{O}}\Phi_{B}
. The desired result follows from Lemma
5.10
.
∎

Part II
Patching and duality

In this part we summarize the commutative algebra needed for the patching construction in the derived setting. Our main purpose is to show that this construction preserves duality; see Theorem
11.3
.

10.
Abstract patching

This section is mostly a recollection of the patching construction, following the approach of Calegari and Geraghty
[
16
]
, Hansen
[
29
]
,
[
38
]
and Allen et. al.
[
1
]
. In particular, the ultrapatching method is due to Scholze
[
48
]
.

10.1
.

Going forth we assume the residue field
k\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{O}/\varpi\mathcal{O}
of
\mathcal{O}
is finite, and of positive characteristic
\ell
. We fix a power series ring

\mathcal{O}[\![{\boldsymbol{y}}]\!]\quad\text{where $\boldsymbol{y}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}y_{1},\ldots,y_{r}$.}

For each integer
n\geq 1
, fix integers
e(1,n),\ldots,e(r,n)\geq n
and quotient rings

\Lambda_{n}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{O}[\![{\boldsymbol{y}}]\!]/I_{n},\quad\text{where $I_{n}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left((1+y_{i})^{\ell^{e(i,n)}}-1\mid 1\leq i\leq r\right)$}.

These are flat
\mathcal{O}
-algebras, augmented to
\Lambda_{0}=\mathcal{O}
, and complete intersections. For each
n
pick a
\Lambda_{n}
-algebra
R_{n}
that is finite as a
\Lambda_{n}
-module, and such that there is an isomorphism
R_{n}/(\boldsymbol{y})R_{n}=\mathcal{O}\otimes_{\Lambda_{n}}R_{n}\cong R_{0}
; we suppress this isomorphism in our notation and write an equality instead. We assume no other relations between the
R_{n}
. Let
\varphi_{n}\colon\Lambda_{n}\to R_{n}
denote the structure maps.

In what follows we consider derived actions of rings on complexes. We introduce some language and notation to facilitate the discussion.

10.2
.

Let
\Lambda
be a ring,
M
a
\Lambda
-complex and
R
a
\Lambda
-algebra. We say that
M
is a
derived
R
-complex
, or that
R
has a
derived action
on
M
, to mean that there is a map of
\Lambda
-algebras
R\to\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(\Lambda)}(M)
. When
M
is a module this means exactly that there is an
R
-module structure on
M
extending its
\Lambda
-module structure. For a general
M
one only gets an
R
-module structure on the homology modules
\operatorname{H}_{i}(M)
extending their
\Lambda
-structure.

Let
{\mathsf{D}}_{R}(\Lambda)
denote the category of pairs
(M,\iota)
consisting of an
M
in
\mathsf{D}(\Lambda)
and a map
\iota\colon R\to\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(\Lambda)}(M)
of
\Lambda
-algebras. We usually write
M
, instead of the pair, for an object on
{\mathsf{D}}_{R}(\Lambda)
, but one has to keep in mind that an
M
in
\mathsf{D}(\Lambda)
may host different derived
R
-actions. The morphisms in
{\mathsf{D}}_{R}(\Lambda)
are the morphisms of
\Lambda
-complexes compatible with the derived
R
-action, that is to say, a morphism
f\colon M\to N
in
\mathsf{D}(\Lambda)
where for each
r\in R
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

in
\mathsf{D}(\Lambda)
is commutative. We record some stability properties of derived
R
-actions.

Let
(M,\iota)
be a derived
R
-complex. For any
\Lambda
-complex,
C
, the
\operatorname{RHom}_{\Lambda}(M,C)
has a derived
R
-action with structure map the composition

R\xrightarrow{\ \iota\ }\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(\Lambda)}(M)\longrightarrow\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(\Lambda)}(\operatorname{RHom}_{\Lambda}(M,C))

where the one on the right is induced by
\operatorname{RHom}_{\Lambda}(-,C)
. An analogous statement holds for
\operatorname{RHom}_{\Lambda}(C,M)
.

A map
\Lambda\to\Lambda^{\prime}
of rings induces a derived
R^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(R\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda^{\prime})
-action on
\Lambda^{\prime}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{\Lambda}M
in
\mathsf{D}(\Lambda)
via the natural map

\rho\colon\Lambda^{\prime}\otimes_{\Lambda}\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(\Lambda)}(M)\longrightarrow\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(\Lambda^{\prime})}(\Lambda^{\prime}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{\Lambda}M)\,.

Thus one gets a functor
{\mathsf{D}}_{R}(\Lambda)\to{\mathsf{D}}_{R^{\prime}}(\Lambda^{\prime})
that sends
(M,\iota)
to
(\Lambda^{\prime}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{\Lambda}M,\rho(\Lambda^{\prime}\otimes\iota))
.

10.3
.

Fix integers
d
and
\ell_{0}
, and consider a category
\operatorname{PaSy}
of
patching systems
whose objects are collections
\mathfrak{C}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left(C_{n},\alpha_{n}\right)_{n\geqslant 0}
where

(1)

C_{n}
is a perfect
\Lambda_{n}
-complex;

(2)

\operatorname{H}_{i}(k\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{\Lambda_{n}}C_{n})=0
for
i\not\in[d,d+\ell_{0}]
;

(3)

C_{n}
is a derived
R_{n}
-complex;

(4)

\alpha_{n}\colon\mathcal{O}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{\Lambda_{n}}C_{n}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}}C_{0}
is an isomorphism in
{\mathsf{D}}_{R_{n}}(\mathcal{O})
, where the
R_{n}
-action on
C_{0}
is via the surjection
R_{n}\to R_{0}
.

Given (2), condition (1) is equivalent to requiring that
C_{n}
is in
\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\,\Lambda_{n})
.

A morphism
f\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{D}
in
\operatorname{PaSy}
is a family
f_{n}\colon C_{n}\to D_{n}
of morphisms in
{\mathsf{D}}_{R_{n}}(\Lambda_{n})
commuting with the morphisms in the systems defining
\mathfrak{C}
and
\mathfrak{D}
. The category
\operatorname{PaSy}
is defined over the system of rings
(\Lambda_{n})
; this will be emphasized only if needed.

10.4
.

Consider the power series ring

\displaystyle S_{\infty}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{O}[\![{\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{w}}]\!]\quad\text{where $\boldsymbol{w}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}w_{1},\ldots,w_{j}$, and set}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{w})\,.

The ring
\mathcal{O}[\![{\boldsymbol{y}}]\!]
is a subring of
S_{\infty}
. In what follows we write
(-)^{{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}
for the extension of scalars functor (also known as
framing
) from the category of finitely generated
\mathcal{O}[\![{\boldsymbol{y}}]\!]
-modules to finitely generated
S_{\infty}
-modules; thus

M^{{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}S_{\infty}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}[\![{\boldsymbol{y}}]\!]}M\,.

When
\Lambda
is a module-finite
\mathcal{O}[\![{\boldsymbol{y}}]\!]
-algebra and
M
a finitely generated
\Lambda
-module,
\Lambda^{{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}
is a module-finite
S_{\infty}
-algebra and
M^{{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}
a finitely generated
\Lambda^{{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}
-module. Moreover, since the extension
\mathcal{O}[\![{\boldsymbol{y}}]\!]\to S_{\infty}
is flat,
(-)^{{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}
extends to a functor on appropriate derived categories, derived complexes, and even to the category of patching systems. In particular, one gets a functor from patching systems over
(\Lambda_{n})
to those defined over
(\Lambda^{{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}_{n})
. These remarks will be used without further comment.

10.5
.

Let
r,\ell_{0}
and
j
be the integers from
10.1
,
10.3
, and
10.4
, respectively. Fix a complete local, flat, noetherian
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
R_{\infty}
of dimension
r+j-\ell_{0}+1
, equipped with surjective
\mathcal{O}
-algebra morphism
\pi_{n}\colon R_{\infty}\twoheadrightarrow R^{{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}_{n}
for
n\geq 1
. We do not assume the
\pi_{n}
are compatible. The ultrapatching construction of
[
1
, Section 6.4]
and the work of
[
29
]
give the following

Theorem 10.6
.

There exists a homomorphism
\varphi_{\infty}\colon S_{\infty}\to R_{\infty}
making
R_{\infty}
into a finite
S_{\infty}
-algebra and a functor
\mathcal{P}\colon\operatorname{PaSy}\to\mathrm{mod}\,R_{\infty}
with the following properties:

(1)

\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C})
is a maximal Cohen–Macaulay
R_{\infty}
-module, nonzero when
\mathfrak{C}\neq 0
.

(2)

There is a surjection
R_{\infty}/\mathfrak{n}R_{\infty}\twoheadrightarrow R_{0}
and an isomorphism of
R_{0}
-modules

\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C})/\mathfrak{n}\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C})\cong\operatorname{H}_{d}(C_{0})\,.

(3)

Treating
\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C})
as an
S_{\infty}
-module via
\varphi_{\infty}
, for any open ideal
\mathfrak{a}\subseteq S_{\infty}
and for infinitely many
n
, there are isomorphisms

(S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a})\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{S_{\infty}}\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C})[d]\xrightarrow{\ \raisebox{-1.20552pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}\ }(S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a})\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{\Lambda^{{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}_{n}}C^{{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}_{n},

of derived
R_{\infty}
-complexes, where
R_{\infty}
acts on
C^{{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}_{n}
via
\iota_{n}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}\pi_{n}
.

(4)

There is an isomorphism of
R_{\infty}
-algebras:

\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(S_{\infty})}(\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C}))\cong\lim_{\mathfrak{a}}\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a})}((S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a})\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{S_{\infty}}\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C}))

(5)

Fix a map
f\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{C}
in
\operatorname{PaSy}
. If there exists an element
\tau\in R_{\infty}
such that

\pi_{n}(\tau)=f^{{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}_{n}\quad\text{ as elements in }\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(\Lambda^{{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}_{n})}(C^{{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}_{n})

for all but finitely many
n
, then
\mathcal{P}(f)=\tau
in
\operatorname{End}_{R_{\infty}}(\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C}))
.

Proof.

As noted in
10.4
, each patching system
\mathfrak{C}
over
(\Lambda_{n})
gives rise to the framed patching system
\mathfrak{C}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}
over
(\Lambda^{{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}_{n})
. Parts (3)–(5) of the statement only involve
{\mathfrak{C}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}}
, so does the construction of the functor
\mathcal{P}
. Thus replacing
(\Lambda_{n}),(R_{n})
and
\mathfrak{C}
by
(\Lambda^{{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}_{n}),(R^{{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}_{n})
, and
(\mathfrak{C}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\square}})
, we assume we are in the framed context.

We use the ultrapatching method introduced in
[
48
, §9]
. Fix a non-principal ultrafilter
\mathfrak{F}
on
\mathbb{N}
. Take any finite local ring
A
(that is to say, a ring with finite cardinality). Consider the ring
\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}A
. As
A
is finite and
\mathfrak{F}
is an ultrafilter, for any element
(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots)
in
\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}A
there is a unique element
a\in A
for which
\{n\in\mathbb{N}|a_{n}=a\}\in\mathfrak{F}
. Denote this element
f(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots)
. It is easy to verify that the function
f\colon\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}A\to A
is an
A
-algebra homomorphism, and that for any
I\in\mathfrak{F}
,
f
factors through the natural quotient map
\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}A\twoheadrightarrow\prod_{n\in I}A
.

Let
\mathfrak{m}_{A}
be the maximal ideal of
A
and let
\mathfrak{p}=f^{-1}(\mathfrak{m}_{A})
; this is a maximal ideal of
\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}A
. Then
f
induces an isomorphism
\left(\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}A\right)_{\mathfrak{p}}\xrightarrow{\sim}A
and so in particular for any
I\in\mathfrak{F}
, the map
\left(\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}A\right)_{\mathfrak{p}}\to\left(\prod_{n\in I}A\right)_{\mathfrak{p}}
is an isomorphism.

For any sequence
M_{1},M_{2},\ldots
of
A
-modules (respectively
A
-algebras) the kernel of the map
\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}M_{n}\twoheadrightarrow\left(\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}M_{n}\right)_{\mathfrak{p}}
is the set of sequences
(m_{1},m_{2},\ldots)\in\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}M_{n}
for which
\{n\in\mathbb{N}|m_{n}=0\}
is in
\mathfrak{F}
. By definition this implies that
\left(\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}M_{n}\right)_{\mathfrak{p}}
is the ultraproduct of the
M_{n}
’s.

If the
M_{n}
’s are all finite with uniformly bounded cardinality, standard properties of ultraproducts imply that the set of
i\in\mathbb{N}
for which there is an isomorphism

\left(\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}M_{n}\right)_{\mathfrak{p}}\cong M_{i}

as
A
-modules (respectively, as
A
-algebras) is an element of
\mathfrak{F}
. In particular, as
\mathfrak{F}
is a
nonprincipal
ultrafilter,
\left(\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}M_{n}\right)_{\mathfrak{p}}
is isomorphic to
M_{i}
for infinitely many
i
.

For any open ideal
\mathfrak{a}\subseteq S_{\infty}
and integer
s\geq 0
set

R(s,\mathfrak{a},\infty)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left(\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}\otimes_{\Lambda_{n}}R_{n}/\mathfrak{m}_{R_{n}}^{s}\right)_{\mathfrak{p}}

where when
I_{n}\not\subseteq\mathfrak{a}
we interpret

S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}\otimes_{\Lambda_{n}}R_{n}/\mathfrak{m}_{R_{n}}^{s}=0\,;

this only happens for finitely many
n
. As in
[
1
, Remark 6.4.14]
, we get surjections
R_{\infty}\twoheadrightarrow R(s,\mathfrak{a},\infty)
for all
s,\mathfrak{a}
, inducing a surjection
R_{\infty}\twoheadrightarrow\lim_{s,\mathfrak{a}}R(s,\mathfrak{a},\infty)
. Since each
R(s,\mathfrak{a},\infty)
is an
S_{\infty}
-algebra, the structure map
S_{\infty}\to\lim_{s,\mathfrak{a}}R(s,\mathfrak{a},\infty)
lifts to a homomorphism

\varphi_{\infty}\colon S_{\infty}\to R_{\infty}

as desired. For the moment, any lift will do, but later on we prove that there exists a lift that ensures that
R_{\infty}
is finite as an
S_{\infty}
-module. In any case,
[
1
, Lemma 6.4.15]
gives the surjection

R_{\infty}/\mathfrak{n}R_{\infty}=\mathcal{O}\otimes_{S_{\infty}}R_{\infty}\twoheadrightarrow R_{0}\,.

Let
\mathfrak{C}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left(C_{n},\alpha_{n}\right)_{n\geqslant 0}
be a patching system. Since each
C_{n}
is perfect, we can replacing it by its minimal free resolution over
\Lambda_{n}
, and assume
C_{n}
is a bounded complex of free modules; see
[
47
, Chapter 2, Theorem 2.4]
. Then
\mathcal{O}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{\Lambda_{n}}C_{n}
is represented by the complex
\mathcal{O}\otimes_{\Lambda_{n}}C_{n}
, which is also free and minimal as an
\mathcal{O}
-complex. Thus we can assume
\alpha
is a morphism of
\mathcal{O}
-complexes:

\alpha_{n}\colon\mathcal{O}\otimes_{\Lambda_{n}}C_{n}\xrightarrow{\ \raisebox{-1.20552pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}\ }C_{0}\,.

Since this is a quasi-isomorphism between minimal free complexes, it is an isomorphism; see
[
47
, Chapter 2, Theorem 2.4]
. For any open ideal
\mathfrak{a}\subseteq S_{\infty}
set

C(\mathfrak{a},\infty)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left(\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}\otimes_{\Lambda_{n}}C_{n}\right)_{\mathfrak{p}}

where as before when
I_{n}\not\subseteq\mathfrak{a}
the tensor product in question is assumed to be
0
.

Since
C_{n}
is a minimal free complex over
\Lambda_{n}
, for each integer
i
one has

\displaystyle\operatorname{rank}_{\Lambda_{n}}(C_{n})_{i}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{rank}_{k}\operatorname{H}_{i}(k\otimes_{\Lambda_{n}}C_{n})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{rank}_{k}\operatorname{H}_{i}(k\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{O}\otimes_{\Lambda_{n}}C_{n}))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{rank}_{k}\operatorname{H}_{i}(k\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}C_{0})

The last equality holds because
\mathcal{O}\otimes_{\Lambda_{n}}C_{n}\cong C_{0}
, via
\alpha_{n}
. Thus the integers

c_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{rank}_{\Lambda_{n}}(C_{n})_{i}

are independent of
n
. It follows that the complex
S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}\otimes_{\Lambda_{n}}C_{n}
over
S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}
is minimal, free and for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and all
n
, one has

\operatorname{rank}_{S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}}(S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}\otimes_{\Lambda_{n}}C_{n})_{i}=c_{i}\,.

In particular, the modules
(S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}\otimes_{\Lambda_{n}}C_{n})_{i}
are all finite of bounded cardinality. It follows that
C(\mathfrak{a},\infty)
is a minimal complex of finite free modules over
S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}
and that for infinitely many
n
there are isomorphisms

C(\mathfrak{a},\infty)\cong S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}\otimes_{\Lambda_{n}}C_{n}\,.

Thus
\operatorname{rank}_{S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}}C(\mathfrak{a},\infty)_{i}=c_{i}
for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. As in
[
1
, Proposition 6.4.10]
one gets

S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}\otimes_{S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{b}}C(\mathfrak{b},\infty)\cong C(\mathfrak{a},\infty)

for any
\mathfrak{b}\subseteq\mathfrak{a}
. Set

C_{\infty}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\lim_{s}C\left(\mathfrak{m}_{S_{\infty}}^{s},\infty\right).

By
[
38
, Lemma 2.13]
this is a minimal complex of
S_{\infty}
-modules with

S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}\otimes_{S_{\infty}}C_{\infty}\cong C(\mathfrak{a},\infty)\quad\text{for all $\mathfrak{a}$,}

and for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
we have

\operatorname{H}_{i}(C_{\infty})\cong\lim_{s}\operatorname{H}_{i}\left(C\left(\mathfrak{m}_{S_{\infty}}^{s},\infty\right)\right)\,.

Also (the proof of)
[
38
, Lemma 2.13(3)]
implies that the natural map

\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(S_{\infty})}(C_{\infty})\longrightarrow\lim_{\mathfrak{a}}\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a})}(C(\mathfrak{a},\infty))

is an isomorphism.

The structure maps
R_{\infty}\xrightarrow{\pi_{n}}R_{n}\to\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(\Lambda_{n})}(C_{n})
induce maps

R_{\infty}\longrightarrow R(s,\mathfrak{a},\infty)\longrightarrow\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a})}(C(\mathfrak{a},\infty))

for all
s\gg 0
, compatible with the maps

\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{b})}(C(\mathfrak{b},\infty))\longrightarrow\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a})}(C(\mathfrak{a},\infty))

for
\mathfrak{b}\subseteq\mathfrak{a}
. Thus these maps induce a map

\iota_{\infty}\colon R_{\infty}\longrightarrow\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(S_{\infty})}(C_{\infty})\,.

As the maps

S_{\infty}\longrightarrow R_{n}\longrightarrow\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(\Lambda_{n})}(C_{n})

induce the natural action of
S_{\infty}
on
C_{n}
it follows that the map

S_{\infty}\xrightarrow{\varphi_{\infty}}R_{\infty}\longrightarrow\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(S_{\infty})}(C_{\infty})

induces the natural action of
S_{\infty}
on
C_{\infty}
. Thus the
S_{\infty}
action on
C_{\infty}
extends to a derived
R_{\infty}
-action.
Set

\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{H}_{d}(C_{\infty})\quad\text{in $\operatorname{Mod}{R_{\infty}}$.}

This is finitely generated as an
S_{\infty}
-module, for
C_{\infty}
is a finite free complex over
S_{\infty}
, and hence also as an
R_{\infty}
-module. Thus
\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C})
is in
\operatorname{mod}{R_{\infty}}
.

We check that this assignment is functorial on
\operatorname{PaSy}
. Without loss of generality, in the remainder of the proof we assume
d=0
, to ease up notation. Let
f\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{D}
be a morphism in
\operatorname{PaSy}
. As before we assume each
D_{n}
is a finite free
\Lambda_{n}
-complex, and that
\mathcal{O}\otimes_{\Lambda_{n}}D_{n}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}}D_{0}
is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{O}
-complexes. Moreover, we assume that the maps
f_{n}\colon C_{n}\to D_{n}
are morphisms of complexes; these are uniquely defined up to homotopy, and we leave it to the reader to check that the patching process preserves these homotopies.

For each open ideal
\mathfrak{a}\subseteq S_{\infty}
the
f_{n}
induce maps

f_{n}\colon C_{n}\longrightarrow D_{n}\quad\text{and}\quad f_{\mathfrak{a},n}\colon S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}\otimes_{\Lambda_{n}}C_{n}\longrightarrow S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}\otimes_{\Lambda_{n}}D_{n}\,,

and hence a map
f_{\mathfrak{a},\infty}\colon C(\mathfrak{a},\infty)\to D(\mathfrak{a},\infty)
. By construction

S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}\otimes_{S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{b}}f_{\mathfrak{b},n}=f_{\mathfrak{a},n}\quad\text{for all $\mathfrak{b}\subseteq\mathfrak{a}$ and $n$},

so
S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}\otimes_{S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{b}}f_{\mathfrak{b},\infty}=f_{\mathfrak{a},\infty}
. By
[
38
, Lemma 2.13(3)]
, for each
s
the map
f_{\mathfrak{m}_{S_{\infty}}^{s},\infty}
determine a unique, up to homotopy, map
f_{\infty}\colon C_{\infty}\to D_{\infty}
satisfying

S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{m}_{S_{\infty}}^{s}\otimes_{S_{\infty}}f_{\infty}=f_{\mathfrak{m}_{S_{\infty}}^{s},\infty}

and hence
S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}\otimes_{S_{\infty}}f_{\infty}=f_{\mathfrak{a},\infty}
. We can now define the
S_{\infty}
-module morphism

\mathcal{P}(f)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{H}_{0}(f_{\infty})\colon\operatorname{H}_{0}(C_{\infty})\longrightarrow\operatorname{H}_{0}(D_{\infty}),.

This map depends only on the homotopy class of
f_{\infty}
, so does not depend on the choices of
f_{n}
. It follows that
f\mapsto\mathcal{P}(f)
is functorial.

As the maps
f_{n}
commute with the structure maps

R_{n}\to\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(\Lambda_{n})}(C_{n})\quad\text{and}\quad R_{n}\to\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(\Lambda_{n})}(D_{n})

the map
f_{\infty}\colon C_{\infty}\to D_{\infty}
commutes with the maps

R_{\infty}\longrightarrow\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(S_{\infty})}(C_{\infty})\quad\text{and}\quad R_{\infty}\longrightarrow\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(S_{\infty})}(D_{\infty})\,,

and so
\mathcal{P}(f)
is in fact an
R_{\infty}
-module homomorphism. It follows that
\mathcal{P}
defines a functor
\operatorname{PaSy}\to\mathrm{mod}\,{R_{\infty}}
.

Now we verify claims (1)–(5). Recall that we have assumed
d=0
.

(1) Each
C_{\infty}
is a finite free
S_{\infty}
-complex with a derived
R_{\infty}
-action. For each
n
one has
(C_{n})_{i}=0
for
i\not\in[0,\ell_{0}]
, thus
(C_{\infty})_{i}=0
for the same range of values of
i
. Since
\ell_{0}=\dim S_{\infty}-\dim R_{\infty}
, we can apply Lemma
10.7
below to deduce that the
R_{\infty}
-module
\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C})
is maximal Cohen–Macaulay, as claimed.

Let
R^{\prime}
denote the image of
R_{\infty}
in
\operatorname{End}_{S_{\infty}}(\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C}))
. Then
\dim R^{\prime}=\dim R_{\infty}
, as the
R_{\infty}
-module
\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C})
is maximal Cohen–Macaulay. Moreover,
R^{\prime}
is a finite
S_{\infty}
-algebra. At this point, one can readily adapt the proof of
[
8
, Lemmas 3.6 and 3.7]
to deduce that the lift
S_{\infty}\to R_{\infty}
of the map
S_{\infty}\to R^{\prime}
can be chosen to ensure that
R_{\infty}
is finite as an
S_{\infty}
-module.

(2) Take any integer
s\geq 0
and consider the open ideal
\mathfrak{a}_{s}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathfrak{n}+\varpi^{s}S_{\infty}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C})/\mathfrak{n}\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{O}/(\varpi^{s})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C})/{\mathfrak{a}_{s}}\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{H}_{0}(S_{\infty}/{\mathfrak{a}_{s}}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{S_{\infty}}\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C}))

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{H}_{0}(S_{\infty}/{\mathfrak{a}_{s}}\otimes_{S_{\infty}}C_{\infty})

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{H}_{0}(C(\mathfrak{a}_{s},\infty)).

But now as
\mathcal{O}\otimes_{\Lambda_{n}}C_{n}\cong C_{0}
, in
\mathsf{D}(\mathcal{O})
one gets

\displaystyle C(\mathfrak{a}_{s},\infty)

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}S_{\infty}/{\mathfrak{a}_{s}}\otimes_{\Lambda_{n}}C_{n}\right)_{\mathfrak{p}}

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}S_{\infty}/{\mathfrak{a}_{s}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{O}\otimes_{\Lambda_{n}}C_{n})\right)_{\mathfrak{p}}

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}S_{\infty}/{\mathfrak{a}_{s}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}C_{0}\right)_{\mathfrak{p}}

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{O}/(\varpi^{s})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}C_{0}\right)_{\mathfrak{p}}

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{O}/(\varpi^{s})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}C_{0}\,.

Combining the computations above, one gets isomorphisms

\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C})/\mathfrak{n}\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{O}/(\varpi^{s})\cong\operatorname{H}_{0}(\mathcal{O}/(\varpi^{s})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}C_{0})\cong\mathcal{O}/(\varpi^{s})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\operatorname{H}_{0}(C_{0})\,.

Taking inverse limits gives the desired isomorphism
\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C})/\mathfrak{n}\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C})\cong\operatorname{H}_{0}(C_{0})
.

(3) The fact that
\operatorname{H}_{i}(C_{\infty})=0
for
i\neq 0
also implies that

C_{\infty}\simeq\operatorname{H}_{0}(C_{\infty})=\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C})\quad\text{in ${\mathsf{D}}_{R_{\infty}}(S_{\infty})$.}

The discussion above thus yields

(S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a})\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{S_{\infty}}\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C})\simeq(S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a})\otimes_{S_{\infty}}C_{\infty}\cong C(\mathfrak{a},\infty)\cong(S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a})\otimes_{\Lambda_{n}}C_{n}

for infinitely many
n
, proving (2).

(4) This now follows from the above observation that the natural map

\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(S_{\infty})}(C_{\infty})\longrightarrow\lim_{\mathfrak{a}}\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a})}(C(\mathfrak{a},\infty))

is an isomorphism.

(5) If
f_{n}=\pi_{n}(\tau)
in
\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(\Lambda_{n})}(C_{n})
for all but finitely many
n
, then for any open ideal
\mathfrak{a}
the map
f_{\mathfrak{a},\infty}
is the image of
\tau
under the map

R_{\infty}\longrightarrow R(s,\mathfrak{a},\infty)\longrightarrow\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a})}(C(\mathfrak{a},\infty))=\operatorname{End}_{{\mathsf{K}}(S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a})}(C(\mathfrak{a},\infty))\,,

and so by the construction of
\mathcal{P}(f)
we get
f_{\infty}=\iota_{\infty}(\tau)
, which gives
\mathcal{P}(f)=\tau
by the definition of the
R_{\infty}
-module structure on
\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C})
.
∎

The result below was used in proof of Theorem
10.6
(1); see
[
16
, Lemma 6.2]
.
The basic argument goes to the acyclicity criteria of Buchsbaum and Eisenbud
[
13
, Proposition 1.4.12]
, and Peskine and Szpiro
[
13
, 1.4.24]
.

Lemma 10.7
.

Let
S
be a Cohen–Macaulay local ring with residue field
k
. If
C
is an
S
-complex in
\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\,S)
admitting a derived action of a noetherian local
S
-algebra
R
with
\dim R\leq\dim S
and

\operatorname{H}_{i}(k\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{S}C)=0\quad\text{for $i\notin[0,\dim S-\dim R]$}\,,

then
C\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}}\operatorname{H}_{0}(C)
and the
R
-module
\operatorname{H}_{0}(C)
is maximal Cohen–Macaulay.

Proof.

Given that
C
is in
\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\,S)
and the hypothesis on
\operatorname{H}_{i}(k\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{S}C)
replacing
C
by its minimal free resolution, we can assume
C
is a finite free complex with
C_{i}=0
for
i\notin[0,\dim S-\dim R]
. Let
R^{\prime}
denote the image of
R
in
\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(S)}(C)
. Then
R^{\prime}
is a finite
S
-algebra and
C
has a derived
R^{\prime}
-action. Thus the
S
-complex
\operatorname{Hom}_{S}(C,S)
inherits a derived
R^{\prime}
-action so each
\operatorname{H}_{i}(\operatorname{Hom}_{S}(C,S))
is an
R^{\prime}
-module. Since
R^{\prime}
is a finite
S
-algebra, for each
i
we get

\displaystyle\dim_{S}\operatorname{H}_{i}(\operatorname{Hom}_{S}(C,S))\leq\dim_{S}R^{\prime}=\dim R^{\prime}\leq\dim R\,.

For the next step, it is convenient to use the notion of dimension of the complex
\operatorname{Hom}_{S}(C,S)
, defined as follows:

\dim_{S}\operatorname{Hom}_{S}(C,S)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\max\{\dim_{S}\operatorname{H}_{i}(\operatorname{Hom}_{S}(C,S))-i\mid i\in[\dim R-\dim S,0]\}\,.

See
[
19
, (2)]
and references therein. Since the complex
\operatorname{Hom}_{S}(C,S)
is zero outside the range
[\dim R-\dim S,0]
, one immediately gets the inequality below:

\dim_{S}\operatorname{Hom}_{S}(C,S)\leq\dim S\,.

This gives the inequality on the right in:

0\leq\max\{i\mid\operatorname{H}_{i}(C)\neq 0\}=\dim_{S}\operatorname{Hom}_{S}(C,S)-\dim S\leq 0\,.

The equality is from
[
19
, Proposition 6]
. It follows that
\operatorname{H}_{i}(C)=0
for
i>0
, that is to say,
C
is a free resolution of
\operatorname{H}_{0}(C)
over
S
. Then the Auslander–Buchsbaum formula
[
13
, Theorem 1.3.3]
implies the inequality below:

\displaystyle 0pt_{R}\operatorname{H}_{0}(C)

\displaystyle=0pt_{R^{\prime}}\operatorname{H}_{0}(C)

\displaystyle=0pt_{S}\operatorname{H}_{0}(C)

\displaystyle\geq\dim S-(\dim S-\dim R)=\dim R\,.

The equalities hold because the maps
R\to R^{\prime}
and
S\to R^{\prime}
are finite. Thus the
R
-module
\operatorname{H}_{0}(C)
is maximal Cohen–Macaulay.
∎

The result below on derived action is used in the proof of Proposition
(Wt1)
.

Lemma 10.8
.

Let
\Lambda
be a noetherian local ring with residue field
k
and fix
C
in
\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\,\Lambda)
such that
\operatorname{H}_{i}(k\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{\Lambda}C)=0
for
i\neq 0,1
and
\mathrm{projdim}_{\Lambda}\operatorname{H}_{0}(C)\leq 1
. Set
C^{\dagger}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{RHom}_{\Lambda}(C,\Lambda)[1]
.

If
\varphi\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(\Lambda)}(C)
is such that
\operatorname{H}_{0}(\varphi)=0=\operatorname{H}_{0}(\varphi^{\dagger})
, then
\varphi=0
in
\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\,\Lambda)
.

Proof.

Given the hypotheses on
C
, replacing it by its minimal free resolution, we can assume the
\Lambda
-module
C_{i}
is finite free for each
i
, and equal to
0
for
i\neq 0,1
. Thus
\varphi
can be represented as a pair
(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{0})
where
\varphi_{i}\colon C_{i}\to C_{i}
are
\Lambda
-linear maps satisfying
\varphi_{0}d=d\varphi_{1}
, where
d\colon C_{1}\to C_{0}
is the differential on
C
. Moreover
\varphi^{\dagger}
can be realized as the morphism
\operatorname{Hom}_{\Lambda}(\varphi,\Lambda)[1]
, and hence is represented by the pair
(\varphi_{0}^{*},\varphi_{1}^{*})
. Moreover
\varphi\cong\varphi^{\dagger\dagger}
so
\varphi
is zero in
\mathsf{D}(\Lambda)
if and only if so is
\varphi^{\dagger}
.

The hypothesis
\operatorname{H}_{0}(\varphi)=0
implies that there is an map
h\colon C_{0}\to C_{1}
of
\Lambda
-modules with
\varphi_{0}=dh
. Replacing
\varphi
by the homotopy-equivalent morphism
\varphi+[h,d]
one can assume
\varphi_{0}=0
. Thus the morphism
\varphi
factors as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{H}_{1}(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}

with
\iota j=\varphi_{1}
. Since
\mathrm{projdim}_{\Lambda}\operatorname{H}_{0}(C)\leq 1
the
\Lambda
-module
\operatorname{Coker}(\iota)\cong\mathrm{Image}(d)
is free, so
\iota
is split-injective, and in particular
\operatorname{H}_{1}(C)
is free as well. It follows that
\iota^{*}
is surjective. Applying
(-)^{*}
to the diagram above yields the diagram of complexes of
\Lambda
-modules

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{H}_{1}(C)^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{*}}

Since
j^{*}i^{*}=\varphi_{1}^{*}
and
\iota^{*}
is surjective, the hypothesis that
\operatorname{H}_{0}(\varphi^{\dagger})=\operatorname{H}_{-1}(\varphi^{*})=0
implies that
\operatorname{H}_{-1}(j^{*})=0
, and since
\operatorname{H}_{1}(C)^{*}
is free,
j^{*}
lifts through
d^{*}
, that is to say,
j^{*}
is homotopic to 0. Thus
\varphi^{\dagger}
is homotopic to zero, as claimed.
∎

11.
Duality

In this section we prove that patching commutes with duality, in a suitable sense. The setup and notation is as in Section
10
. In particular, we consider local rings

\mathcal{O}[\![{\boldsymbol{y}}]\!]\,,\quad S\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{O}[\![{\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{w}}]\!]\,,\quad\text{and $R_{\infty}$.}

All rings considered are quotients of these local rings, and hence possess dualizing complexes. We normalize them as in Section
4
, making them unique up to isomorphism in the appropriate derived category. Recall that
\omega_{A}
denotes the dualizing complex of a local ring
A
and
\omega_{A}(-)
the duality functor; see (
4.1
).

11.1
.

The following observations about dualizing complexes are used implicitly.

(1)

If
A
is Gorenstein, for
M\in\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\,A)
and free resolution
F\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}}M
, one has

\omega_{A}(M)\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(F,A)[\dim A]\qquad\text{in $\mathsf{D}(A)$.}

In particular, when
M
is perfect, so is
\omega_{A}(M)
.

(2)

If
A\to B
is a finite map, then
\omega_{A}(M)\cong\omega_{B}(M)
for
M\in\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\,B)
.

(3)

If
A\to B
is a flat map with
A,B
Gorenstein, and
M
is in
\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\,A)
, then

\omega_{A}(M)\otimes_{A}B\cong\omega_{B}(B\otimes_{A}M)[\dim A-\dim B]\quad\text{in $\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\,B)$.}

Indeed,
A
Gorenstein means
\omega_{A}\cong A[\dim A]
—see
[
51
,
Tag 0DW7
]
—so (1) follows from isomorphisms

\operatorname{RHom}_{A}(M,\omega_{A})\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(F,\omega_{A})\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(F,A)[\dim A]\,.

As to (2), since
\operatorname{RHom}_{A}(B,\omega_{A})\cong\omega_{B}
in
\mathsf{D}(B)
, by

[
51
,
Tag 0AX1
]
, one has

\operatorname{RHom}_{A}(M,\omega_{A})\cong\operatorname{RHom}_{B}(M\operatorname{RHom}_{A}(B,\omega_{A}))\cong\operatorname{RHom}_{B}(M,\omega_{B})\,.

Part (3) is a direct consequence of the isomorphism:

\operatorname{RHom}_{A}(M,A)\otimes_{A}B\cong\operatorname{RHom}_{B}(B\otimes_{A}M,B)\,,

which is valid because
M
is in
\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\,A)
and
B
is flat as an
A
-module.

11.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(C_{n},\alpha_{n})_{n\geqslant 0}
be a patching system over
(\Lambda_{n})
; see
10.3
. The
dual
of
\mathfrak{C}
is the patching system
{\mathfrak{C}}^{\dagger}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(C^{\dagger}_{n},\alpha^{\dagger}_{n})_{n\geqslant 0}
where

C^{\dagger}_{n}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{RHom}_{\mathcal{O}}(C_{n},\mathcal{O})[2d+\ell_{0}]=\omega_{\mathcal{O}}(C_{n})[2d+\ell_{0}-1]

Since
\Lambda_{n}
is Gorenstein and
\dim\Lambda_{n}=1
, one gets

C^{\dagger}_{n}=\omega_{\Lambda_{n}}(C_{n})[2d+\ell_{0}-1]\cong\operatorname{RHom}_{\Lambda_{n}}(C_{n},\Lambda_{n})[2d+\ell_{0}]\,.

In particular,
C^{\dagger}_{n}
is a perfect complex over
\Lambda_{n}
, and hence it satisfies condition (1) in
10.3
. The suspension by
2d+\ell_{0}
ensures that condition (2) is also satisfied. Since duals and suspension are functors on the category of derived
R_{n}
-complexes,
C^{\dagger}_{n}
satisfies (3) as well. Finally, a standard computation yields that applying
\omega_{\mathcal{O}}(-)
to
\alpha_{n}
yields an isomorphism

\omega_{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha_{n})\colon\omega_{\mathcal{O}}(C_{0})\xrightarrow{\ \raisebox{-1.20552pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}\ }\mathcal{O}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{\Lambda_{n}}\omega_{\Lambda_{n}}(C_{n})

compatible with derived
R_{n}
-actions. Setting
\alpha^{\dagger}_{n}
to be the
(2d+\ell_{0})
suspension of the inverse of this morphism completes the definition of the
{\mathfrak{C}}^{\dagger}
.

It is clear that
(-)^{\dagger}
defines a contravariant functor on
\operatorname{PaSy}
, and that the equivalence
(-)\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}}\omega_{\Lambda_{n}}^{2}(-)
induce a natural isomorphism in
\operatorname{PaSy}
:

\mathfrak{C}\xrightarrow{\ \raisebox{-1.20552pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}\ }({\mathfrak{C}}^{\dagger})^{\dagger}

The statement below is the desired result on duality. The argument is an extension of that for
[
44
, Proposition 4.10]
. Here
(-)^{\vee}
denotes the duality functor on Cohen–Macaulay modules defined in (
4.3
). Applying it is justified for
\mathcal{P}(-)
takes values in maximal Cohen–Macaulay
R_{\infty}
-modules by the Theorem
10.6
.

Theorem 11.3
.

For
\mathfrak{C}
in
\operatorname{PaSy}
, there is an natural isomorphism of
R_{\infty}
-modules

\mathcal{P}({\mathfrak{C}}^{\dagger})\cong\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C})^{\vee}\,.

This is functorial in
\mathfrak{C}
, in that, if
f\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{D}
is a morphism in
\operatorname{PaSy}
then

\mathcal{P}(f^{\dagger})=\mathcal{P}(f)^{\vee}\,.

Proof.

To begin with, given the discussion in
11.2
, we can assume we are already in the framed situation,
j=0
, as in the proof of Theorem
10.6
. Moreover, we can assume the
\Lambda_{n}
-complex
C_{n}
is finite free and concentrated in degrees
[0,\ell_{0}]
for each
n
. The functor
(-)^{\dagger}
preserves these properties. Let

\widehat{\mathcal{P}}\colon\operatorname{PaSy}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{D}}_{R_{\infty}}(S_{\infty})

be the functor
\mathfrak{C}\mapsto C_{\infty}
and
f\mapsto f_{\infty}
constructed in the proof of Theorem
10.6
, so that
\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C})=\operatorname{H}_{0}(\widehat{\mathcal{P}}(\mathfrak{C}))
. It suffices to verify that there is an isomorphism

\widehat{\mathcal{P}}({\mathfrak{C}}^{\dagger})\xrightarrow{\ \raisebox{-1.20552pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}\ }\omega_{S_{\infty}}(\widehat{\mathcal{P}}(\mathfrak{C}))[-\dim R_{\infty}]

in
\mathsf{D}(S_{\infty})
, functorial in
\mathfrak{C}
, and compatible with
R_{\infty}
-actions. Indeed, given this isomorphism one gets

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}({\mathfrak{C}}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{H}_{0}(\widehat{\mathcal{P}}({\mathfrak{C}}^{\dagger}))

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{H}_{\dim R_{\infty}}(\omega_{S_{\infty}}(\widehat{\mathcal{P}}(\mathfrak{C})))

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{H}_{\dim R_{\infty}}(\omega_{S_{\infty}}(\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C})))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C})^{\vee}

keeping in mind that
\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C})
is Cohen–Macaulay of dimension
\dim R_{\infty}
.

As to the isomorphism above, fix
\mathfrak{C}=(C_{n},\alpha_{n})_{n\geqslant 0}
in
\operatorname{PaSy}
. For any open ideal
\mathfrak{a}\subseteq S_{\infty}
containing the ideal
\operatorname{Ker}(S_{\infty}\twoheadrightarrow\Lambda_{n})
the complex
S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{\Lambda_{n}}C_{n}
of
S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}
-modules is finite free, so using the properties of duality listed in
11.1
, one gets a natural isomorphism

S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{\Lambda_{n}}\omega_{\Lambda_{n}}(C_{n})\xrightarrow{\ \raisebox{-1.20552pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}\ }\operatorname{RHom}_{S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}}(S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{\Lambda_{n}}C_{n},S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a})[1]

respecting the action of
R_{\infty}
on both sides. Here we are using the fact that
\Lambda_{n}
is Gorenstein and
C_{n}
is a perfect
\Lambda_{n}
-complex. Similarly there is an isomorphism

S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{S_{\infty}}\omega_{S_{\infty}}(\widehat{\mathcal{P}}(\mathfrak{C}))\xrightarrow{\ \raisebox{-1.20552pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}\ }\operatorname{RHom}_{S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}}(S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{S_{\infty}}\widehat{\mathcal{P}}(\mathfrak{C}),S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a})[\dim S_{\infty}]

again compatible with the action of
R_{\infty}
and functorial in
\mathfrak{C}
. This uses the fact that
S_{\infty}
is Gorenstein and that the
S_{\infty}
-complex
\widehat{\mathcal{P}}(\mathfrak{C})
is perfect. Then, for any open ideal
\mathfrak{a}\subseteq S_{\infty}
, Theorem
10.6
(3) gives an isomorphism

\displaystyle S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{S_{\infty}}\widehat{\mathcal{P}}(\mathfrak{C}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle\cong S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{\Lambda_{n}}C_{n}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{\Lambda_{n}}\omega_{\Lambda_{n}}(C_{n})[\ell_{0}-1]

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{RHom}_{S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}}(S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{\Lambda_{n}}C_{n},S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a})[\ell_{0}]

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{RHom}_{S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}}(S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{S_{\infty}}\widehat{\mathcal{P}}(\mathfrak{C}),S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a})[\ell_{0}]

\displaystyle\cong S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{S_{\infty}}\omega_{S_{\infty}}(\widehat{\mathcal{P}}(\mathfrak{C}))[-\dim R_{\infty}]

for infinitely many
n
, again, compatible with the action of
R_{\infty}
and functorial in
\mathfrak{C}
. As
\widehat{\mathcal{P}}(\mathfrak{C})
is a bounded complex of finite free
S_{\infty}
modules, it follows from
[
51
,
Section 06XY
]
that we have natural isomorphisms

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{P}}(\mathfrak{C}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{\mathfrak{a}}\left(S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{S_{\infty}}\widehat{\mathcal{P}}(\mathfrak{C}^{\dagger})\right)

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{\mathfrak{a}}\left(S_{\infty}/\mathfrak{a}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{S_{\infty}}\omega_{S_{\infty}}(\widehat{\mathcal{P}}(\mathfrak{C}))[-\dim R_{\infty}]\right)

\displaystyle\cong\omega_{S_{\infty}}(\widehat{\mathcal{P}}(\mathfrak{C}))[-\dim R_{\infty}]

respecting the action of
R_{\infty}
and functorial in
\mathfrak{C}
. This is as desired.
∎

Remark 11.4
.

As an immediate application of Theorem
11.3
, one can generalize the main result of
[
44
]
.

Specifically let
F
be a CM field in which
\ell
does not ramify, let
D
be a quaternion algebra over
F
, and let
Y_{K}
be the manifold associated to a compact open
K\subseteq D^{\times}(\mathbb{A}_{F}^{\infty})
(analogous to the manifolds constructed in Section
13
). Assume appropriate analogues of Conjectures
A
,
B
and
C
. Let
\mathfrak{m}
be a non-Eisenstein ideal of the Hecke algebra of
Y_{K}
, corresponding to a Galois representation
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}\colon G_{F}\to\mathrm{GL}_{2}(k)
, satisfying certain “Taylor–Wiles conditions.”

Similarly to the construction given in Sections
13
and
15
, one can use the chain complexes for the
Y_{K}
’s (localized at
\mathfrak{m}
) to produce a patching system
{\mathfrak{C}}
and then apply the functor
\mathcal{P}
to produce a maximal Cohen–Macaulay module
M_{\infty}
over a ring
R_{\infty}
. By Theorem
10.6
(2), one gets

M_{\infty}/\mathfrak{m}_{R_{\infty}}M_{\infty}\cong\operatorname{H}_{d}(Y_{K},\mathcal{O})_{\mathfrak{m}}/\mathfrak{m}_{R_{0}}\operatorname{H}_{d}(Y_{K},\mathcal{O})_{\mathfrak{m}}\,,

with
d
the lowest degree for which
\operatorname{H}_{*}(Y_{K},\mathcal{O})_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0
. Thus determining
M_{\infty}
allows us to determine the
multiplicity
of
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}
in
\operatorname{H}_{d}(Y_{K},\mathcal{O})
, just as in
[
44
, Section 4]
.

Crucially
R_{\infty}
is the same ring as the one in
[
44
]
, since both rings are determined by local information at finite places, which is not affected by the transition from totally real fields to CM fields (or general number fields). Moreover, by an analogous construction to the one used in Proposition
(Wt1)
, one can show that
{\mathfrak{C}}^{\dagger}\cong{\mathfrak{C}}
, and so Theorem
11.3
gives
M_{\infty}^{\vee}\cong M_{\infty}
, just as in
[
44
, Proposition 4.10]
.

If
\operatorname{H}_{1}(Y_{K},E)_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0
, then standard multiplicity one theorems for automorphic forms imply that
M_{\infty}
has generic rank
1
, and so
[
44
, Theorem 3.1]
implies that
\dim M_{\infty}/\mathfrak{m}_{R_{\infty}}M_{\infty}=2^{k}
, giving
\dim\operatorname{H}_{1}(Y_{K},\mathcal{O})_{\mathfrak{m}}/\mathfrak{m}_{R_{0}}=2^{k}
, where
k
is an integer (defined in
[
44
, Theorem 1.1]
) depending only the local properties of
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}
at the places where the quaternion algebra
D
ramifies. The method of
[
44
]
crucially relies on the assumption that
M_{\infty}
has generic rank
1
.

This observation is not original to this paper. Frank Calegari has remarked to one of us (JM) that he discovered this result (and Theorem
11.3
) before we did.

In addition to the multiplicity statement,
[
44
, Theorem 3.1]
also implies (again under the assumption that
\operatorname{H}_{1}(Y_{K},E)_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0
) that the natural map

\tau_{M_{\infty}}\colon M_{\infty}\otimes_{R_{\infty}}M_{\infty}\longrightarrow\omega_{R_{\infty}}

induced by the self-duality of
M_{\infty}
is surjective. In the setting of
[
44
]
, that is to say, the “
\ell_{0}=0
” case, this implies an important statement about the associated congruence module (see
[
44
, Theorem 1.2]
and
[
7
, Theorem 3.2]
). This argument does not generalize to the “
\ell_{0}>0
” case considered here, but it seems plausible that the surjectivity of
\tau_{M_{\infty}}
still implies some statement about the homology group
\operatorname{H}_{*}(Y_{K},\mathcal{O})_{\mathfrak{m}}
.

Part III
Deformation rings and Hecke algebras

We now use our work in Parts
I
and
II
to prove integral, non-minimal modularity lifting theorems in defect
\ell_{0}>0
. We prove two versions of our main ‘
R=\mathbb{T}
’ theorem, one in the the case of
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}
over an arbitrary number field and one in the case of weight one modular forms on a Shimura curve over
\mathbb{Q}
. Our main argument (given in Section
15
) will be essentially identical in the two cases, so the only difference in the two cases will be in the setup. When necessary, we refer to these two cases as cases
(PGL2)
and
(Wt1)
:

Case (PGL2)
.

This deals with Hecke eigenclasses arising from the cohomology of symmetric manifolds associated to
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}
over an arbitrary number field
F
. Our main result in this case is Theorem
F
. Most of our work in this case is contingent on conjectures
A
,
B
,
C
and
D
.

Case (Wt1)
.

This deals with weight one modular forms on a Shimura curve over
\mathbb{Q}
. Our main result in this case is Theorem
H
. As a corollary of this we also obtain an integral Jacquet–Langlands statement, Theorem
(Wt1)
, in certain situations. In this case, our results hold unconditionally.

In Section
12
, we introduce the necessary Galois deformation theory required for our results. We work in a generality which covers both cases for most of this section, and only specialize when necessary. In Section
13
we define the Hecke algebras and complexes involved in case
(PGL2)
, and introduce the conjectures
A
,
B
,
C
and
D
. In Section
14
we define the Hecke algebras and complexes needed in case
(Wt1)
, and prove the analogues of conjectures
A
,
B
,
C
and
D
. We deduce Theorem
I
from Theorem
H
. Our main result, Theorem
(Wt1)
, is proved in Section
15
. It implies Theorem
F
in Case
(PGL2)
and Theorem
H
in Case
(Wt1)
.

The work in this part is illustrative of the applicability of the commutative algebra developed earlier to number theory. As such, we have not worked in the fullest generality possible. We have indicated a few other plausible number theoretic applications in the introduction.

12.
Galois deformation theory

For the remainder of this paper we fix a prime
\ell>2
and a finite extension
E/{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
with ring of integers
\mathcal{O}
, uniformizer
\varpi
and residue field
k\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{O}/\varpi
. Let
F
denote an arbitrary number field and assume that
\ell
does not ramify in
F
. Let

\overline{\rho}\colon G_{F}\to\mathrm{GL}_{2}(k)

be a continuous Galois representation with
\overline{\rho}|_{G_{F(\zeta_{\ell})}}
is absolutely irreducible. For convenience we assume that for each
\sigma\in G_{F}
the eigenvalues of
\overline{\rho}(\sigma)\in\mathrm{GL}_{2}(k)
lie in
k
(which can be done by replacing
k
by its unique quadratic extension if necessary). Let
\psi\colon G_{F}\to\mathcal{O}^{\times}
be a character lifting
\det\overline{\rho}:G_{F}\to k^{\times}
.

While we work in the generality stated above for most of this section, in the remainder of the paper we apply these results only in two special cases, corresponding to the cases
(PGL2)
and
(Wt1)
mentioned above.

•

Case
(PGL2)
: Here
F
is an arbitrary number field,
\psi
is the cyclotomic character
\varepsilon_{\ell}
and
\overline{\rho}|_{G_{F_{v}}}
is flat at all places
v|\ell
.

•

Case
(Wt1)
: Here
F\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbb{Q}
, the character
\psi
is the Teichmuller lift of
\det\overline{\rho}
(and hence has finite image), and
\overline{\rho}
is unramified at
\ell
and odd.

12.1.
Local deformation rings

We introduce the various local (framed) deformation rings needed for the rest of the paper.

Fix a prime
v
of
F
, and consider the local Galois representation

\overline{\rho}_{v}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\overline{\rho}|_{G_{F_{v}}}\colon G_{F_{v}}\to\mathrm{GL}_{2}(k)

Write
q_{v}=\Nm(v)=\#(\mathcal{O}_{F}/v)
for the norm of
v
. Let
I_{F_{v}}\unlhd G_{F_{v}}
be the inertia subgroup and
P_{F_{v}}\unlhd I_{F_{v}}
be the wild inertia subgroup.

Let
\varphi_{v}\in G_{F_{v}}
be a lift of (arithmetic) Frobenius
\Frob_{v}
and
\sigma_{v}\in G_{F_{v}}
a lift of a topological generator of
I_{F_{v}}/P_{F_{v}}
, so that
\varphi_{v}\sigma_{v}\varphi_{v}^{-1}=\sigma_{v}^{q_{v}}
in
G_{F_{v}}/P_{F_{v}}
.

Let
R_{v}^{\square}
be the unrestricted framed deformation ring parameterizing lifts of
\overline{\rho}_{v}
with determinant
\psi|_{G_{F_{v}}}
. Let
\rho_{v}^{\square}\colon G_{F_{v}}\to\mathrm{GL}_{2}(R_{v}^{\square})
be the universal lift of
\overline{\rho}_{v}
.

Suppose first that
v|\ell
. If
\overline{\rho}_{v}
is flat, as is
\psi|_{G_{F_{v}}}
(which will be the case if
\psi=\varepsilon_{\ell}
), then let
R^{\fl}_{v}
be the quotient of
R_{v}^{\square}
parameterizing flat deformations of
\overline{\rho}_{v}
, with determinant
\varepsilon_{\ell}
. As
\ell
does not ramify in
F
, from
[
20
, Section 2.4.1]
we get

R_{v}^{\fl}\cong\mathcal{O}[\![{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{3+[F_{v}:{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}]}}]\!]\,.

If
\overline{\rho}_{v}
and
\psi|_{G_{F_{v}}}
are unramified at
v
, let
R_{v}^{\ur}
be the quotient of
R_{v}^{\square}
parameterizing unramified lifts of
\overline{\rho}_{v}
with determinant
\psi|_{G_{F_{v}}}
. Then we clearly have

R_{v}^{\ur}\cong\mathcal{O}[\![{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}}]\!].

From now on assume that
v\nmid\ell
. It follows (see for example
[
50
, Theorem 2.5]
) that
R_{v}^{\square}
is a reduced complete intersection, flat over
\mathcal{O}
of relative dimension
3
.

Also, as
\operatorname{Ker}(\mathrm{GL}_{2}(R_{v}^{\square})\to\mathrm{GL}_{2}(k))
is pro-
\ell
, the natural map
\rho_{v}^{\square}(P_{F_{v}})\to\overline{\rho}_{v}^{\square}(P_{F_{v}})
is an isomorphism, and so if
\overline{\rho}_{v}
is tamely ramified,
\rho^{\square}_{v}
is as well.

Let
R^{\min}_{v}
be the quotient of
R_{v}^{\square}
parameterizing “minimally ramified” lifts of
\overline{\rho}_{v}
(see
[
20
, Definition 2.4.14]
). Then by
[
20
, Lemma 2.4.19]
we get that

R_{v}^{\min}\cong\mathcal{O}[\![{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}}]\!]\,.

From now on we consider the case where
v
is unramified in
\overline{\rho}
, and
q_{v}\not\equiv 1\pmod{\ell}
and
\overline{\rho}_{v}(\Frob_{v})
has eigenvalues with ratio
q_{v}
. Fix a choice
\sqrt{q_{v}\psi(\varphi_{v})^{-1}}\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
of square root of
q_{v}\psi(\varphi_{v})^{-1}
in
\mathcal{O}^{\times}
, and also use
\sqrt{q_{v}\psi(\varphi_{v})^{-1}}
to denote the image in
k^{\times}
. In Case
(PGL2)
we have
\psi=\varepsilon_{\ell}
, so that
q_{v}\psi(\varphi_{v})^{-1}=1
, and so we take
\sqrt{q_{v}\psi(\varphi_{v})^{-1}}=1
for convenience.

As
q_{v}\not\equiv 1\pmod{\ell}
the eigenvalues of
\overline{\rho}(\varphi_{v})
are distinct and so up to conjugation,
\overline{\rho}(\varphi_{v})
has the form

\overline{\rho}(\varphi_{v})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\psi(\varphi_{v})\epsilon_{v}\sqrt{q_{v}\psi(\varphi_{v})^{-1}}&0\\
0&\epsilon_{v}\sqrt{q_{v}\psi(\varphi_{v})^{-1}}^{-1}\end{array}\right)

for some
\epsilon_{v}\in\{1,-1\}
. If
q_{v}\not\equiv\pm 1\pmod{\ell}
, then the choice of
\epsilon_{v}
is uniquely determined by
\overline{\rho}(\varphi_{v})
. In the case when
q_{v}\equiv\pm-1\pmod{\ell}
, either
\epsilon_{v}=1
or
\epsilon_{v}=-1
is possible.

For
\epsilon_{v}\in\{1,-1\}
the Steinberg quotient
R_{q}^{\mathrm{St}(\epsilon_{v})}
is the unique torsion free reduced quotient of
R_{v}^{\square}
characterized by the fact that the
L
-valued points of its generic fiber, for any finite extension
L/E
, correspond to representations conjugate to

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\psi\chi_{v}&\ast\\
0&\chi_{v}^{-1}\end{array}\right)

where
\chi_{v}
is an unramified character with
\chi_{v}(\varphi_{v})=\epsilon_{v}\sqrt{q_{v}\psi(\varphi_{v})^{-1}}
. If
q_{v}\not\equiv\pm 1\pmod{\ell}
, then there is a unique
\epsilon_{v}
for which
R_{q}^{\mathrm{St}(\epsilon_{v})}\neq 0
, while if
q_{v}\equiv-1\pmod{\ell}
then
R_{q}^{\mathrm{St}(1)}
and
R_{q}^{\mathrm{St}(-1)}
are both nonzero.

We define the unipotent quotient
R_{v}^{{\rm uni}(\epsilon_{v})}
of
R_{v}^{\square}
to be
the unique reduced quotient of
R_{v}^{\square}
such that

\operatorname{Spec}R_{v}^{{\rm uni}(\epsilon_{v})}=\operatorname{Spec}R_{v}^{{\rm St}(\epsilon_{v})}\cup\operatorname{Spec}R_{v}^{\rm min}

inside
\operatorname{Spec}R_{v}^{\square}
.

The computations of
[
49
, Section 5]
give the following result.

Proposition (Wt1)
.

Assume that
\overline{\rho}_{v}
is unramified and
q_{v}\not\equiv 1\pmod{\ell}
.

If the ratio of the eigenvalues of
\overline{\rho}_{v}(\varphi_{v})
is not
q_{v}^{\pm 1}
, then

R_{v}^{\square}=R_{v}^{\min}\cong\mathcal{O}[\![{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}}]\!]\,.

Now assume that the eigenvalues of
\overline{\rho}_{v}(\varphi_{v})
have ratio
q_{v}
. If
q_{v}\not\equiv\pm 1\pmod{\ell}
, let
\epsilon_{v}
be the unique element of
\{1,-1\}
for which
R_{q}^{\mathrm{St}(\epsilon_{v})}\neq 0
. If
q_{v}\equiv-1\pmod{\ell}
, let
\epsilon_{v}
be either
1
or
-1
.

We have an isomorphism
R_{v}^{{\rm uni}(\epsilon_{v})}\cong\mathcal{O}[\![{A_{v},B_{v},X_{v},Y_{v}}]\!]/(A_{v}B_{v})
and the corresponding universal representation sends:

\displaystyle\sigma_{v}

\displaystyle\mapsto U^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}1&A_{v}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}U\qquad\text{where $U\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\begin{pmatrix}1&X_{v}\\
Y_{v}&1\end{pmatrix}$, and }

\displaystyle\varphi_{v}

\displaystyle\mapsto U^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{v}(\varphi_{v})\epsilon_{v}\sqrt{q_{v}\psi(\varphi_{v})^{-1}}(1+B_{v})^{-1}&0\\
0&\epsilon_{v}\sqrt{q_{v}\psi(\varphi_{v})^{-1}}^{-1}(1+B_{v})\end{pmatrix}U

We have
and
R^{\min}_{v}=R_{v}^{{\rm uni}(\epsilon_{v})}/(A_{v})=\mathcal{O}[\![{B_{v},X_{v},Y_{v}}]\!]
and
R_{v}^{\mathrm{St}(\epsilon_{v})}=R_{v}^{{\rm uni}(\epsilon_{v})}/(B_{v})=\mathcal{O}[\![{A_{v},X_{v},Y_{v}}]\!]
. If
q_{v}\not\equiv\pm 1\pmod{\ell}
, then
R_{v}^{{\rm uni}(\epsilon_{v})}=R_{v}^{\square}
.∎

Remark (Wt1)
.

In our main results (e.g., Theorem
(Wt1)
) we restrict ourselves to allowing (non-minimal) ramification of lifts of
\overline{\rho}
only at the places in Proposition
(Wt1)
above (these are our “level raising” primes); this is done mainly for simplicity. We should be able to treat unrestricted ramification at places
v
such that

q_{v}\equiv 1\pmod{\ell}
and
\overline{\rho}
is unramified at
v
with
\overline{\rho}(\Frob_{v})
having distinct eigenvalues, or unipotent ramification at places
v
such that

q_{v}\equiv 1\pmod{\ell}
and
\overline{\rho}
is unramified at
v
with
\overline{\rho}(\Frob_{v})
of order divisible by
\ell
, without too much trouble. (It would be more work to allow unipotent ramification at places
v
that are trivial for
\overline{\rho}
.)

We note that if
\overline{\rho}
is odd at a real place of
F
, with corresponding (conjugacy class of) complex conjugation
c\in G_{F}
, then any
v
such that
\overline{\rho}(\Frob_{v})
is conjugate to
\overline{\rho}(c)
is a level raising prime (with
q_{v}\equiv-1\pmod{\ell}
) in our sense.
If the projective image of
\overline{\rho}
is
{\rm PSL_{2}}(k)
(for
|k|\geq 5
) and
[F(\zeta_{\ell}):F]>2
then there are places
v
such that
\overline{\rho}(\Frob_{v})
has eigenvalues with ratio
q_{v}
and
q_{v}\not\equiv\pm 1\pmod{\ell}
; these are again level raising primes in our sense.

12.2.
Global deformation rings

Let
\overline{\rho}\colon G_{F}\to\mathrm{GL}_{2}(k)
be as before. Let
S
be the set of all finite places
v
of
F
, such that either
\overline{\rho}
or
\psi
is ramified at
v
, or
v|\ell
. At places
v\in S
and
v\nmid\ell
, we either assume that
q_{v}\not\equiv-1\pmod{\ell}
, or that
\overline{\rho}|_{I_{v}}
is irreducible, or that
\overline{\rho}|_{D_{v}}
is reducible. (We allude to this condition as
non-vexing
following the use of this terminology in
[
18
]
; it avoids considerations of types on the automorphic side to match conditions of minimal ramification on the Galois side.) Also fix three disjoint finite collections
T
,
\mathfrak{D}

and
Q
of finite places of
F
such that for each
v\in T\cup\mathfrak{D}
:

•

\overline{\rho}_{v}
is unramified.

•

v\nmid\ell
and
q_{v}\not\equiv 1\pmod{\ell}
.

•

The ratio of the eigenvalues of
\overline{\rho}_{v}(\Frob_{v})
is
q_{v}
.

and for each
v\in Q
:

•

\overline{\rho}_{v}
is unramified.

•

q_{v}\equiv 1\pmod{\ell}
.

•

\overline{\rho}_{v}(\Frob_{v})
has has distinct eigenvalues in
k
.

For each
v\in T\cup\mathfrak{D}
we fix a
\epsilon_{v}\in\{1,-1\}
for which
R_{v}^{\mathrm{St}(\epsilon_{v})}\neq 0
(and recall that for
q_{v}\not\equiv\pm 1\pmod{\ell}
, there is a unique such choice). Let
\Sigma
be any subset of
T
.

In our applications,
\Sigma
represents a collection of ‘level raising primes’ for
\overline{\rho}
and
Q
represents a collection of ‘Taylor–Wiles primes’, which are used in our patching construction. In the case
(Wt1)
,
\mathfrak{D}
is the discriminant of a Shimura curve over
\mathbb{Q}
, while
\mathfrak{D}=\varnothing
in the case
(PGL2)
.

The goal of this section is to construct global Galois deformation rings
R_{\Sigma}^{\mathfrak{D}}
and
R_{\Sigma,Q}^{\mathfrak{D}}
, and their framed variants
R_{\Sigma}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}
and
R_{\Sigma,Q}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{O}}}
(respectively,
{\mathrm{CNL}_{\mathcal{O}}}
) denote the category of Artinian (respectively, of complete local noetherian)
\mathcal{O}
-algebras with residue field
k
.

Define a functor
\mathcal{D}\colon{\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{O}}}\to{\mathrm{Sets}}
which sends a ring
A\in{\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{O}}}
to the set
\mathcal{D}(A)
of equivalence classes of continuous representations
\rho\colon G_{F}\to\mathrm{GL}_{2}(A)
for which
\det\rho=\psi
and the composition

G_{F}\xrightarrow{\rho}\mathrm{GL}_{2}(A)\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{2}(k)

is equal to
\overline{\rho}\colon G_{F}\to\mathrm{GL}_{2}(k)
, where two such representations
\rho,\rho\colon G_{F}\to\mathrm{GL}_{2}(A)
are considered equivalent if
\rho^{\prime}=\gamma\rho\gamma^{-1}
for some
\gamma\in 1+M_{2}(\mathfrak{m}_{A})
.

Define subfunctors
\mathcal{D}_{\Sigma}^{\mathfrak{D}}\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{\Sigma,Q}^{\mathfrak{D}}\subseteq\mathcal{D}\colon{\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{O}}}\to{\mathrm{Sets}}
such that for any
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(A)
, we have
\rho\in\mathcal{D}^{\mathfrak{D}}_{\Sigma}(A)
if

•

In Case
(PGL2)
,
\rho
is flat at
v
for all places
v|\ell
;

•

In Case
(Wt1)
,
\rho
is unramified at
\ell
;

•

If
v
is any place of
F
for which
v\nmid\ell
and
\overline{\rho}
is ramified at
v
, then
\rho
is minimally ramified at
v
;

•

If
v
is any place of
F
with
v\not\in S\cup\Sigma\cup\mathfrak{D}
(including the case
v\in T\smallsetminus\Sigma
) then
\rho
is unramified at
v
.

•

If
v\in\mathfrak{D}
then
\rho|_{G_{F_{v}}}
arises from
R_{v}^{\mathrm{St}(\epsilon_{v})}
.

•

If
v\in\Sigma
and
q_{v}\equiv-1\pmod{\ell}
then
\rho|_{G_{F_{v}}}
arises from
R_{v}^{{\rm uni}(\epsilon_{v})}
.

and
\rho\in\mathcal{D}^{\mathfrak{D}}_{\Sigma,Q}(A)
if

•

In Case
(PGL2)
,
\rho
is flat at
v
for all places
v|\ell
;

•

In Case
(Wt1)
,
\rho
is unramified at
\ell
;

•

If
v
is any place of
F
for which
v\nmid\ell
and
\overline{\rho}
is ramified at
v
, then
\rho
is minimally ramified at
v
;

•

If
v
is any place of
F
with
v\not\in S\cup\Sigma\cup\mathfrak{D}\cup Q
(including the case
v\in T\smallsetminus\Sigma
) then
\rho
is unramified at
v
.

•

If
v\in\mathfrak{D}
then
\rho|_{G_{F_{v}}}
arises from
R_{v}^{\mathrm{St}(\epsilon_{v})}
.

•

If
v\in\Sigma
and
q_{v}\equiv-1\pmod{\ell}
then
\rho|_{G_{F_{v}}}
arises from
R_{v}^{{\rm uni}(\epsilon_{v})}
.

Note that for
\rho\in\mathcal{D}^{\mathfrak{D}}_{\Sigma}(A)
(respectively,
\rho\in\mathcal{D}^{\mathfrak{D}}_{\Sigma,Q}(A)
) there is no restriction on the ramification of
\rho
at primes in
\Sigma
(respectively, at primes in
\Sigma\cup Q
) with
q_{v}\not\equiv-1\pmod{\ell}
.

Similarly define framed versions of these functors

\mathcal{D}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}_{\Sigma},\mathcal{D}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}_{\Sigma,Q}\colon{\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{O}}}\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Sets}}

as follows: For
A\in{\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{O}}}
, let
\mathcal{D}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}_{\Sigma}(A)
(respectively,
\mathcal{D}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}_{\Sigma,Q}(A)
) denote the set of tuples
(\rho,(\beta_{v})_{v\in S\cup T\cup\mathfrak{D}})
where
\rho\in\mathcal{D}_{\Sigma}(A)
(respectively,
\rho\in\mathcal{D}_{\Sigma,Q}(A)
) and each
\beta_{v}
is an element of
1+M_{2}(\mathfrak{m}_{A})
, where two tuples
(\rho,(\beta_{v})_{v\in S\cup T\cup\mathfrak{D}})
and
(\rho^{\prime},(\beta^{\prime}_{v})_{v\in S\cup T\cup\mathfrak{D}})
are equivalent if there is some
\gamma\in 1+M_{2}(\mathfrak{m}_{A})
satisfying
\rho^{\prime}=\gamma\rho\gamma^{-1}
and
\beta_{v}^{\prime}=\gamma\beta_{v}
for all
v\in S\cup T\cup\mathfrak{D}
.

One may view
\beta_{v}
as a choice of basis for
A^{2}
lifting the standard basis for
k^{2}
.

It is well known that the functors
\mathcal{D}^{\mathfrak{D}}_{\Sigma}
,
\mathcal{D}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}_{\Sigma}
,
\mathcal{D}^{\mathfrak{D}}_{\Sigma,Q}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\Sigma,Q}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}
are all pro-representable by rings
R^{\mathfrak{D}}_{\Sigma},R_{\Sigma}^{\mathfrak{D},\square},R^{\mathfrak{D}}_{\Sigma,Q}
and
R_{\Sigma,Q}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}
, respectively, in
{\mathrm{CNL}_{\mathcal{O}}}
. When
\mathfrak{D}
is either clear from context or empty (as it is in Case
(PGL2)
) we sometimes omit it from our notation, and write these rings as
R_{\Sigma},R_{\Sigma}^{\square},R_{\Sigma,Q}
and
R_{\Sigma,Q}^{\square}

By definition we have
\mathcal{D}_{\Sigma}^{\mathfrak{D}}\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{\Sigma,Q}^{\mathfrak{D}}
and for any
\Sigma_{1}\subseteq\Sigma_{2}\subseteq T
we have
\mathcal{D}_{\Sigma_{1}}^{\mathfrak{D}}\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{\Sigma_{2}}^{\mathfrak{D}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\Sigma_{1},Q}^{\mathfrak{D}}\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{\Sigma_{2},Q}^{\mathfrak{D}}
. These maps on functors induce a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\Sigma_{2},Q}^{\mathfrak{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\Sigma_{2}}^{\mathfrak{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\Sigma_{1},Q}^{\mathfrak{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\Sigma_{1}}^{\mathfrak{D}}}

Similarly we have
\mathcal{D}_{\Sigma}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{\Sigma,Q}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}
(for any
\Sigma
),
\mathcal{D}_{\Sigma_{1}}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{\Sigma_{2}}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\Sigma_{1},Q}^{\square}\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{\Sigma_{2},Q}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}
giving rise to a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\Sigma_{2},Q}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\Sigma_{2}}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\Sigma_{1},Q}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\Sigma_{1}}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}}

It is important here that the definitions of
\mathcal{D}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}_{\Sigma},\mathcal{D}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}_{\Sigma,Q}
included
\beta_{v}
’s for all
v\in S\cup T\cup\mathfrak{D}
, including
v\in T\smallsetminus\Sigma
(but not for
v\in Q
). Otherwise we would not be able to compare the functors
\mathcal{D}_{\Sigma_{1}}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}
,
\mathcal{D}_{\Sigma_{2}}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}
,
\mathcal{D}_{\Sigma_{1},Q}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\Sigma_{2},Q}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}
in this way.

The map
(\rho,(\beta_{v})_{v\in S\cup T\cup\mathfrak{D}})\mapsto\rho
clearly induces maps
R_{\Sigma}^{\mathfrak{D}}\to R_{\Sigma}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}
and
R_{\Sigma,Q}^{\mathfrak{D}}\to R_{\Sigma,Q}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}
. It is well-known and easy to see that these maps are formally smooth, and thus induce (non-canonical) isomorphisms

R_{\Sigma}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}\cong R_{\Sigma}^{\mathfrak{D}}[\![{w_{1},\ldots,w_{4|S\cup T|-1}}]\!]\quad\text{and}\quad R_{\Sigma,Q}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}\cong R_{\Sigma,Q}^{\mathfrak{D}}[\![{w_{1},\ldots,w_{4|S\cup T|-1}}]\!]\,,

respecting the commutative diagrams above.

Also for any
v\in S\cup T\cup\mathfrak{D}
, the map
(\rho,(\beta_{v})_{v\in S\cup T\cup\mathfrak{D}})\mapsto\beta_{v}^{-1}\rho|_{G_{F_{v}}}\beta_{v}
induces a map
R_{v}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}\to R_{\Sigma,Q}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}
and (and hence a map
R_{v}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}\to R_{\Sigma,Q}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}\to R_{\Sigma}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}
). By the definition of
R_{\Sigma,Q}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}
, it is easy to see that this induces a map

R_{\loc,\Sigma}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\widehat{\bigotimes_{v|\ell}}R^{\fl}_{v}\widehat{\otimes}\widehat{\bigotimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in S,\\
v\nmid\ell\end{subarray}}}R^{\min}_{v}\widehat{\otimes}\widehat{\bigotimes_{v\in\Sigma}}R_{v}^{{\rm uni}(\epsilon_{v})}\widehat{\otimes}\widehat{\bigotimes_{v\in T\smallsetminus\Sigma}}R^{\min}_{v}\to R_{\Sigma,Q}^{\square}.

in Case
(PGL2)
, and in Case
(Wt1)
a map

R_{\loc,\Sigma}^{\mathfrak{D}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\widehat{\bigotimes_{v|\ell}}R^{\ur}_{v}\widehat{\otimes}\widehat{\bigotimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in S,\\
v\nmid\ell\end{subarray}}}R^{\min}_{v}\widehat{\otimes}\widehat{\bigotimes_{v\in\Sigma}}R_{v}^{{\rm uni}(\epsilon_{v})}\widehat{\otimes}\widehat{\bigotimes_{v\in T\smallsetminus\Sigma}}R^{\min}_{v}\widehat{\otimes}\widehat{\bigotimes_{v\in\mathfrak{D}}}R^{\mathrm{St}(\epsilon_{v})}_{v}\to R_{\Sigma,Q}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}.

Moreover for
\Sigma_{1}\subseteq\Sigma_{2}\subseteq T
it is easy to see that the natural map
R_{\loc,\Sigma_{2}}^{\mathfrak{D}}\twoheadrightarrow R_{\loc,\Sigma_{1}}^{\mathfrak{D}}
, given by the quotient map

R_{v}^{{\rm uni}(\epsilon_{v})}\twoheadrightarrow R_{v}^{{\rm uni}(\epsilon_{v})}/(A_{v})=R_{v}^{\min}

for all
v\in\Sigma_{2}\smallsetminus\Sigma_{1},q_{v}\not\equiv-1\pmod{\ell}
gives the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\loc,\Sigma_{2}}^{\mathfrak{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\Sigma_{2},Q}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\Sigma_{2}}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\loc,\Sigma_{1}}^{\mathfrak{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\Sigma_{1},Q}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\Sigma_{1}}^{\mathfrak{D},\square}}

12.3.
Taylor–Wiles deformations.

Suppose
v
is a prime of
F
that is unramified in
\overline{\rho}
and that
q_{v}\equiv 1\pmod{\ell}
, and
\overline{\rho}|_{G_{F_{v}}}
is unramified. Let
\Delta_{v}=k(v)^{\times}(\ell)
and
\Lambda_{v}=\mathcal{O}[\Delta_{v}]
, and suppose that
\overline{\rho}(\Frob_{v})
has distinct eigenvalues
\gamma_{v,1},\gamma_{v,2}\in k
.
Then the universal representation
\rho^{\rm univ}_{\Sigma,Q}:G_{F}\to\mathrm{GL}_{2}(R_{\Sigma,Q}^{\mathfrak{D}})
when restricted to
G_{F_{v}}
is of the form

\chi_{1,v}\oplus\chi_{2,v},

for continuous characters
\chi_{1,v},\chi_{2,v}\colon G_{F_{v}}\to(R_{\Sigma,Q}^{\mathfrak{D}})^{\times}
satisfying the following conditions: we have
(\chi_{i})(\Frob_{v})\equiv\gamma_{v,i}\pmod{{\mathfrak{m}}_{R_{\Sigma,Q}}}
and
\chi_{i}|_{I_{F_{v}}}
induces via the Artin map a homomorphism
\Delta_{v}\to(R_{\Sigma,Q}^{\mathfrak{D}})^{\times}
that we denote by the same symbol
\chi_{i}
.

Let
\Delta_{Q}=\prod_{v\in Q}\Delta_{v}
, and consider the group algebra
\mathcal{O}[\Delta_{Q}]
.
Then
R_{\Sigma,Q}^{\mathfrak{D}}
is naturally a
\mathcal{O}[\Delta_{Q}]
-algebra via
\prod_{v}\chi_{1,v}\colon\mathcal{O}[\Delta_{Q}]\to R_{\Sigma,Q}^{\mathfrak{D}}
. If
\mathfrak{a}_{Q}\subset\mathcal{O}[\Delta_{Q}]
is the augmentation ideal, then there is a canonical isomorphism
R_{\Sigma,Q}^{\mathfrak{D}}/(\mathfrak{a}_{Q})\cong R_{\Sigma}^{\mathfrak{D}}
. We record this as the following proposition.

Proposition (Wt1)
.

For any
\Sigma
, there is a map
\mathcal{O}[\Delta_{Q}]\to R_{\Sigma,Q}^{\mathfrak{D}}
such that

R_{\Sigma,Q}^{\mathfrak{D}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}[\Delta_{Q}]}\mathcal{O}=R_{\Sigma}^{\mathfrak{D}}

via the natural map
R_{\Sigma,Q}^{\mathfrak{D}}\twoheadrightarrow R_{\Sigma}^{\mathfrak{D}}
, and for any
\Sigma_{1}\subseteq\Sigma_{2}
, the map
R_{\Sigma_{2},Q}^{\mathfrak{D}}\twoheadrightarrow R_{\Sigma_{1},Q}^{\mathfrak{D}}
is an
\mathcal{O}[\Delta_{Q}]
-algebra homomorphism. ∎

13.
Hecke algebras for
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}/F

In this section we work exclusively in the case
(PGL2)
. As before,
F
is an arbitrary number field such that the prime
\ell
does not ramify in
F
. Let
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
respectively denote the number of real and complex places of
F
. For the remainder of this section we use the notation and results of Sections
10
and
11
with
d=r_{1}+r_{2}
and
\ell_{0}=r_{2}
. In particular, for any
C\in\mathsf{D}(\mathcal{O})
, we set

C^{\dagger}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{RHom}_{\mathcal{O}}(C,\mathcal{O})[2d+\ell_{0}]=\operatorname{RHom}_{\mathcal{O}}(C,\mathcal{O})[2r_{1}+3r_{2}].

In the case when
C
is a derived
A
-complex for an
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
A
, this induces a derived
A
-complex structure on
C^{\dagger}
, and so we may view
(-)^{\dagger}
as a contravariant functor
